# Changelog

All notable changes to `precision-soft/melody/v2` will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/), and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Added

- tooling: `.dev/validate/changelog.sh` and `.dev/validate/changelog.baseline` read the SHAPE of every changelog block, which nothing here had ever read. The neighbouring bands ask what a document claims and whether what it names exists; none of them reads a heading, so a session that opened a second `### Fixed` at the head of a block instead of writing into the one already there left half a cycle where no reader of that block would find it — ten of the fourteen `[Unreleased]` blocks of the two published majors were doing exactly that, and the entries of this cycle were split across two sections in each. Four dimensions are asserted: the same section opened twice inside one block, a heading outside the vocabulary, the sections of a block out of the declared order, and an entry common to both published majors filed under different sections. The vocabulary is not invented here — it is the list the release auditor already judges published release bodies against, narrowed to the eight this tree writes, because two sources of truth for one list is how they drift. The order is derived from the tree rather than chosen (`Breaking Changes` opens its block in every block that has one, `Documentation` closes it in every one, and the middle is the Keep a Changelog order these files name as their format), and the one judgement made rather than measured is written into the script's header with its count. The cross-major dimension is the only one no baseline row may excuse, and it is empty by construction on a tree whose two majors were written from one another — it is built for what it can prove rather than for what it finds, being the only thing that can say a session which moved an entry moved it on both majors. What the band cannot do is written down too: no reader of markdown decides which section an entry BELONGS under

- event: `RequiredListenerSkippedError` and its three constructors — `NewRequiredListenerSkippedError`, `NewRequiredListenerSkippedErrorWithCause` and `NewRequiredListenerSkippedErrorWithStoppedListenerFailure` — name the class a caller has to separate from an ordinary listener failure: a dispatch ended, by a stop or by a failure, while a listener marked required was still behind it. The type is what carries that distinction, not the message, and it is what lets the http kernel refuse the dispatch outright on `kernel.request` — a stopping listener that also produced a response would otherwise have that response served with access control never consulted. The two causal constructors keep the ending decision as the refusal and hand the listener's own failure on as the cause, so the diagnostic of what actually broke is not lost behind it
- exception: `IsAlreadyLogged` reads the already-logged mark at the depth `MarkLogged` writes it. It is the single reader: asking a concrete `*Error` instead misses a marked `HttpException` and anything wrapping a marked error, which are then rendered a second time
- logging: `LogOnRecoverAndExitAfter` logs a recovered value like `LogOnRecoverAndExit` and runs a step of the caller's between the record and the process exit. It is the one place that is both after the record and before the exit: a teardown deferred below never runs, because `os.Exit` skips it, and one run before closes the logger the final record has to travel through
- serializer: `ErrNotAcceptable` is the cause the manager's refusal carries when the accept header refused every media type it can produce. It is read with `errors.Is` rather than matched on the message — the error renderer and the result handler both answer `406` from it — which is what tells that refusal apart from a serializer that failed on the value, the other error the same call answers
- session: `ErrSessionDeleted` is the cause the save refusal carries for a session deleted while the request holding it was still running. It says the session ended rather than that the storage failed, and the two want different answers: the response path expires the browser cookie and serves the handler's response, where a storage outage suppresses the cookie and answers `500`
- session: `(*Session).Snapshot` reads the values, the modified flag and the cleared flag under one lock acquisition, for the response path that has to pair its branch decision with the values it acts on. Read through the individual accessors, a concurrent `Clear` slips between them and the save branch writes the emptied map under a live id — a session neither alive nor deleted
- config: `(*Configuration).MarkServing` records that the wiring phase is over, after which `Resolve` is refused: every service built during boot copied the values it read, so re-resolving reconfigures nothing and only rewrites the parameter store under readers entitled to treat it as settled. Registering a parameter still works, resolving itself on registration, which is what keeps a late module functioning. The framework's own boot marks it; an application wiring the configuration by hand is what the door exists for
- httpclient: `(*HttpClient).RequestStreamWithContext` is `RequestStream` bound to a context: cancelling it ends the request and the body read, which is the only remedy for a stream a server never ends. The close obligation is unchanged — cancelling releases the connection, it does not close the `StreamResponse` for the caller
- debug: `MiddlewareDescriptionProvider` and `MiddlewareBuildProvider` are the two seams `debug.NewMiddlewareCommand` takes. The first answers what the http pipeline would run without building it — the ordered descriptions, the selection report carrying the inactive entries with their reasons, or the same refusal the build gives a cycle or a missing reference. The second runs the real build for an explicit `--build`, answering the failure as an error, with a factory panic recovered by the command around the call

- validation: `.dev/validate/citation.sh` and `.dev/validate/citation.baseline` check what the documents of a major CITE against what that major declares — the direction the neighbouring documentation check structurally cannot ask. That check asks whether a symbol a major ships is documented; it stayed green for months over an upgrade note announcing, with a code example and a symptom, a method no major declares, and it reads nothing outside `<major>/.documentation`, so the per-integration README and CHANGELOG files were compared to nothing by anything. Three dimensions are asserted: a citation qualified by one of the major's own package names must resolve in that major, a citation linking to a .go file must be answered by that file, and every relative link target must exist. A bare backticked name is deliberately not asserted and the omission is written into the header with its measurement — 189 of some 650 distinct bare tokens on one major resolve to nothing, being header names, environment keys, http verbs and placeholder spellings — because a dimension that needs its exceptions counted in the hundreds is not a dimension. The baseline is a set of claims rather than a filter, so an entry that stops matching fails the run as loudly as an unrecorded citation, and wildcards are refused. v1 and v2 are gated; v3 is measured and reported. A `--samples` pass type-checks the fenced go blocks that are whole programs, by writing each into the module that owns the document; it is opt-in and wired into neither the local run nor ci, because it needs a Go toolchain where both neighbouring bands read the tree alone. `.dev/validate/all.sh` and `.github/workflows/ci.yml` carry the band, ungated in the staged tail for the reason its neighbours are: a citation goes stale when the code moves, and the commit that moves it stages a .go file while the document that names it stays untouched

- logging: `loggingcontract.LevelReporter`, the optional capability a `Logger` implements to answer whether a record at a given level would survive its threshold, and `logging.LevelEnabled`, the single door onto it. It exists for the callers that BUILD what they log rather than for the ones that merely hand a record over — see the Fixed entry for what the event dispatch was paying. `jsonLogger` implements it against its own threshold and reports nothing enabled once closed, `nopLogger` reports nothing enabled at all, and `requestLogger` forwards to the base it decorates, which is what makes the capability worth anything on the request path, since the decorator is what every handler and listener actually holds. The answer for a logger that does not implement it is **enabled**, so the reporting behaviour is the floor and never the thing the capability turns off; `loggingcontract.Logger` is untouched, so an existing logger of your own keeps working unchanged
- http: `(*Kernel).OpenRequestScopes` reports how many request scopes are open right now — one per request being served, hijacked connections included, because the scope belongs to `ServeHttp` rather than to the connection. It is what lets a shutdown tell a drained server from one that still has work inside it; see the Fixed entry for the stop that used to be reported clean

- cache: `RememberOption.WithContext` ties a caller's wait to the request that opened it, and `Context` reads it back. `Remember` takes no context of its own — the option is where the optional things about this call already live, beside `WithWaitTimeout` and `WithCancelable`, so no existing caller changes and the three settings that govern one wait are documented together. The context ends that caller's wait alone: the computation belongs to whoever leads the flight and is awaited by everyone coalesced onto it, so a client that disconnects takes its own goroutine out and leaves the value being computed for the others. On the uncoalesced path — stampede protection off, or a value-kind `Cache` — there is no flight, so the callback itself runs under the caller's context. See the Fixed entry for what waiting without one cost
- cli: `--format=json-pretty` renders the same envelope indented for a person reading it by hand. `--format=json` is now one document per line — see the Changed entry — and this is the door for the reading that wants the whitespace back without a `| jq`

- security: `NewAccessControlRawPrefixRule` names the cross-segment prefix rule, and `NewAccessControlRule` now builds the segment-bounded one — the plain name is the bounded tool. The default constructor built a raw prefix rule that matched across segment boundaries, so `NewAccessControlRule("/admin", …)` governed `/administrator` as readily as `/admin/panel` and, being the longest match, shadowed a bounded rule that would have denied; the safe bounded constructor carried the longest name, `NewAccessControlRuleWithSegmentPrefix`. The plain name now builds the segment-bounded rule (`/admin` governs `/admin` and its descendants under a `/` boundary, never `/administrator`), refuses an empty prefix rather than making it a catch-all, and permits `PUBLIC_ACCESS`; the cross-segment reach moves to the explicit `NewAccessControlRawPrefixRule`, which keeps the `PUBLIC_ACCESS` refusal; `NewAccessControlRuleWithSegmentPrefix` stays as a deprecated alias of `NewAccessControlRule`. **Behavioural change**: an existing caller of `NewAccessControlRule` gets stricter matching without a signature change — a rule that relied on the cross-segment reach to protect a path now governs less, so it moves to `NewAccessControlRawPrefixRule`
- security: `RolesReplacer`, the optional capability a `Token` implements to answer its own twin under another role set, and `(*AuthenticatedToken).WithRoles`, which implements it. `RoleHierarchyVoter` asks the token for the twin instead of rebuilding one of melody's own, so a token of the application's own keeps its dynamic type through the expansion — see the Fixed entry for what the rebuild cost. A token that does not carry the capability is rebuilt exactly as before
- logging: `RunShieldedStep` runs one step under the exit handler's own budget and answers whether it finished, so the normal return of `Run` tears down under the same shield the panic path has had since the exit-step budget was installed

- security: `RoleHierarchyAware`, the optional capability an `AccessDecisionManager` implements to receive the declared role hierarchy at compilation, and `(*AccessDecisionManager).WithRoleHierarchy`, which implements it for the built-in manager by wrapping its own role voters. The compilation asks for the capability instead of asserting on the concrete type, and refuses by firewall name a manager that is handed a hierarchy it cannot apply
- http: `Kernel.SetMethodPolicy` installs the method policy the kernel reads on every request — whether `HEAD` falls back to the `GET` route, whether an unrouted `OPTIONS` is answered with the computed `Allow` header. `KernelOptions`, `MethodPolicy` and `DefaultKernelOptions` were a documented surface with no door to hand them to: `NewKernel` fixed the defaults and the field behind them was unexported, so an api that had to answer `405` to `OPTIONS` wrote the configuration and watched it change nothing. `MethodPolicy` now lives on `http/contract` beside `ForwardedHeadersPolicy` and `SessionCookiePolicy`, with an alias under its old name in `http`. **Breaking**: the method is added to `http/contract.Kernel`, which breaks an out-of-tree implementation of that interface

- exception: `PanicCause` reads a recovered panic value as the cause of the error a recovery boundary fabricates in its place — the error itself when the panic was error-shaped, and nothing when it was a typed nil whose `Error()` would dereference a nil receiver at the first render. It names in one place the grammar the framework's own recovery boundaries follow: a panic value kept only in a context slot collapses to its bare message, because the json logger stringifies an error it finds in a context, so the context map and the cause chain of the very error that was raised reach no record at all
- validation: `IsRuleWiringErrorCode`, `ValidationErrors.HasRuleWiringError` and `ValidationErrors.WithoutRuleWiringContext` tell a mistake in the rule DECLARATION — `unknownRule`, `invalidRuleSyntax`, `invalidPattern` — apart from a refusal of the submitted value. None of the three is reachable from any input a client can send: the struct tag is what is wrong, and it is wrong for every request that route will ever serve

- exception: `Logged` answers an error that reports itself already logged, and is what a writer returns after filing its record. An error whose chain carries an `AlreadyLogged` implementer is marked in place and handed back unchanged, so its identity — and every `errors.Is` and `errors.As` its readers perform — survives; an error whose chain carries none has nowhere for the mark to live, and comes back wrapped in a marked melody error keeping the original as its cause. `MarkLogged` is a silent no-op on `errors.New`, `fmt.Errorf` and every runtime error, which is how one failure produced two records
- security/config: the seven `FirewallOverrideConfiguration` setters the family always documented — `WithAccessControl`, `WithRoleHierarchy`, `WithAccessDecisionManager`, `WithEntryPoint`, `WithAccessDeniedHandler`, `WithMergeStrategy` and `WithInheritGlobalAccessControl` — beside the `WithStateless` that was the only one. The fields are unexported, so outside the package no override could opt out of the global access control, choose a merge strategy, or carry its own entry point: `overrideOnly`, `globalFirst` and `inheritGlobalAccessControl == false` were unreachable and the four `…Source` accessors could never report `SourceFirewall`. Each setter reads the exact zero value as the constructor defaults before applying its field, and `WithMergeStrategy` refuses by name a strategy that is none of the three, the empty string included
- security: the `RefusalReason*` constants naming which branch of the access decision produced a 403. Every branch answers the same status and the same client-facing message, so the journal could not tell a real denial from a firewall whose attribute no configured voter looks at
- logging: `NewStandardErrorLogger` adapts a melody logger to the `*log.Logger` net/http's `Server.ErrorLog` wants, so the connection-level failures the http kernel never sees — a TLS handshake that fails before any request exists, a malformed request line, a superfluous `WriteHeader`, a listener degrading — reach the application's journal instead of being printed to stderr as unstructured text
- bunorm: `SecretParameterProvider`, the optional capability of naming the configuration parameters that hold a provider's credentials (see the integration's own changelog)
- container: `contract.ContainerCarrier` — the door a resolver answers its container through — implemented by the container itself, by the concrete scope and by the resolution context a provider receives. A process-lifetime service that defers work past its own construction — a lazily-dialed pool, a registry that opens on first use — replays that work through the container, not through the resolution context that built it: a resolution context is single-threaded and dies with its scope, and a captured one replayed later regained exactly the scope visibility the container's own suspension refuses at every normal resolution. The scope's door answers nil once the scope is closed
- config: a `.env` value may reference a parameter the composition root registers between construction and boot. The constructor's resolution pass defers an undefined parameter reference in a non-reserved parameter instead of failing on it: the parameter is marked unreadable — any accessor reading it before the boot resolution settles it panics naming the parameter, rather than serving the raw template as though it were the value — and the boot resolve settles every deferred reference in one order-independent batch. A reference that is still undefined at boot fails exactly as it always did, and a `kernel.*` parameter never defers, since melody registers the whole reserved namespace before the pass and no later registration can repair it. **Behavioural change**: a typo that reads as a reference — an unescaped literal percent, `pa%ss%word` — now fails at the boot resolve instead of inside `NewConfiguration`, and reaches the operator with the same actionable message

- config: `MELODY_HTTP_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT` (`kernel.http.shutdown_timeout`) sets how long a stopping http server waits for the requests it has already admitted, reaching the server through `Configuration.Http().ShutdownTimeout()`. The default is unchanged at `5s`, which was previously a constant nothing could override — one sixth of what the write timeout promises every request — so a deployment whose supervisor grants a longer termination grace can now align the two instead of cutting at five seconds requests it promised thirty. Only a positive duration describes a wait: zero and negative fail the boot rather than silently becoming a default the operator did not choose. Exceeding the budget keeps reporting a failed shutdown and a non-zero exit, because requests were lost. **Breaking**: the accessor is added to `config/contract.HttpConfiguration`, which breaks an out-of-tree implementation of that interface

- bag: `*ParameterBag.AppendString` appends inside one critical section. The helper of the same name works over the contract through Get and Set, and the window between those two locks loses one of two concurrent appends without an error — a lost update the race detector cannot see, because each individual access is locked; the helper now delegates to the concrete method and keeps the two-step path only for foreign implementations of the contract
- cache: `NewManagerOwningBackend` builds a manager that closes its backend when it is closed itself, for the caller that builds both by hand and wants one Close to end both. `NewManager` no longer cascades — see the Changed entry — so the cascade is now something a caller asks for by name instead of something the container path has to survive
- cache: `DeserializationError` marks a read that found a payload the serializer cannot decode, with `NewDeserializationError` and `IsDeserializationError` beside it. The type is the contract: `Remember` treats it as a miss and recomputes, and a `Cache` implementation of your own that wraps its deserialization failures in it inherits the same self-healing. `Manager.Get` and `Manager.Many` produce it, naming the culprit keys in the error context

- http: `RequestContextMustFromResolver` and `RequestContextFromResolver` resolve the current request's context — its id and start moment — out of the request scope. `ServiceRequestContext` was the one name in the file without a typed door, reachable only through the raw string, while the container documentation's scoped-provider example had been calling `RequestContextMustFromResolver` all along — a function that did not exist. Both take a resolver rather than the container, because the service lives only on a request scope: the kernel installs it per request and the root container never carries it. The tolerant form answers nil where the context is absent — a console process, the root container — for code shared between the two process shapes
- application: `ProcessContext` — `ServiceProcessContext`, `NewProcessContext`, `ProcessContextMustFromResolver`, `ProcessContextFromResolver` — is the console counterpart of the http request context: the generated process id every log record of the run is correlated under, and the moment the run started. The cli entry point installs it into the run's scope, where until now it computed both values for the logger and threw them away, leaving a scoped service no way to ask which run it belongs to. The tolerant accessor answers nil on the http side, mirroring the request context's absence on the console side
- logging: `NewProcessLogger` is the console sibling of `NewRequestLogger`, and the cli entry point now decorates with it. The correlation id wins the context key on every record on both paths; what differs is the fate of a value the caller wrote under the key. The request path keeps a different non-empty string claim under `...Claimed`, because there the value frequently originates in request data a client controls, and letting it win would forge the record's correlation. The console caller is the process's own code — there is no client to forge anything — so its value is legitimate data displaced by the correlation, not a claim to be suspected: it survives verbatim, whatever its type, under `...Provided`. **Behavioural change**: a cli record that carried its own value under `processId` now keeps it beside the id as `processIdProvided` instead of `processIdClaimed`, and a non-string value is preserved there instead of silently dropped
- http: `cors.RegisterListeners` wires the two cors listener doors at once, and `cors.RegisterRequestListener` is the new half of the pair: a kernel.request listener at `cors.RequestListenerPriority` (100) that answers a well-formed preflight from an allowed origin before the security chain runs. The middleware alone could not: access control listens at priority 20 and matches on the path alone, a preflight carries neither cookie nor `Authorization` by specification, and the middleware chain is only built for a request no listener answered — so a preflight to a protected path was structurally condemned to `401` and every cross-origin write behind access control was impossible. The listener answers only what the middleware would have answered had the request reached it — a disallowed or absent origin, an OPTIONS request that is not a preflight, a request something already answered all fall through to security untouched — so it can never widen what access control refuses. The response listener half, `RegisterResponseListener`, existed already and decorates every response including the refusals and error pages the chain never sees; what was missing was anything wiring it: the middleware's documentation now says it decorates the handler path only, and the kernel's `Use` names kernel.response listeners as the door for cross-cutting response decoration
- http: `middleware.RegisterRateLimitRequestListener` meters requests on kernel.request at `RateLimitRequestListenerPriority` (200), ahead of token resolution (50) and access control (20), sharing `RateLimitConfig` with the middleware. `RateLimitMiddleware` meters only the handler path: a request the security chain refuses is answered before the chain is built, so a burst of wrong credentials — the shape of a credential-stuffing run — consumed no budget at all while paying a full authenticator round each time. Through this door the burst is charged and, once the budget is gone, answered `429` before the token source runs. The default key is the client address, which exists before authentication; the refusal is rendered through kernel.exception exactly as the middleware's error path renders it. The middleware is unchanged and remains the door for per-route and per-identity budgets
- config: `MELODY_HTTP_SESSION_TOMBSTONE_RETENTION` (`kernel.http.session_tombstone_retention`) sets how long a deleted session id keeps refusing a write-back, reaching the session manager through `Configuration.Http().SessionTombstoneRetention()`. The default is unchanged at `5m`, which was previously a constant nothing could override, and the constant states an assumption nothing enforces: the window has to cover the longest a request can still be holding a session snapshot loaded before the delete, nothing in the chain bounds a handler's lifetime — the server's socket timeouts cut the connection, not the goroutine — and a request that outlives the window saves the deleted session back with the pre-logout identity intact, measured. A deployment whose slowest legitimate request exceeds five minutes now sizes the window to match, at the cost of one remembered entry per deletion inside it; the record lives in the manager, per process, so a multi-instance deployment is guarded instance by instance. Only a positive window refuses anything: zero and negative fail the boot rather than silently disarming the logout defence. **Breaking**: the accessor is added to `config/contract.HttpConfiguration`, which breaks an out-of-tree implementation of that interface
- session: `NewManagerWithTombstoneRetention` builds a manager whose write-back refusal window is sized by the caller, for wiring the manager by hand; it refuses a non-positive window with a panic naming the rule, the same answer the constructor gives a negative ttl. `NewManager` and `NewManagerOwningStorage` keep the five-minute default, now written on `TombstoneRetention` as the default it always effectively was
- http: `Kernel.HasErrorHandler` reports whether the application installed an error handler. It is deliberately off the `httpcontract.Kernel` contract — the composition root reads it through a Has door, so an out-of-tree kernel implementation neither breaks nor changes behavior: without the door the framework exception listener is registered exactly as before
- container: `ServiceDescriptions` on the built container answers what it can say WITHOUT running a provider — every name either lifetime knows, with the type read from the built instance when one exists and from the provider's declared return type otherwise, which the container now records at registration. `container/contract.ServiceDescription` carries the shape and `ServiceLifetimeContainer`/`ServiceLifetimeScoped` name the two lifetimes; the method is deliberately off the `Container` contract, asked through an interface by whoever needs it, so an out-of-tree container neither breaks nor has to answer
- http: `pipeline.Builder.Describe` answers what `Build` would run without running it — the same selection, gating and ordering, refused for the same cycles and missing references, but no factory invoked — and `HttpMiddlewareDefinition.SetFunctionName` records at registration the function a description names, so listing a pipeline never has to build one. `pipeline.MiddlewareDescription` carries the entry
- debug: `debug:events` renders a `SERVING-PROCESS LISTENERS` block for the listeners only the serving process wires, fed by the composition root through `debug.NewEventCommand` and `debug.DeferredListenerProvider`: with security configured, a console process declares the security resolution and access control listeners with their real event and priorities instead of rendering an absence that reads as "not wired". The verbose json document carries the same declaration under `servingProcessListeners`; the zero-value command stays valid and declares nothing

- example: the example carries the source of its own frontend bundle, in `v2/.example/assets/` — `app.ts`, the `melody-routes.ts` URL generator, and the `package.json` that bundles them into `public/assets/app.js` with esbuild. All five pages loaded `/assets/app.js` and drove every interaction through `window.melodyExample.*`, but no `assets/` directory existed: the file could not be produced by anything, so the browser interface was dead behind five pages that rendered perfectly — a login answered `TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'ui')` and nothing else in the application could be reached from a browser. The manifest the generator builds URLs against is the example's own, assembled in `url.RoutesJsonFromContainer` and injected into every page as `window.melodyRoutes`; it is now the object `{"routes":[…]}` the generator reads rather than a bare array, whose `.routes` is undefined and which therefore built the generator over an empty manifest. The bundle is generated, not committed — `cd assets && npm ci && npm run build`, which the development container runs per major at startup, and which the README now names at the packaging matrix because `melody_static_embedded` freezes `public/` into the binary at compile time

- example: the development stack serves all three example applications at once, each under a name that says which it is — `v1-example.`, `v2-example.` and `example.melody.localhost.precision-soft.com`. They are the same image with a different example directory, so nothing about the supervision is per-major, and none of them publishes a host port: they are reached through the load balancer, which keeps the per-developer host-port map out of it entirely. An end-to-end section tells them apart where a status code cannot — all three answer the same routes, and v1 and v2 are the same application on different majors — by writing through each vhost and reading which catalogue table the row landed in
- example: the example application is a working nomenclature rather than a set of routes that exist to be driven. Products, categories, currencies and users are kept through bunorm when the environment configured a database and in memory when it did not, so the application still boots on a machine with no containers; the listing is served from redis and dropped when a product changes; every write leaves a journal entry naming whoever made it; the write endpoints sit behind a per-address budget while the reads stay open; and the catalogue reading is refreshed on a schedule instead of being computed inside the request that finds a cold cache. The routes that only demonstrated a backend — `/integration/cache/`, `/integration/database/`, `/integration/ratelimit/` — are gone, and what they proved is now proven against the nomenclature itself, half of it read out of band by the end-to-end harness straight from redis and mysql
- example: every redis key and every table the example writes carries its major. The three example applications share one redis and one mysql in development, the cache holds gob-encoded entities whose wire format is keyed on the fully qualified type name — which carries the major — and the rate-limit counter is asserted on exactly by the end-to-end harness. Both example applications had been writing under the same prefix, so with the two of them running at once one would read entries it cannot decode and spend the other's budget; the comment above the constants had said so while the code did the opposite
- config: `MELODY_STATIC_EXCLUDED_PATHS` (`kernel.static.excluded_paths`) names the path prefixes the built-in file server declines without touching the disk, comma separated. A declined request continues down the rest of the middleware chain, which is how an application serves part of the url space behind its own middleware — authentication on a directory, a stricter policy, a file server of its own. An entry is compared against the raw request path exactly as `security.NewPathPrefixMatcher` reads one, so a prefix written for a firewall rule and one written here select the same requests. An empty entry, or one that does not start with a slash, fails the boot. **Breaking**: the accessor is added to `config/contract.HttpConfiguration`, which breaks an out-of-tree implementation of that interface
- http: `SimpleRateLimitWithResolver`, `IpRateLimitWithResolver` and `UserRateLimitWithResolver` take the client-ip resolver the convenience helpers could not reach. The three original helpers build their configuration internally and return only the middleware, so `SetClientIpResolver` — the remedy the documentation prescribes for a deployment behind a reverse proxy — was unreachable through them, and `IpRateLimit(n)` therefore gave every client one shared budget behind any proxy. The original three are unchanged and remain correct where the direct peer is the client
- http: `static.FileServerConfig.SetAllowedDotPrefixList` names the dot-prefixed first path elements the file server may retrieve. The default allows `.well-known` alone, so an ACME http-01 challenge, `security.txt` and `assetlinks.json` published under the public directory stay retrievable while `.env`, `.git` and `.htpasswd` do not. The allowance never reaches past the first element, so `.well-known/.env` is refused exactly like `/.env`, and an empty list refuses every dot-prefixed path
- http: `RegenerateRequestSession` rotates the session id of a request and republishes the rotated session in one call, which is the whole session-fixation defence a login handler needs; write the authenticated identity to the session it returns. `session.Manager.RegenerateSession` remains the storage-level primitive: it mints a fresh id, carries the values over, removes the entry the previous id pointed at, and latches the session passed in out of use, so a caller that does not publish the rotated one logs the client out cleanly instead of leaving it presenting a deleted id. The latch matters because `Session.Set` lifts the cleared flag: a caller that rotated and then kept writing to the original object would otherwise have the response path re-create the just-deleted id and re-issue it as the cookie, silently undoing the rotation and letting a pre-login, attacker-plantable id carry the authenticated identity. A rotated-away session can no longer be resurrected. **Breaking**: the added contract method breaks an out-of-tree implementation of `session/contract.Manager`
- config: `MELODY_HTTP_SESSION_TTL` (`kernel.http.session_ttl`) sets how long a stored session stays valid, reaching the session manager through `Configuration.Http().SessionTtl()`. The default is unchanged at `0`, which stores sessions with no expiry; a negative value fails the boot. A deployment that leaves it at `0` should know the default in-memory store then grows without bound — an http application running on both the framework-supplied storage and a ttl of zero is told so once at boot
- http: `SessionCookiePolicy.Secure` takes a `SessionCookieSecurePolicy`. The default `SessionCookieSecureFromScheme` derives the attribute from the resolved scheme as before, `SessionCookieSecureAlways` forces it on for a deployment whose proxy terminates TLS but is not trusted for `X-Forwarded-Proto`, and `SessionCookieSecureNever` is the explicit opt-out — the only value that can weaken the cookie, so it is discouraged — previously such a deployment emitted every session cookie without `Secure` and had no way to override it

- cache: `Manager.GetCounter` reads a key written by `Increment` or `Decrement`. Those operations are backend-native so a distributed backend keeps them atomic, which means they store the count as decimal text rather than through the serializer — `Get` handed that raw payload to a deserializer that does not expect it, and with any serializer other than the default the two were mutually unreadable in both directions. The contract is now stated on `Increment` as well
- application: two boot warnings name a resource melody supplied that nothing will ever reclaim, so the deployment learns it from a log line rather than from a memory graph. One reports that the framework-supplied in-memory cache backend carries no item ceiling, so a key cached without a ttl is kept until the process exits. The other reports the framework-supplied in-memory session storage paired with a session ttl of zero: melody mints a session for every request that arrives without a session cookie, so one write on a public path turns every such request into a permanent entry, and an unauthenticated caller decides how many arrive. Either half alone is silent — a storage the application registered is the operator's to prune, and a lifetime the deployment set reclaims on its own. Both are raised from the http path only: a command builds its map, runs, and takes it away with it

- container: a scope is a registrar in its own right, layered over the container it came from. `ScopeManager.RegisterScoped` — reached through the container, which is a scope manager — declares a service whose lifetime is one scope — one http request, one command run — built lazily on the first resolution through a scope, held for as long as that scope lives, and closed when it closes; `container.RegisterScoped[T]`, `MustRegisterScoped[T]`, `RegisterScopedType[T]` and `MustRegisterScopedType[T]` are the typed front doors, and `Scope.RegisterScoped` adds one to a single live scope. The root container never sees a scoped service and two scopes never share one: each holds its own creation guard, so neither waits on the other's build. A scoped service reads both levels — the scope's overrides and the container's singletons — while a container provider stays request-agnostic exactly as before, which is the direction that had to stay closed. Until now the only way to put anything request-specific in front of userland was the override the framework installs for its own logger and request context, so an application had no way to declare a request-lifetime service at all. The declaration sits on `ScopeManager` rather than beside the container's own registrations, because a scope does not exist until a request arrives: the services a scope owns have to be declared by whatever will be making the scopes. **Breaking**: `container/contract.ScopeManager` and `container/contract.Scope` gain `RegisterScoped` and `MustRegisterScoped`, which breaks an out-of-tree implementation of either — including an implementation of `Container`, which embeds `ScopeManager`
- container: `Replacing()` admits a registration whose name — or whose registered type — the other lifetime already claims. Without it the collision is refused where it is made, in both directions and whichever order the two registrations arrive in, because a name that answers with a process singleton outside a scope and with a per-request service inside one is an ambiguity whose symptom appears one lifetime away from its cause. It admits substitution, not decoration: a scoped provider that resolves the name it replaces re-enters itself and is reported as the circular dependency it is
- container: `ClosedWithScope()` hands an installed override to the scope's teardown, through the new `OverrideInstanceWithOptions` and `OverrideProtectedInstanceWithOptions`. The default is unchanged and stays the right one — an override belongs to whoever installed it and outlives the scope, which is what lets the http kernel report a scope-close failure through the request logger it installed — so the option is for the caller that built the value for one scope and holds no other reference to it. The four `OverrideService` signatures are untouched
- application: `ScopedServiceModule` is the module hook for request-lifetime services, with `RegisterScopedServices(kernelInstance, registrar)` running at boot beside `RegisterServices`. It is a separate interface, so no existing module changes. `ScopedServiceRegistrar` and `ServiceRegistrar` deliberately share no method: a container provider handed to the scoped hook, or a scoped provider handed to `RegisterServices`, is a compile error at the call site rather than a service quietly rebuilt and closed once per request. A name claimed at both lifetimes joins the aggregated boot collision report

### Changed

- documentation: the package documents catch up with the code they describe. BAG no longer claims a nil-valued key reads as present (every string accessor answers absent) nor that `String` is permissive for every non-string type (a `[]string` value panics naming the key); CONFIG's recognised-keys table carries `MELODY_HTTP_SESSION_TOMBSTONE_RETENTION` and `MELODY_HTTP_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT` and describes the late secret-marking as the fixpoint walk it is; SESSION lists `NewManagerWithTombstoneRetention`; DEBUG documents the two-provider `NewMiddlewareCommand`, the describe-by-default listings with `--build`, and the `-vvv` escape of the trace/stack filter; APPLICATION's usage examples compile against the real handler shape and the shutdown timeout is no longer called fixed; UPGRADE describes one consistent `NewMiddlewareCommand` and carries the missing `ShutdownTimeout()` accessor entry; SECURITY names the firewall-manager service id the code registers (`service.security.firewall_manager`); HTTP documents the cors request-listener pair and the rate-limit listener door; CACHE states the full shared key grammar; LOGGING's console example uses `NewProcessLogger`

- cli, logging: text of unknown origin reaching a terminal or a plain-text log line is escaped rather than obeyed. The table printer's free-text channels — summary lines, block titles, warning messages and details, the error block — and its cells wrote values verbatim, so a stored record carrying `\x1b[2J` cleared the operator's terminal from a listing and a `\r` in an error message overwrote what the report appeared to say; the cells are escaped before the widths are measured, so the escaped spelling is the one the alignment counts. The command status line escapes the error it interpolates — an embedded carriage return plus the erase sequence the colored line already emits could repaint a failure as a success banner — and the `--no-color` branch now keeps its own written promise: no escape byte reaches a redirected file through the data either. The plain-text default logger and the `LogError` nil-logger fallback escape the message and context they interpolate, so one record stays one line and an embedded line break cannot forge a fully-formed record at a level the operator alerts on — the json logger always had this from its encoder. Every C0 control character and DEL is rendered as its visible escape spelling (`\r`, `\n`, `\x1b`), through `internal.EscapeControlCharacters`. **Behavioural change**: output that legitimately carried control characters — a table cell relying on a raw tab, a status line relying on embedded ANSI — now renders them escaped; a newline inside a table cell still breaks the line, and the json output channels are unchanged

- cli: `--format=json` writes one document on one line, terminated by a newline, instead of an indented block. The json format is what a machine reads, and a machine reads a stream: a long-running command that renders a document per unit of work is only followable if each document is a line a reader can hand to a parser whole. `melody:cron:run` documented exactly that — "a stream of one closed document per line" — in four places while emitting twenty lines per minute, so every line-framed consumer (a log collector's json stage, a `while read line`, a `grep '"failed": true'`) received fragments that each failed to parse. Every command of the framework and of the integrations renders through the one printer, so all of them move together, and `output.IsJsonFormat` is now what the four sites asking "is this the json format?" ask, so the second spelling cannot be recognised by some of them and not by others. **Behavioural change**: output read by eye, or asserted as text with the spacing `encoding/json` puts after a colon, sees the compact spelling; `--format=json-pretty` restores the old rendering and nothing that decodes the document is affected
- cache: `InMemoryBackend.Get` and `Many` take the exclusive lock only when an entry's place in the recency list has actually gone stale, where they used to take it on every hit just to move the entry to the front. The access mark is atomic and is refreshed under the read lock on every read; the list surgery happens at most once a second per key. The recency order is approximate at that granularity — which is what an LRU with a clock does — and the eviction it feeds is a bounded probe that prefers an expired victim and never relied on an exact order, `Has` having never moved anything at all. Measured at sixteen goroutines against one key: 2.2–3.2 million reads a second before, 8.1–9.0 million after, against a `Has` control of about 10 million. **Behavioural change**: two entries read within the same second are ordered by which was written last rather than by which was read last, so an eviction choosing between them may pick the other one

- container: the teardown breaks a tie the dependency graph leaves open on **creation order, latest first**, not on the node key descending. Two services with no edge between them are closed newest first, because a service built during the construction of another was needed by it whether or not the edge was declared. The comparison this replaces was a string comparison nobody wrote: it decided by spelling alone which services still had a logger to report their shutdown through, and renaming `app.worker` to `zz.worker` was the whole difference between a shutdown record written and one dropped — measured, with the same worker keeping 219 bytes of journal under one name and 90 under the other. The logger needs no special case under the new rule: the boot resolves it first, so it is closed last. Declared edges still decide everything they cover, and the request scope uses the same rule, since the two teardowns share one walk. **Behavioural change**: a teardown order that was relying on the alphabet
- container: closing has two states, and a service's own `Close` may still resolve. `IsClosed()` answers true from the moment the teardown begins and refuses every new creation for its whole duration, as before; resolutions of what is already built keep answering until the last `Close` returns — a worker reporting its drain is entitled to the logger it reports through — and are refused with "container is closed" from then on. **Behavioural change**: see the Fixed entry for what the second state closes
- logging: the json logger takes its timestamp inside the write mutex, together with the encoding, so the order of the stamps is the order of the writes — which is what `LOGGING.md` has always promised and what the comment on that line claimed the precision was paying for. Only the normalization of the caller's own context stays outside the lock. **Behavioural change**: the encoding is serialized across writers, measured at eight goroutines against a real file as 8648 ns per record against 13755; a logger writes to one destination through one lock either way, and this is what that ordering is worth
- security/config: a firewall's access decision manager, entry point and access denied handler are refused at compilation when they hold a **typed nil**, by firewall name and naming which of the two configurations declared it; `Builder.SetGlobal` refuses the same three at the door. The matcher, the token source and the login and logout handlers were already refused by name in the same loop; these three were not, and one of them decides access. **Breaking**: a configuration that declared `var manager *myManager` and handed it over booted green before, dropped the declared role hierarchy in silence, and answered every request behind the firewall with a recovered panic

- http: `Router.AllowedMethods` applies the locale filter the matcher applies, so it no longer announces the methods of a route the path's locale excludes. A route restricted to a set of locales does not accept a request whose path carries another one, and a caller building its own `Allow` header out of this door advertised methods that answer `405` — the two readers of the locale rule are now one, `matchesLocale`, which the matcher itself calls. What this door answers is the routing table's own set: the kernel adds the synthetic `OPTIONS` and `HEAD` its `MethodPolicy` allows on top of it, and says so on the GoDoc. **Behavioural change**: only for locale-restricted routes, where the previous answer was wrong
- config: `Parameter.Int` reads the same grammar its sibling accessors read, delegating to the shared parser instead of a hand-written `strconv.Atoi`. An `int64` registered at runtime — what `RegisterRuntime("app.batch_size", int64(500))` hands over — converted through `Float` and was refused through `Int` with "cannot convert parameter value to int" and a nil cause, on a value that is plainly a whole number; an integral `float64` was refused the same way. The one thing this door adds over the shared parser is the narrowing: a value outside the `int` range is refused by name rather than truncated silently on a 32-bit build
- http: the in-process rate limiters say on their constructors that their counters live in the process and nowhere else — a restart hands every caller a full budget back, and each replica enforces the limit on its own — with the pointer to the distributed drop-in in `integrations/rueidis`. Melody says the same at boot about its two other in-process defaults, the cache backend and the session storage, and could not say it here: a limiter is wired by the application rather than by the framework
- cache: `NewJsonSerializer` writes down the hazard of a bare json document with no schema discriminant — a value written by one release decodes cleanly in the next, so a field added since reads as the zero value with no decoding error, and an entry cached with a ttl of zero never lapses to heal it. The session package carries the neighbouring warning on its file storage, and it is the other half: a JSON round trip changes the SHAPES a session comes back with — every number a `float64`, an `int64` beyond 2^53 changed — which is type drift within one schema, where this is a schema that moved under a document nothing stamps a version on
- security/config: `Compile` says what its argument implies — `Configuration` has only unexported fields and no constructor, and `Builder` never hands one out, so a caller outside the package can only pass the empty value, whose answer is a nil compiled configuration and a nil error. `Builder.BuildAndCompile` is the public path from a declaration to the runtime

- http/static: in the embedded mode the cache validators stop being derived from a modification time that does not exist. An `embed.FS` reports the zero instant for every file, so the entity tag degenerated into size alone — identical across every rebuild, which made a redeployed asset that kept its length revalidate 304 and stay served stale — and `Last-Modified`, rendered as year 1, answered 304 to every `If-Modified-Since` for the life of the deployment, since the zero instant is never later than anything. The tag is now derived from the file's size and `version.BuildVersion()`, and `Last-Modified` is not emitted at all when the filesystem carries no time. **Behavioural change**: every embedded asset revalidates once after a deploy that linked a new version — see `UPGRADE.md`

- http: a 400 whose validation errors blame the rule declaration is recorded at **error**, not at the warning a deliberate 4xx earns. A typo in a struct tag — `validate:"reqiured"`, a `regex` named without parameters, a pattern that does not compile — refuses every request that route serves, with a body naming the client's own field; at warning it sat in the journal among the users who mistyped their address while the route stayed permanently broken and nothing said so. A genuine field refusal keeps the warning level unchanged
- http: the per-field validation errors in a 400 body no longer carry the internal context of a rule-declaration fault. `rule`, `params` and `cause` name the developer's typo, the parameters the constraint refused and its own reason: the operator's material, which stays in full in the record. Every other entry is unchanged, so the bounds a numeric constraint reports still reach the client that has to correct its request. **Behavioural change** on the response body — see `UPGRADE.md`
- http: a session another request ended while this one was running is recorded at **warning**, under its own name. It is the session ending, which `SESSION.md` and `HTTP.md` both state and which the branch answering it says in as many words; at error it was byte-for-byte a storage outage, so a user who logged out in a second tab produced one "failed to save session" per concurrent request against a healthy backend. The two genuine outages on that path — a failed save and a failed delete — keep the error level
- http: the session-persistence records carry a one-way reference to the session id — a SHA-256 truncated to 16 hex characters — beside the request method and the path. Only the middle one ever named the session, and that by accident, through the context of the cause it was handed; none of them named the route, so a record that did arrive could not be tied to the request that produced it. The id itself stays out on purpose: on a failed delete the store still holds the session and on a failed save the id is one the client presented, so a verbatim id would hand anyone with log-read access a cookie they can present as that user
- http: the rate-limit request listener records the caller's own cancellation at warning under its own name instead of as a store failure at error. Its middleware twin has classified this since the fourth pass; the listener door — which meters every request, ahead of authentication, and therefore sees more client disconnects than the middleware ever does — had kept the old shape, so a client that hung up mid-round-trip paged the operator for a perfectly healthy store
- cache: the two recovery boundaries of `Remember` hand the panic value on as the cause and capture the stack of the goroutine that raised it. Stringified into the context alone, the framework's own idiom — a `MustGet` on a mistyped parameter, which panics with an error whose own context names it — reached the operator as a message and a cache key, with the cause chain empty and no file or line anywhere. **Behavioural change**: `errors.Is` and `errors.As` on the returned error now reach the failure underneath, where before they stopped at the fabricated wrapper

- httpclient: `SetHeader` stores the header under its canonical spelling, the one the constructor stores under. The map is applied to every request with `Header.Set`, which canonicalizes, so a credential rotated under a spelling other than the configured one left two live entries collapsing onto one header — and which credential travelled was decided by map iteration order, measured at 166 of 200 requests carrying the rotated-in value and 34 the rotated-out one. The collision is now structurally impossible and a rotation overwrites the entry it means to. **Behavioural change**: two spellings of one header name are one entry, and the last write wins
- config: a parameter registered after boot is published before its own template is resolved, so the resolution's secret propagation — which marks the reader it finds by name in the parameter map — can see it. A dsn assembled after boot from a credential the configuration had marked inherited nothing and printed in full beside the redacted key it was built from. Nothing observes the intermediate state: the publish, the resolution and the rollback all run under the write lock every reader takes, and a failed resolution removes the name again before the panic. **Behavioural change**: `RegisterRuntime("a", "%a%")` now reports the circular reference rather than the undefined key — both panic, both roll back, and the new message is the accurate one
- application: the http server's `ErrorLog` routes net/http's own reports into the application logger, at warning with the line in the record's context. They used to go to stderr as unstructured text, invisible to a deployment whose journal is a json file
- security: the access-control merge strategies state what they decide — the strategy orders the merged rule LIST, while the matcher resolves by category first (exact beats prefix, longer prefix beats shorter, prefix beats regex, fallback last), so position decides only what the categories leave tied; the names carried a first-match connotation the matcher's documented longest-prefix design never honoured, in an authorization decision. `Match` states the same rule from its own side
- session: `FileStorage` states the shape degradation its own flush makes invisible until the first redeploy — values reload from JSON at construction, so a session survives a restart with an `int` reading back `float64` and a struct reading back `map[string]any`, while the same session read in-process keeps the types the handler stored
- event: `RegisteredEvents` states on both dispatchers that it reports a point-in-time view a concurrent (un)installation is observed mid-step through, and `MarkListenerRequired` states the window its two-step shape forces — a dispatch between registration and mark sees the listener unmarked, so a runtime registrar must not dispatch the event until the mark is applied; boot-time registration closes the window by construction
- container: `Close` states its re-entrancy contract — the blocking that protects concurrent callers makes a Close called from inside a service's own Close a deadlock by construction, and the documented answer is the protocol `IsClosed` already describes: the flag is set before the first service Close runs, so a defensive closer that asks first skips during the teardown. The scope's opposite choice — reading a closed scope instead of blocking — is named beside it as the deliberate contrast
- container: the coalesced-waiter receive states its one assumption — the wait channel closes on every exit of the creating call, return and panic alike, so what parks the waiters forever is only a provider that neither returns nor panics, and Close deliberately does not release them because failing them open would hand out services from a teardown in progress
- httpclient: `Response.Headers` and `Response.Body` state their single-owner contract — they hand out the live map and slice on purpose, the response being the caller's own result object, and a caller fanning it across goroutines copies first
- serializer: the qvalue grammar moved to `internal.ParseQualityValue` so the three negotiating readers — the Accept parser here, `PrefersHtml` and the compression middleware — share one rule; behavior of this package is unchanged

- container: the package documentation states the close-time ownership rule the teardown has always applied — when the container itself closes, everything still standing in its maps joins the teardown, installed overrides included, because the container's lifetime is the process's and there is no opt-in; a scope is deliberately the opposite, closing only what it built, with `ClosedWithScope()` as the one door an outside override joins its teardown through
- cli: `output.StandardFlags` and `output.DebugFlags` document the quiet defaults the two have always carried: quiet defaults to true on the standard set — a command's essential output is never gated on quiet, so the flag governs headers and decoration alone — and the debug set flips it to false, because an introspection command's headers are part of its answer
- application: the http shutdown wait's documentation names the deliberate outcome of an exceeded budget — the deadline error surfaces and the process exits non-zero, because requests were lost; a graceful drain that ran out of time is the operator's signal, not a success to smooth over
- cache: the key grammar is part of the `cachecontract.Backend` promise — non-empty, no spaces or newlines, at most 1024 bytes — and the in-memory backend enforces it with the exact refusals the redis backend answers. Two implementations of one contract refused different keys, so a key built from user input worked against the development backend and failed against the production store on every operation. **Behavioural change**: an in-memory key carrying a space or a newline now refuses loudly instead of working only in development
- application: the application's own http routes register before any module's. `Boot` drains the `RegisterHttpRoute` queue ahead of the module phases, so where a root route and a module route are equal to the router — same priority, both matching — the root route wins the registration-order tie-break: `/users/me` written by the composition root used to be dispatched as a module's `/users/:id` with `id="me"`, because the module phase registered first and nothing said so. **Behavioural change**: an application that relied on a module route shadowing a root route dispatches the root route now
- application: a module's identity is its instance — the same instance reached through two providers, or registered twice, boots once. Both halves of the duplicate boot used to happen: the loud half was a duplicate service name in the boot report, and the silent half was every listener and middleware attached twice, doubling whatever side effects they carry. `Name()` stays informative and two distinct instances sharing a name stay two modules; an instance of an uncomparable type keeps the old behavior, since it cannot enter the identity set. **Behavioural change** for an application that deliberately registered one instance twice
- application: `RegisterModuleProvider` handed a provider that itself implements `Module` registers it as that module, own hooks included, followed by its children — the door used to keep only the children and silently drop the provider's own registrations, so the two registration doors registered different applications from the same value. The children of a bare provider register at expansion depth 1 on both doors. **Behavioural change**: a provider-module registered through `RegisterModuleProvider` now boots its own hooks
- application: both module doors refuse a registration arriving from inside a module boot hook, and `RegisterHttpRoute` refuses during the same window. The phase loops iterate a snapshot of the module list, so a module registered mid-boot received only the hooks of whatever phases had not run yet — booted by a fraction of the lifecycle, reported as a success — and a route registrar queued from inside a hook would never drain now that the queue empties before the module phases. A module that carries other modules implements `ModuleProvider`; a module's routes belong on `RegisterHttpRoutes`. **Breaking**: both silences are panics naming the rule
- application: the second module boot phase runs one loop per hook for the event, middleware, http and cli hooks, the granularity every other phase already had — each hook runs across every module before the next hook begins, in registration order inside each group — and the contracts now document that rule. `SecurityModule` moved beside its eight sibling hooks into `application/contract`; the name under `application` remains as an alias, so every existing implementation and assertion compiles unchanged
- application: the firewall manager is registered whenever a security configuration compiled, whatever the process mode — configured means resolvable. It was registered only in http mode, while `security.FirewallManagerMustFromContainer` carries no mode guard, so a console command of an application whose security is configured panicked "service is not registered": a message that sends the operator hunting a registration when the actual difference was the process shape, which is decided from argv and not readable through the container. The manager is a plain view of the compiled configuration with no request in it; the two kernel listeners — the enforcement — remain http-only, because a console process has no request to guard. **Behavioural change**: a console process with security configured now resolves the firewall manager instead of panicking

- cli, application: the container is closed by the recover handler that owns the process exit, on the linear path exactly as on the panic path — the cli action no longer closes it after the command returns. Closing it there closed the file logger the final record must travel through, so the record of a command that ran and failed reached stderr alone while a mistyped command name — refused before the action, container untouched — landed in the application log: the promise held for the trivial refusal and broke for the real failure. The action still closes the scope and still reports the scope's teardown failure beside the command's own error. **Behavioural change**: `cli.Register`'s action leaves the container open for the exit owner, and a container-close failure after a successful command surfaces through the application teardown — exit 1 with the failure recorded — instead of being folded into the command's aggregated error
- http: the kernel exception listener records a 4xx at warning level. A deliberate refusal a handler returns — a 403, a 404, a validation 422 — was filed at error under "unhandled exception", so a routine denial read as an incident and an operator filtering at error received every one of them; a 5xx and every error that is not an http exception keep the error level. **Behavioural change**: the level of an existing record moves from error to warning for status codes below 500
- application: the logger registration is gated behind `Has`, like the cache, the session storage and the firewall manager beside it. The logger was the one default service the application or a module could never substitute: its registration was unconditional, so bringing your own produced a guaranteed duplicate-registration report at boot. A substituted logger is still resolved eagerly at boot — see the Fixed entry on the swallowed logger failure — so one registered wrong fails the boot naming the logger rather than the first request that needed it

- logging: `LogOnRecoverAndExit` and `LogOnRecoverAndExitAfter` refuse an exit code outside 1..255 with a panic naming the rule — the rule `exception.NewExitError` has always enforced, applied to the code the handler itself would exit with. Zero silences the stderr echo and makes `os.Exit(0)` report success after a fatal panic, and anything past 255 is rewritten by the operating system, which keeps only the low 8 bits. The refusal runs before the no-panic return, so a caller wired with a bad code is caught deterministically on its first healthy pass rather than on its first panic months later. **Breaking**: a caller passing 0 used to get a silent success-on-panic and now gets a panic at the first run
- logging: a recovered `*exception.ExitError` carrying an out-of-range code is no longer honored as an exit. The zero value is constructible around the constructor that enforces 1..255 and answers code 0, so a process dying through a fatal panic exited reporting success — the record written, the stderr echo silent, the failure invisible to every supervisor. The wrapper is normalized under the caller's exit code with an error naming the anomaly, the same direction the typed nil already takes and without a panic in the one handler that must not panic; a valid wrapper keeps its own code exactly as before. **Behavioural change**: the process exits with the caller's non-zero code instead of 0 for a zero-value exit error
- logging: each step of the exit handler — the record, the before-exit teardown — is abandoned after ten seconds instead of being waited on forever. The steps stand between a fatal failure and `os.Exit`, so a single `Close` that never returns — a drain on a channel nobody feeds, a lock somebody died holding — turned a dying process into a hung one: the record written, the exit never taken, nothing for a supervisor to restart. The abandonment writes one stderr line naming the step and the budget and the exit proceeds; the budget is a package constant because this package cannot read the configuration — the logger it builds is what the configuration is loaded through. **Behavioural change**: a teardown still running at the budget is cut mid-flight by the process exit instead of postponing it forever

- security: `AccessDecisionManager.DecideAll` refuses an empty attribute list instead of granting it. Read as an AND over nothing it was vacuously true, so a caller that asked for no attribute at all was granted — an attribute list a configuration value resolved away, or a variadic call that lost its argument — while `DecideAny`, the sibling on the same contract, refused the identical input. The compiled access control cannot produce an empty list, which is precisely why the refusal matters: it is reached only by a caller going straight to the decision manager, the one caller nothing else guards. Symfony answers the same shape denied, its strategy over zero results falling back to a flag that defaults to refusing. **Behavioural change**: a direct caller passing no attributes was granted and is now refused
- security: a typed nil is refused where a nil is refused, on every interface-typed piece of a firewall — the matcher, the token source, the login and logout handlers, a rule, an authenticator, a decision voter and the token a security token wraps. An interface holding a nil pointer is not equal to nil, so such a value passed the eager validation at the definition site, passed `Compile`, and was first dereferenced on the request path, inside no recovery, taking the process down on a wiring mistake that had two chances to be caught at boot. The reflective check the rest of the framework uses now decides at both walls, and a stateless firewall handed a typed-nil login handler reads it as the absent handler it is rather than as a contradictory configuration. **Breaking**: an application that registers a typed nil booted and failed on its first request; it now fails the boot, naming the piece and the firewall
- security: the constructors that keep a caller's slice copy it, matching the copy they already make when handing it back. `NewFirewall`, `NewAuthenticatorManager`, `NewAccessDecisionManager`, `NewCompiledFirewall`, `NewCompiledConfiguration` and the builder's firewall registration retained the slice header they were given, so a caller that kept its own reference could swap a rule, an authenticator or a voter after the boot that validated them — past the nil checks, past compilation, into the decision path of a live request. `Rules()`, `Voters()` and `Firewalls()` had guarded the read side of exactly this for as long as they have existed

- internal: `Duration` refuses a bare `int`/`int64`. A number without a unit was read as **nanoseconds** — the Go-native interpretation and almost never the caller's — so a runtime parameter registered as `30` and read through `Parameter.Duration()` became a timeout that fired instantly, with no error anywhere, while the same value spelled `"30"` was refused by `time.ParseDuration` for missing its unit. Both spellings are refused the same way now, with the missing unit named as the cause; a `time.Duration` and a string with a unit remain the two accepted numeric shapes. **Behavioural change**
- internal: `Float64` refuses NaN and the infinities on every branch. `strconv.ParseFloat` parses `"NaN"`, `"Inf"` and `"Infinity"` without an error, and the typed `float64`/`float32` branches passed a non-finite value through untouched — so `MELODY_RATIO=NaN` reached the caller as a value every ordered comparison is false against, and a threshold guard written the normal way silently stopped guarding. The sibling `Int` refuses the same shapes explicitly, with a hand-written comment explaining exactly this hazard; the two agree now. A config parameter or bag value declared float is guaranteed finite or refused. **Behavioural change**
- internal: `MapStringString` reports a typed-nil map as absent. Reported as present it came back as an empty non-nil map — `nil == value` is false for an interface holding a nil map — so a caller branching on the presence flag to apply a default silently got the empty map instead, where the strict accessors beside it answer absent for a nil value. **Behavioural change**
- clock: `FrozenClock.Advance` is forward-only and panics on a negative duration. A negative one silently moved the frozen clock backwards and broke the monotonic invariants the code under test relies on — an idle-pruning threshold compared against a now that went back deletes nothing, forever. `TravelTo` remains the deliberate door for backwards motion. **Behavioural change**
- clock: the frozen ticker's `Stop` returns only after its relay goroutine has exited, so no tick can be minted after `Stop` returns. Without the wait, a tick already pending inside the runtime ticker could be taken after `Stop` came back, its timestamp sampled from the clock after the caller had moved on — a Stop-then-TravelTo sequence found the traveled time in the channel of a stopped ticker, a tick that `time.Ticker` can never produce. A tick accepted into the buffered channel before `Stop` may still be read afterwards, exactly as with `time.Ticker`
- clock: the `Ticker` and `Clock` contracts state their obligations in the GoDoc, and the documentation carries them. `Stop` is mandatory — the frozen ticker owns a relay goroutine, and abandoning it leaks that goroutine and pins the ticker and its clock for the life of the process — idempotent, and forbidden from closing the channel; the frozen clock's ticker is named for what it is: driven by real wall time, with `Advance`/`TravelTo` never firing a tick and each tick carrying the frozen `Now()` at delivery time. The rules lived in a private comment inside the frozen ticker, which implementers of the interface never see — an in-tree test fake closed its channel on `Stop`, surviving only through the order of two defers
- internal: `testhelper.AssertPanics` is removed. It passed on any recovered value, so it could not tell the guard under test firing from the code crashing for an unrelated reason three lines later — deleting the guard kept its test green. `AssertPanicsWithError`, which pins the panic's identity by message and is what every call site already uses, is the remaining form; it now also names a typed-nil error panic value instead of crashing the test binary at its own line. **Breaking** for a consumer of the internal test helper, which the package documentation marks as free to change
- internal: `testhelper.HttpTestRequest.Header` mirrors the production request and dereferences its underlying request unguarded. It was the one accessor that masked a zero-value `HttpTestRequest` assembled around the constructor — a middleware under test read an absent `Authorization` header from it and the test proved the wrong branch, while the next accessor crashed inside the code under test
- httpclient: a client configured with a base url refuses an absolute url that leaves that origin. The base url was applied only to a relative target, so an absolute one bypassed it silently and the request went wherever the url string pointed — carrying the headers and the authorization the client was configured with, to a host chosen by whatever produced that string. That is the leak the redirect policy exists to stop, one hop earlier: the policy strips the api key when the server at the base url redirects the client away, while the same key travelled freely to a foreign host named in the call itself. A client that talks to more than one origin is built without a base url, and one built without a base url is unchanged: it is the client that goes anywhere. **Behavioural change**
- httpclient: a basic credential travels whenever the caller asked for one, empty halves included. Basic auth was attached only when the username was non-empty, so `WithBasicAuth("", key)` — the ordinary shape of curl's `-u :key`, an api key spelled as the password of an empty user, and legal per RFC 7617 — sent the request unauthenticated with no error to say the credential had been dropped; the failure surfaced as a 401 from the far end, or as data served to an anonymous caller. An empty password was already accepted, so the two halves of one credential were judged by different rules. A bearer token still wins over a basic credential, because the two cannot share one Authorization header, and the contract says so now. **Behavioural change**
- httpclient: an empty target names the base resource itself. The join appended a slash unconditionally, so a client based at `https://host/v1` could not request `/v1` — every call reached `/v1/`, which a server distinguishing the two answers with a redirect or a 404 — and the base resource was unreachable through the client configured for it. An explicit `"/"` still names the trailing slash. The base url remains a **prefix**, deliberately unlike the RFC 3986 reference resolution Symfony and Guzzle implement, where an absolute path replaces the base path entirely; the divergence is stated in the documentation rather than left to be discovered. **Behavioural change**
- httpclient: a nil `RequestOption` is refused instead of called. An option chosen by a condition whose other branch produced nothing is a nil function value, and invoking it panicked on the request path, outside any recovery this package owns; the refusal names the position in the option list. **Behavioural change**
- httpclient: `NewHttpClient` refuses a nil configuration where the wiring mistake is made. It dereferenced the argument to read the timeout, so a nil died on an anonymous nil dereference instead of naming what was missing — while the sibling `resolveTransportConfig` handles its own nil argument explicitly and `NewHttpClientConfig` tolerates nil headers. `NewDefaultHttpClient` is the constructor that asks for the defaults. **Behavioural change**
- httpclient: a negative per-request timeout bounds a stream instead of unbounding it. `RequestStream` read a non-positive duration as "no deadline" only for zero and let a negative one through to the unbounded client, so a caller computing what remained of a deadline that had already passed — the shape that produces a negative duration — got a stream that runs forever out of an exhausted budget, where the same input on `Request` fell back to the configured timeout. **Behavioural change**
