- event: a dispatch that skipped a listener marked required refuses the response the stopping listener produced. The http kernel failed closed on a `kernel.request` or `kernel.controller` dispatch error only when no listener had set a response, and a listener that stops propagation to answer the request itself — an http cache serving a hit, at a priority above the access-control listener's 20 — sets one: the error naming the skipped access control was logged and the cached page was served to whoever asked for it, so a cached `/admin` page reached an anonymous client. The refusal is carried by a distinct type, `event.RequiredListenerSkippedError`, which is what lets the kernel separate this class from an ordinary listener failure; the response the stopping listener produced is closed rather than written. A listener that legitimately short-circuits past a required listener says so with `MarkListenerMaySkipRequiredListeners`, and one that only stops propagation with nothing required behind it still answers the request exactly as before. The kernel judges the error on itself rather than through its cause chain, so an application event dispatched by a listener, skipping a required listener of its own, stays an ordinary listener failure. **Behavioural change**
- event: a listener that stops propagation and fails in the same call is reported for the listeners it skipped. Its own failure was returned before the required listeners behind it were weighed, so the caller learned that a listener had failed and never that access control had been skipped — the same fail-open the required mark exists to close, reached one line earlier. The skipped required listener is reported first; a stopping failure that skipped nothing required still reports the listener's own error. **Behavioural change**
- event: an event that arrives already stopped runs no listener. The propagation test closed the iteration rather than opening it, so re-dispatching the event a previous dispatch returned — `Dispatch` hands back the same object — ran exactly the first listener and then named that listener as the one that stopped propagation, in an error about required listeners it had never reached. **Behavioural change**
- event: `AddSubscriber` refuses a second registration of a subscriber already registered, naming the type. A subscriber that carries no fields occupies no memory, and every zero-size allocation in Go answers one address, so two instances of such a type — the natural shape of a stateless subscriber — were one identity to the dispatcher: `RemoveSubscriber` for either instance took both instances' listeners down and reported a plausible count for it. Nothing in the two values can tell them apart, so the ambiguity is refused where it is created instead of resolved where it is used. The refusal also catches the same subscriber registered twice by two boot paths, which ran every one of its listeners twice. **Behavioural change**
- event: `AddSubscriber` validates every subscribed event before registering any listener. Validation ran as it registered, so a subscriber whose second event was malformed was left half-installed — the listeners of the first one live and firing under a subscriber the caller had been told was refused. **Behavioural change**
- event: `AddSubscriber` refuses a subscriber that declares no subscribed events, and an event name mapped to an empty list. Both registered nothing while reporting success, which is how a subscriber assembled from configuration — an audit listener behind a flag that was never set — ends up silently absent. **Behavioural change**
- event: `MarkListenerRequired` and `MarkListenerMaySkipRequiredListeners` refuse a registration the dispatcher does not hold, naming the event and the listener id. An unknown registration was a documented no-op, so a mark applied to a stale registration, or to one issued by a different dispatcher, left the fail-closed guarantee unarmed while reporting that it had been applied — and the caller had no way to tell. **Behavioural change**
- event: `EventDispatcherAdapter` refuses to mark a required listener over a wrapped dispatcher that cannot mark one, naming the wrapped type. Callers probe for `RequiredListenerRegistrar` precisely to learn whether the guarantee is available, and the adapter satisfies that probe on its own behalf, so absorbing the mark answered the probe yes and left the firewall unarmed in production. **Behavioural change**
- event: `contract.RegisteredListener` reports the required-listener marks a listener carries, and `debug:events --verbose` renders them in a `required` column. An unarmed fail-closed guarantee was indistinguishable from an armed one at every level of inspection. **Breaking**: the two added fields break an out-of-tree composite literal of `RegisteredListener` written without field names
- event: `NewEventDispatcherAdapter` takes the dispatcher alone. The clock it also required was validated at construction and never read, and the documentation had described the one-argument signature all along. **Breaking**: the call sites of the constructor
- event: an `*exception.ExitError` raised by a listener travels to whoever owns the process boundary instead of being folded into a listener error. A deliberate exit was reported as an ordinary request failure with its code discarded, against the policy `logging.LogOnRecover` states and implements for the same value. **Behavioural change**

- cli: `output.MergeFlags` refuses a duplicated flag name at the line that declares it. The flag parser resolves a name to the FIRST declaration, so a command-specific flag reusing a standard name — its default, its validator — was silently inert, with the help output listing the name twice as the only trace: an application flag `--limit` with a default of 100 and a ceiling validator parsed through the standard flag instead, whose default of 0 means unlimited. **Behavioural change**
- cli: `TableBlockBuilder.AddRow` refuses a row whose cell count disagrees with the block's declared columns, naming the block and both counts. The printer sizes and prints by the columns alone, so a surplus cell silently never rendered and a missing one rendered as an empty cell nobody intended — a report reading complete while it is not. The single-token separator row remains admitted; it is a marker the printer expands to the full width. **Behavioural change**
- cli: the standard integer flags — `--verbosity`, `--limit`, `--offset`, `--table-width` — refuse a negative value at parsing, naming the flag, the way `--format` and `--order` have always refused an unsupported one. The normalization clamped a negative to zero, and zero means unlimited for the limit and "from the start" for the offset, so an argument asking for less than nothing silently delivered everything; the clamp stays as the defensive floor for an `Option` assembled in code. **Behavioural change**
- cli: the table format prints the warnings under `--quiet` too, and renders the envelope error whole — message, code, details, cause. Quiet suppresses the decorative headers, but a warning is the one thing a command said beside its result, and with `StandardFlags` defaulting quiet to true an application command's `AddWarning` was invisible in the default format at every verbosity, `-vvv` included; the warning details stay behind the verbose flag. The envelope error rendered nowhere at all in the table format — the code, the details and the cause existed only in the json document — leaving the red one-line echo as the entire failure report. **Behavioural change**
- cli: a failure reported through the rendered envelope reaches the application log. `output.Render` and the command-suggestion refusal pre-marked their exit-coded errors as logged on the premise that the rendered report is the record — but the report lives only on the output streams, so a run that failed was invisible to anything reading the log file; the errors now travel unmarked and the exit path writes them through the logger it resolves while the container is still open. **Behavioural change**
- cli: a table printing failure fails the command. Every table write discarded its error, so a report truncated by a full disk ended with a success banner and exit zero while the json path has always propagated its encoding and write failures; the first write failure is now remembered and returned. **Behavioural change**
- http/bag: the request bags keep the single and the repeated key apart by type, and `Request.Input` delivers the query and form values it silently lost. Go's url.Values carries every value as a list even for a key that appeared once, the bags stored that list verbatim, and the lax string accessor answered the empty string for any list — so `Input("term")` on `?term=melody` returned `""` for a value the client sent, `StringOrDefault` skipped its default, and `HasNonEmptyString` answered false for a provided parameter, on every query and every form, with route parameters the only source that worked. One occurrence is now stored as the string it is; a genuinely repeated key stays a slice, and reading it as one string panics naming the key and pointing to `StringSlice`/`StringAt` — the Symfony separation, where a scalar read never guesses at an array. **Behavioural change**
- http: a form that does not parse is refused the way a body that does not read is. `ParseForm` failing — an invalid percent escape in a urlencoded body — logged one warning and handed the handler an empty form, so a real submission was processed as "field missing" and answered 200; the failure is now recorded on the request and the kernel answers 400 before the handler runs, symmetric with the read-failure refusal. **Behavioural change**
- config: the template scanner finds the real `")%"` closer and refuses what silently survived as text. The scan stopped at the first `)`, so `%env(A))%` and a dsn whose closing percent was forgotten — `postgres://user:%env(DB_PASS)@db` — kept their literal spelling in the resolved value and the application authenticated with placeholder text as its password; the candidate now runs to the first `")%"` no percent interrupts, an unterminated `%env(` is an error, and a `%name` reference a percent opened and nothing closed (`%app-name%`) is refused too — the contract already demands a literal percent be doubled. The percent-interrupted protection for adjacent placeholders is untouched. **Behavioural change** ×3, all fail-closed at boot
- config: a braced `${...}` reference in a `.env` value whose name breaks the key grammar is refused instead of surviving literally — `${DB-PASS}` rode into the dsn as text with no signal, while nobody types `${...}` into a password by accident; the bare-dollar grammar that keeps `pa$sword` and `$1.50` data is untouched, and the refusal names the enclosing key, never the content. **Behavioural change**
- config: the `.env` trailing comment is cut once, by godotenv's own countback. The preprocessor cut at the FIRST whitespace-preceded hash and godotenv then cut again, so `GREETING=hello # world # x` read as `hello` where godotenv had always read `hello # world`; the preprocessor now leaves the post-separator text alone and only drops whole-line comments. **Behavioural change** for values carrying more than one hash mark
- config: the `.env` preprocessor walks bytes, not runes. The rune round-trip re-encoded everything invalid as UTF-8 through U+FFFD, so a file saved as Latin-1 had its quoted password silently rewritten and the credential sent to the database differed from the one in the file — godotenv alone passes a quoted value through untouched. **Behavioural change** for non-UTF-8 files, which now read byte-exact
- config: `MarkSecret` after the boot resolution travels to every parameter that reads the marked key, and follows the marking to a fixpoint — a reader of a freshly marked name is scanned in turn, so a whole derivation chain is covered however late the mark arrives, the way the early marking always did — the late call redacted the key while the dsn assembled from it printed in full in `debug:parameters`, and the end-of-boot retry reported that unapplied markings had landed when their propagation had nothing left to travel through. A match inside escaped text over-marks, which errs toward redacting more, never less. **Behavioural change**
- config: `IntWithDefault` answers the default only for an absent (or typed-nil) parameter; one that exists but does not parse panics — `1O0` silently became the default and the operator believed the configured value was live. **Behavioural change**
- config: a runtime parameter name is judged trimmed: the whitespace-only name passed the empty guard and registered a phantom, the padded name registered a parameter no exact-match lookup could ever reach; both are refused at the registration line, the whitespace-only one as the empty name it is. **Behavioural change**
- bag: `ParameterBag.All` copies as deep as the bag's own writers go — a `[]string` or `map[string]string` handed back live aliased the stored value, and a caller mutating the copy wrote into the bag behind its lock. **Behavioural change** for callers that relied on the aliasing

- cache: `NewManager` builds a manager that does not own its backend — Close leaves it open. On the container path both are registered services and the container closes each one itself, so the cascade closed the backend twice: benign on the idempotent in-memory backend, a reported failure on any backend wrapping a connection whose second Close answers with one, and a premature close for every other service still depending on the backend the manager reached first. **Behavioural change**: a hand-built manager whose Close used to stop the backend now leaves that to whoever built the backend; `NewManagerOwningBackend` keeps the cascade
- cache: a closed `InMemoryBackend` refuses every operation with `cache backend is closed` instead of silently serving a map whose cleanup goroutine is gone — an entry written after Close was never reclaimed by anything and grew the map for the rest of the process, while Close had already reported the backend gone; Close itself stays idempotent. **Behavioural change**: reads and writes that raced a shutdown past Close answered success and now answer the error, the same way the in-memory session storage already does
- cache: the in-memory backend refuses the degenerate inputs it silently absorbed. The empty key was a real key — every caller whose key came up empty shared one entry, serving one user's data under another's miss, and the same call errors on the rueidis backend, so the bug surfaced only on the backend switch; it now answers `cache key is empty` on every operation. A negative ttl stored an immortal entry — the exact opposite of a ttl computed from an already-passed deadline — and now answers `cache ttl is negative` on `Set` and `SetMultiple`, with zero keeping its documented meaning of no expiry. `Increment` and `Decrement` on an existing empty or blank payload adopted it as a zero counter and overwrote it — destroying a marker entry where redis INCRBY errors, and diverging from `GetCounter`, which refused the very same value — and now answer `cache value is not a valid int64` like any other textual payload. A negative `maxItems` silently meant unbounded — a ceiling computed wrong out of a configuration disarmed eviction the operator believed was armed, invisible even to the boot warning, which covers only the backend melody registers itself — and now panics at construction. **Behavioural change** ×4
- cache: a payload the serializer cannot decode is a miss for `Remember`, not a terminal failure. The deserialization error from the initial read reached the caller and the callback never ran, so a key corrupted out-of-band answered the same error on every call until the entry lapsed — which a ttl of zero postpones forever — while the callback would have repaired it; the error is now typed (`DeserializationError`), `Remember` recomputes over it and the winning `Set` overwrites the corrupt payload, with stampede protection bounding the recomputation to one leader. Every other error keeps meaning the cache itself failed. **Behavioural change**
- cache: `Manager.Many` no longer discards the whole answer over one corrupt entry, and the error finally names its culprits. One payload that failed to deserialize threw away every good value already decoded and surfaced as an anonymous encoding error — with several corrupt keys, even which error came back depended on map iteration order. The corrupt entry is now left out the way an absent key is — `Get` answers the same entry with exists false — and the good values come back beside a `DeserializationError` naming the corrupt keys deterministically, in requested order. **Behavioural change**
- cache: the zero-value `RememberOption` reads as the constructor defaults. The struct is exported with unexported fields, so `&RememberOption{}` was constructible only as the zero value — which silently disarmed the stampede protection it never asked to configure and carried a zero waitTimeout that made every miss answer an instant timeout; it now means what `NewDefaultRememberOption()` means, and a deliberate protection-off option built through the constructor carries waitTimeout -1 and stays what it says. Separately, a zero waitTimeout set explicitly means no waiting rather than no answer: a result the in-flight call has already memoized is taken without blocking, and only a flight still in the air answers with the timeout. **Behavioural change**
- cache: `Remember` coalesces concurrent callers only for pointer-kind `Cache` implementations, whose address tells instances apart. A value-kind implementation shared one flight per type: two different instances with different backends collapsed onto one leader, the second caller received the value computed for somebody else's cache and its own cache was never populated. A value-kind instance now runs every callback itself — the stampede optimization is the price, the answer never is. **Behavioural change** for value-kind implementations only; the framework's own `Manager` is a pointer

- validation: a parameterized rule named without parameters fails closed instead of silently validating with the registered singleton. A bare `regex` or `regex()` validated with the match-everything `.*` default — accepting every value on a field that visibly declares a pattern — a bare `max` enforced a limit of 100 nobody wrote, and a bare `lessThan` meant "less than 0", refusing every positive number; a mistyped parameter name already failed closed, so the missing parameter list was the one permissive gap. The registered instance is a template for `WithParams`, never a fallback configuration, and the contract now says so. **Behavioural change**: a tag naming a parameterized constraint without parameters answered with the singleton's configuration and now yields `invalidRuleSyntax` on every value
- validation: the regex constraint refuses an empty pattern. `regex=` handed `WithParams` an empty string that compiled to a regular expression matching everything, so the tag silently enforced nothing; a pattern genuinely meant to match everything can say so explicitly. **Behavioural change**: `regex=` and `regex(pattern=)` accepted every value and now yield `invalidRuleSyntax` on every value
- validation: a numeric rule parameter is an integer in its entirety or it is refused. The parse accepted a valid leading integer and dropped the rest, so `lessThan=-0.5` became a bound of 0 — accepting -0.2, which the tag as written refuses, the one direction that widens what gets in — `lessThan=1e3` became "less than 1", and `max=10.5` a limit of 10; the documentation described the fractional truncation, but no reading of it covers `1e3` or `5abc`. `min`/`max` additionally refuse a negative bound: a length is never negative, so `max=-1` rejected every value with a message naming an impossible limit while `min=-1` was a silent no-op. **Behavioural change**: a fractional, suffixed or negative-length bound validated with a different number than written and now yields `invalidRuleSyntax` on every value
- validation: the string-form constraints — `regex`, `email`, `alpha`, `alphanumeric`, `numeric` — refuse a value that is not a string instead of silently passing it. A `regex` on a `[]byte` field never ran its pattern against the bytes it was written for, and an `any` field passed every string-form rule whatever it carried, while the numeric constraints refuse the types they cannot interpret; the asymmetry is gone. A nil pointer and the empty string still pass, so optional-field composition with `notBlank` is unchanged. **Behavioural change**: a string-form rule on a non-string field passed every value and now refuses every value
- validation: `min`, `max` and `notBlank` measure strings, not Go renderings. The value was fmt-formatted before measuring, so an empty slice passed `min=1` because its rendering `[]` is two runes long, a `[]byte` of five bytes failed `max=5` as the 21 runes of its rendering, a larger float rendered shorter than a smaller one once the exponent form kicked in, and `notBlank` accepted `false`, `0`, the empty slice and the empty map — a consent checkbox bound to a `bool` passed the rule that was written to require it. All three now refuse a non-string value; `notEmpty` is the constraint that understands collections. **Behavioural change**: `min`/`max`/`notBlank` on a non-string field measured its rendering and now refuse the type
- validation: a `validate` tag that parses to no rule at all is refused. `validate:","` survived the empty/skip-marker guard and produced zero rules with no error, leaving a field that visibly declares validation silently unenforced — the one hole in an otherwise fail-closed parser. **Behavioural change**: a tag of only separators was a silent no-op and now yields `invalidRuleSyntax`
- http: the per-field detail of a failed validation reaches the client. `BindJsonAndValidate` attached the validation errors to the exception context, but the exception listener never rendered an `HttpException` context — not even in debug mode — so the client of a failed validation received `{"error":"validation failed"}` with every field, message and code the validator computed thrown away, and `ValidationError.MarshalJSON` had no caller at all. The listener now includes the `errors` context key of an http exception in the JSON body — that key only, so the rest of an exception's context stays private. **Behavioural change**: the 400 body of a failed `BindJsonAndValidate` now carries an `errors` array with `field`, `message`, `code` and `context` per violation

- security: a global access control declared without any firewall is enforced rather than silently discarded. `Builder.BuildAndCompile` returned nil the moment no firewall was registered, so a module that set only a global policy through `SetGlobal` compiled to nothing, registered neither kernel listener, and left every route open — the access control listener already falls back to the global control and the runtime is tested for exactly that shape, but the compiler refused to produce it. A global-only policy now compiles into an enforcing configuration; with no firewall to authenticate against, a protected path answers `401` rather than `200`. A configuration with neither firewall nor global policy still compiles to nil. **Behavioural change**: an application that declared a global access control but no firewall enforced nothing and now enforces it
- security: the zero value of `config.FirewallOverrideConfiguration` inherits the global access control the way its constructor does. `NewFirewallOverrideConfiguration` sets the inherit flag, but the fields are unexported and a firewall added with a bare `FirewallOverrideConfiguration{}` literal — which compiles and reads like "no overrides" — carried an empty non-nil access control that never fell back to the global policy, opening every route behind the firewall while `AccessControlSource()` still reported `firewall`. An unconfigured override now inherits the global control; the constructor path is unchanged. **Behavioural change**: a firewall configured with the zero-value override enforced nothing and now inherits the global rules
- security: `RoleVoter` and `RoleHierarchyVoter` deny a token that is not authenticated even when it carries roles. The http path already refuses an unauthenticated request before the vote, but a handler that calls a decision manager directly reached the voter, and the roles alone decided — `RoleHierarchyVoter` even rebuilt the token to strip its identity while keeping the roles, a neutralisation the delegate never read. Both voters now return denied for an unauthenticated token. **Behavioural change**: a direct decision-manager caller passing an unauthenticated token with roles was granted and is now denied
- security: `SecurityContextFromRuntime` returns `(nil, false)` when the security context cannot be resolved instead of panicking. It is reached from `IsGranted`, which a handler can call from a goroutine that outlives the request, and the kernel closes the request scope on the way out; resolving the logger through the panicking `Must` variant on a closed scope crashed that goroutine, which no recover covers, taking every in-flight request with it. The logger is now read without panicking and a failed resolution logs nothing and answers not-granted
- security: the authorization denial paths never write a nil response. When a `kernel.exception` dispatch produced no response — no exception listener registered, or a higher-priority one stopped propagation — the listener wrote that nil back to the request event, which the kernel reads as "no decision" and serves the handler for; an entry point that returned no response left the same gap. Both now fall through to a fail-closed response, matching the guard the kernel itself carries on the request dispatch
- security: a failed event dispatch on a refusal path keeps the real error as the cause rather than replacing it. When authentication failed and the `security.login.failure` dispatch itself errored, the login-failure dispatch error was returned in place of the authentication error, so a bad password surfaced as a `500` with the reason gone from the log instead of the `401` the authenticator raised; `CompiledFirewall.Login` and `Logout` had the same shape. The authentication and business errors are now wrapped as the cause so the status they carry survives the exception listener's cause-chain walk
- security: an access denied handler that fails keeps the authorization decision as the cause. The handler returning `(nil, error)` replaced the decision error with the handler error, turning a `403` into whatever the render failure mapped to — usually a `500` — and dropping the refused attributes; the sibling branch for a nil response already wrapped it. The handler failure is now recorded in context with the decision kept as the cause
- security: `CompiledFirewall.Logout` fails closed on a nil result the way `Login` does. A logout handler returning `(nil, nil)` dispatched the logout-success event and returned a nil result, which the caller dereferenced on the request path; it now returns a clean error before the event is emitted
- security: the token-source recovery survives a nil token source. The deferred handler read the source's name off the firewall to build its diagnostic — the same source that had just panicked — so a nil token source panicked a second time after `recover` and the original diagnostic escaped unrecovered; the name is now read defensively and the request still fails closed with an anonymous context
- security: `NewApiKeyHeaderRule` rejects a nil matcher at construction, the way the other firewall dependencies are validated, so a configuration mistake fails at boot rather than panicking in `Applies` on the request path, outside any recovery
- security: a role hierarchy or decision manager that neither the firewall nor the global configuration declares is reported as `SourceNone` rather than `SourceFirewall`, so a debug panel no longer claims a manager that does not exist at the point the runtime answers that it is missing
- security: `NewAccessControlRawPrefixRule` refuses `PUBLIC_ACCESS`. A raw prefix matches across segment boundaries, so a loosely written public rule opened every path that merely began with the prefix and, being the longest match, shadowed a correctly bounded rule that would have denied — `/api/public` marked public shadowed `/api` restricted to a role on `/api/publications/secret`. A public path is declared through a segment prefix (`AllowAnonymous`), an exact rule, or a regex rule, none of which reach past their own boundary. **Breaking**: `NewAccessControlRawPrefixRule` now panics at the definition site when `PUBLIC_ACCESS` is among its attributes; declare the public path with one of the bounded constructors. `NewAccessControlRule`, the segment-bounded default, keeps accepting `PUBLIC_ACCESS` — a segment-bounded public rule cannot shadow a bounded denial the way a raw one can

- container: the protected `service.` namespace holds at both lifetimes. The override path has always refused to substitute a `service.`-prefixed name, but a scoped registration of the same name — with `Replacing()` — performed exactly that substitution inside every scope, which is where the kernel resolves; `RegisterScoped`, on the container and on a live scope, now refuses the prefix outright. **Behavioural change**: a scoped registration of a `service.`-prefixed name was accepted and silently shadowed the protected singleton per scope, and is now refused at the registration line
- container: a closed container refuses new registrations and new overrides the way its scoped registrar always has. A registration was accepted silently and named a service no resolution would ever build; an override landed in a map the teardown had already swept, served by later lookups and closed by nobody. The read paths are untouched: what was built keeps being served, so a request racing the shutdown degrades gracefully and the last-gasp panic report still reaches its logger. **Behavioural change**: `Register` and `OverrideInstance`/`OverrideProtectedInstance` on a closed container reported success and now return the container-is-closed error
- container: an override must fit every type its name is registered under. The propagation wrote the value into the type-keyed map with no assignability check — the provider contract's call-time guard never sees overrides — so `OverrideInstance("db", "a string")` made `GetByType(*sql.DB)` hand out the string and poisoned the type cache with it. **Behavioural change**: a type-incompatible override reported success and now returns a refusal naming the registered type and the value's
- container: `Has` and `HasType` answer under the same suspension `Get` enforces. A container-owned provider asking about a scope-only name heard "yes" from the very entries its `Get` refuses, so the Has-then-MustGet idiom panicked and a process-lifetime service could shape its wiring on one request's substitutes. **Behavioural change**: inside a container-owned provider resolved through a scope, `Has`/`HasType` of a scope-only entry answered true and now answer false
- container: a typed-nil provider function is refused where it is registered. The untyped-nil checks pass a nil `Provider[T]` handed in as a variable, and the signature of a nil function validates perfectly, so boot reported success for a service whose every resolution then failed with a contained call-of-nil panic; the refusal now lands at the registration line, through the one gate all three registration paths share. **Behavioural change**: registering a typed-nil provider succeeded and now errors — `MustRegister` panics at boot
- container: two DIFFERENT types that share one identity key refuse to coexist. The key of a pointer-to-unnamed-composite type drops its package path, so `*[]alpha.Bus` and `*[]beta.Bus` from two same-short-named packages read identically — one creation-guard entry and one close node for two distinct types, which reported a legitimate resolution of the one as a circular dependency through the other and merged what the teardown should order. The second registration is refused at the boot line that declares it; named types and generic instantiations were never affected. **Behavioural change**: the colliding second registration was accepted, mismatched at resolution, and now fails the boot
- container: `Replacing()`'s documentation says what the code does. It promised the waiver in both directions while only the scoped registration paths read it, and called an in-advance declaration inert while the waiver in fact stands for a later container registration of the same name; the option and contract now document the scoped-only direction, the standing waiver, and that the waiver covers the name and the registered type together

- application: an http process whose `.env` artifacts contributed no keys at all refuses to boot. Every built-in parameter has a development default, so a production binary run from a directory without its `.env` files served as `dev` — debug log level, the http profiler listener, the debug commands — announced by nothing louder than one warning; the refusal names the directory that was searched and the remedy, the way the resolve failure already did. A cli process stays permissive: development commands legitimately run without any environment file, and a command takes its configuration with it when it exits. **Behavioural change**: an http boot on zero loaded environment keys served on development defaults and now fails at boot
- application: a middleware factory that yields nil is refused when the pipeline is built. The pipeline skips a nil middleware without recording it anywhere — not even the inactive report — so the operator who registered a rate limiter through `RegisterHttpMiddlewareFactories` ran every request without it and was told nothing; the typed-nil yield was the same absence one assertion later, joining the chain and panicking per request. The registration-time guard on `Use` now reads through the interface as well. **Behavioural change**: a factory yielding nil was silently dropped from the chain and now fails the pipeline build, naming the definition
- http: a route indistinguishable from one already registered is refused where it is declared. Registration was the single channel with no duplicate handling — services, parameters and cli commands all land in the aggregated boot report — so two unnamed routes on one method and pattern were both stored and the later one could never be dispatched: the tie falls to the first registered, and the shadowing was invisible everywhere. The identity is everything the matcher discriminates on — pattern, methods, host, schemes, locales, requirements, priority — so host-based routing, method splits and requirement splits stay accepted; the name and the defaults stay out because neither participates in matching. **Behavioural change**: an exact dispatch duplicate was silently shadowed and now panics at the registration line
- application: a teardown failure on Run's normal return exits non-zero. The cli path already fails the process when a close fails — the action folds it into the command's own result — while the http path logged one Emergency line and exited 0, so a supervisor recorded a clean shutdown for a process whose audit flush had just failed. Only the close that DISCOVERED the failure takes the exit; one somebody else performed keeps its own exit code. **Behavioural change**: an http shutdown whose container close failed exited 0 and now exits 1
- application: `HttpTimeoutConfiguration` and `HttpShutdownConfiguration` are removed. Nothing implemented them and nothing could: the configuration the application consults is always the one it built itself, so the override branches were unreachable through any api and the documented knobs did not exist — worse, the timeout branch applied all five values verbatim, so a partial implementation would have silently disabled server timeouts had it ever become reachable. The server limits are the fixed defaults in this major. **Breaking**: code referencing either interface no longer compiles; no behavior changes, because no implementation could ever be injected
- application: `RegisterConfiguration` refuses a name nothing consumes. The registry is read in exactly one place in this major, under exactly one name — the logging configuration — and no accessor exists through which a module could get anything else back, so a misspelling like `"Logging"` stored a configuration the operator believed was active while the logger silently built on defaults. **Behavioural change**: a name other than `logging` was silently stored and now panics at the registration line, naming the one supported name

- logging: `LogError` anchors the record on the error the caller handed over and reads the already-logged mark at the depth `exception.MarkLogged` writes it. The record was anchored on the nearest `*exception.Error` found by a deep search, so the standard http-exception-wrapping-an-exception shape logged the inner error's message at the inner error's level — the wrapper's framing and context, including the `errors` payload of a validation failure, never reached the record, and an inner error built at info level filed the whole record below a production logger's threshold: an http 500 with no log line at all. The mark was read off that same buried error while `MarkLogged` marks the nearest markable link, so marking a wrapping http exception was invisible here and one failure produced two records — and the non-exception path never consulted the mark at all, while the nil-logger fallback consulted it only after printing. A top-level `*exception.Error` contributes its own level, message and enriched context exactly as before; any other error is logged at error level under its full message, with the context of the nearest provider and the cause chain walked from its own wrap link, the way `exception.LogContext` already renders the same shape. **Behavioural change**: a wrapped exception's record changes message, level and context; a marked wrapper now suppresses the record; an already-logged error is no longer re-printed on the nil-logger fallback
- logging: `jsonLogger.Close` recognizes the process console by identity — the `os.Stdout` and `os.Stderr` values themselves — instead of by file name. The name check also matched a file the application opened on the `/dev/stdout` path, the standard containerized idiom for a configured log path, so that descriptor was never closed — one leaked per boot — while the closed flag stayed false and the teardown reported a close that had not happened. A logger built directly over the process streams is left open exactly as before. **Behavioural change**: a caller-opened file named like a console device was silently skipped and is now really closed
- logging: `NewJsonLoggerWithLabels`, `NewLoggingConfiguration` and `LoggingConfiguration.LevelLabels` copy the label map instead of sharing it. The map is read lock-free on every Log call while the registering module — or any caller of the accessor — kept a live reference, and a later write into that reference against those reads is a fatal concurrent map access no recover reaches. **Behavioural change**: a label mutated after construction no longer reaches the built logger
- logging: the json logger weighs a record whose level is not one of the five known ones as an error instead of as debug. The unknown level fell into the lowest priority, so any threshold above debug dropped the record silently — and the zero-value exception a recover handler can legitimately carry reaches the logger with an empty level, so its fatal record vanished from the file under every production configuration. The label keeps the raw level, so the record still says what it was handed. **Behavioural change**: a record with an unrecognized level was silently dropped under an info-or-higher threshold and is now written at error priority
- logging: the json logger's timestamp carries nanosecond precision (`RFC3339Nano`). Whole-second stamps made every record of a busy second indistinguishable, so the ordering the write mutex guarantees on the wire could not be reconstructed from the records; the fraction still parses under the RFC 3339 layouts consumers already use. **Behavioural change**: the rendered time format gains a sub-second fraction
- logging: an error in a log context that also implements `json.Marshaler` is handed to the encoder instead of being flattened to its message, and `validation.ValidationErrors` now marshals as the array it is — each element through its own marshaler, with field, message, code and context. The log record carrying a validation failure said "a; b; c" while the response body said which field failed with which code; the two now say the same thing, and any userland error can opt into the same structural rendering by implementing the marshaler. **Behavioural change**: the `errors` payload of a validation failure renders structurally in the log
- logging: the request logger writes the real request id under its context key unconditionally. A non-empty value already sitting under the key used to win, and that value frequently originates in an error context assembled from request data — so whatever the client wrote could forge the correlation of the record while the true id appeared nowhere. A different non-empty claim is preserved beside the truth under the key suffixed `Claimed`, so nothing the caller said is lost and the operator sees both. **Behavioural change**: the existing value used to win the key and now moves aside for the real id
- application: a panic during `Boot` tears the container down before the exit. `Run` was given the teardown hook when the exit handler learned to write the record first and close second, but `Boot` — a public entry point, and the frame that owns every boot failure — still exited with the container never closed: the log file the logger service had already opened, and every other resource a boot step had built, was abandoned to the process exit. The same hook now runs on both paths; `Close` asks whether the container is already closed before acting, and a boot that died before the kernel was assembled closes nothing instead of dereferencing the kernel it never got. **Behavioural change**: a boot panic now runs the container teardown between the fatal record and the exit

- serializer: `NewSerializerManager` refuses two mime keys that collapse into one normalized key, naming the normalized key and both spellings. `application/json` and `Application/JSON; charset=utf-8` normalize to one map slot, and the overwrite was silent with the winner decided by map iteration order — one boot served every such response through one serializer and the next boot through the other, with the loser dropped without a trace. The constructor already refuses the empty key and the nil instance; the collision was the one construction mistake it swallowed. **Behavioural change**: a map that happened to work by iteration luck now fails the boot
- serializer: a member of the accept header whose `q` parameter falls outside the RFC 7231 qvalue grammar — a zero with up to three decimal digits, or a one with up to three zero decimals — is dropped whole instead of being scored by guesswork. The previous leniency sent the same malformed header three different ways depending on the spelling: an unparseable `q=abc` was scored as full acceptance, a parseable `q=-1` was clamped into a refusal that could manufacture a `406`, and `q=NaN` passed both clamps into a weight every comparison answers false — neither refused nor selectable, so the type the client actually named lost silently to the default. A header whose every member is malformed resolves like a header that matches nothing. **Behavioural change**: spellings the old clamps accepted, such as `q=1e-1` and `q=1.5`, now drop their member
- serializer: a manager deliberately configured without `application/json` answers an empty accept header — and one that matches nothing it has — with its first configured serializer in lexical mime order, instead of refusing every such request while a serializer sits configured beside the refusal. An empty accept header means the client takes anything, and the practical failure was worse than the error: the result handler swallows non-refusal errors by design and fell through to its hardcoded json rendering, so the one representation the deployment had configured out was exactly the one served. The json serializer remains the default whenever it is registered, and the empty manager still errors. **Behavioural change**: a text-only manager now serves text where it errored
- event: a dispatch aborted by a failing listener scans the listeners behind it for one marked required, exactly as a stop of propagation does, and refuses the dispatch when it finds one. The scan used to run only under a stopped propagation, so a listener that failed — ending the dispatch just as decisively — left the required listener silently skipped: one that had also produced a response had that response served with access control never consulted, which is the hole the required mark exists to close. The refusal is a `RequiredListenerSkippedError` built through the new `NewRequiredListenerSkippedErrorWithCause`, carrying the listener's own failure as the cause so the diagnostic that actually broke the dispatch is not lost behind it; the http kernel's existing guard reads the type and drops the kept response for the fail-closed error page, on kernel.request and kernel.controller alike. A dispatch with nothing required behind the failure, and a failing listener marked may-skip, keep reporting the listener's own failure. **Behavioural change**: a failing listener's response used to be served when a required listener sat behind it; it is now refused
- http: the kernel publishes the response `writeResponse` actually wrote — the function returns it, and every call site assigns it back before the terminate event fires. A session-storage outage on the save path replaces the handler's response with an empty `500` inside `writeResponse`, on what used to be a local variable: the client received the `500` while `kernel.terminate` — and the access log listening on it — reported the handler's `200`, a request logged as a success the client saw fail. **Behavioural change**: the terminate event now carries the replaced `500` on a save outage; the replacement itself is unchanged, headers a kernel.response listener set on the original still do not survive onto it and the session cookie stays suppressed deliberately
- application, http: the framework exception listener is registered only when the application installed no error handler by boot, which makes `SetErrorHandler` reachable for the first time in a framework-booted application. The listener answers every kernel.exception dispatch and the kernel consults the handler only when the dispatch produced no response, so an installed handler could never run — while `SetNotFoundHandler` beside it worked, making the dead door look alive; there is no production caller anywhere in the tree, which is the measure of how dead it was. An installed handler now takes over what the listener did — negotiation, the request-id header, the validation errors payload, the warning-versus-error grading of the log record — and when it returns nil the kernel's default rendering answers. **Behavioural change**: an application that installs a handler at boot no longer gets the framework exception listener registered beside it
- http: every default error rendering goes through one door — the exception listener and each of the kernel's seven fallback paths call the same renderer — and the body honours the negotiation the success path honours: a client that negotiated a registered representation gets its error in it, where the error path used to answer hardcoded json below the two-way html test whatever the accept header said, measured against a success body negotiated to xml on the same header. The one asymmetry is deliberate and fail-closed: an accept header that refuses every available media type keeps the error's own status over the default json body, where the success path answers `406` — an error status is the signal itself, and a `401` masked as not-acceptable would hide the refusal from the client that has to react to it. A runtime without a serializer manager keeps the json body it always had, resolved quietly instead of through the soft resolver that logs its failure, so a deployment without a manager no longer pays an error record per rendered error page. **Behavioural change**: with a non-json serializer registered, error bodies negotiate; the kernel's own fallback html page now carries the status and request id lines the listener's page always carried, and its fallback json responses carry the request-id header
- http: `WriteToHttpResponseWriter` gives a header key named by the response to the response: the writer's values for that key are replaced rather than appended to, and keys the response does not name keep what the writer carries. The kernel sets `X-Request-Id` on the raw writer so every response — streamed, refused, failed — carries it, and the exception listener sets the same header on the response it builds; the merge appended, so every error response reached the client with the header twice, measured. **Behavioural change**: a response header now replaces the writer's same-key values at write time instead of being added beside them — `X-Request-Id` arrives once, and a kernel.response listener that sets a header the kernel pre-set on the writer decorates it instead of duplicating it
- session: `Session.Get` hands out a copy at the depth `All` copies at, for the reason `All`'s own documentation names: the live nested value, mutated in place, changed the session without passing through `Set` — `modified` stayed false, `SaveSession` skipped the write and reported success, and the mutation silently never persisted, measured with the stored role unchanged behind a save that returned nil. Read, mutate the copy, `Set` it back — the pattern that was always correct is unaffected, and scalars cost nothing. **Behavioural change**: a caller that mutated the value returned by `Get` and relied on the live session object changing underneath — within the same request, since the mutation never reached the storage — now works on its own copy
- http: `static.NewFileServer` copies the configuration at construction, struct and both lists, so the server is immutable once built. It used to retain the caller's pointer and write its defaults into it — the caller's own struct came back with `index.html` and `3600` written over its zero values, measured — and `SetExcludedPathList`/`SetAllowedDotPrefixList` raced the in-flight requests reading those fields with no lock, a data race the detector reports on the first concurrent pair; the excluded-path list is a security control by its own documentation. The framework's wiring configures before construction and is unaffected. **Behavioural change**: a setter called after construction configures the next server instead of mutating one already serving, and the constructor no longer writes into the caller's struct
- application: the kernel's default listeners — the profiler under debug mode, the response normalizer, the terminate access log, and the exception listener when no error handler was installed — register at the end of `Boot`, in every process shape, instead of inside the http run. They are inert in a console that never dispatches a kernel event, and the console's dispatcher now answers introspection with the same set the serving process runs: `debug:events` used to render an empty dispatcher for a correctly wired application, measured, so the one command that answers "what listens?" answered nothing exactly where the operator asks it. The conditions are boot-final by contract — an error handler installed by boot takes the listener's place, exactly as before — and the security pair stays http-only: it is the enforcement, and a console process has no request to guard. **Behavioural change**: the four listeners exist from `Boot` on, so a kernel event dispatched between boot and the http run — or from a console process — now reaches them
- debug: `debug:container` describes by default and builds only when asked. The bare listing runs no provider — names, lifetimes, built state and declared types, the container-lifetime services and the scoped ones in their own blocks — where it used to `Get` every windowed name with the default limit of `0` meaning unlimited: every database pool, redis client and http client of the application opened by a command describing itself as "List container services", in a console process. `--build` is the explicit sweep with exactly the old diagnostic value — every service resolved, the failures with their cause chains and redacted contexts — and `debug:container <name>` still builds, because a name asked for by argument is the explicit diagnostic probe. A scoped name resolves through the run's own scope and reports its lifetime, where it used to answer `debug.notFound` — the exact failure the diagnosis comment promises to prevent — and the notFound/buildFailed diagnosis reads both lifetimes before it calls a registration missing. A container without the descriptions door is listed by name with a warning naming the limitation. **Behavioural change**: the bare listing no longer resolves services and its table shape changed; the sweep is behind `--build`
- debug: `debug:middleware` describes the pipeline by default and builds only under `--build`. The listing runs the selection and ordering alone — index, definition name, priority, the function captured at registration, and the inactive entries with their reasons — where it used to build the real chain in a console process: every factory ran, a factory that captured a client dialed it, the serving path's `lastBuildReport` was overwritten by a read-only listing, and a factory panic killed the command with no table at all. Under `--build` the chain is built with a recover, so a failing or panicking factory renders as a `debug.buildFailed` envelope with its cause instead of a dead process. **Breaking**: `debug.NewMiddlewareCommand` takes the description provider and the build provider — `MiddlewareDescriptionProvider` and `MiddlewareBuildProvider` — instead of the single chain provider
- debug: the verbose listener block of `debug:events` prints the DISPATCH order — the dispatcher's own slice, held sorted at insertion — with an `order` column carrying the rank, in the table and in the verbose json alike. The block used to re-sort with `Source` before the id — a key the dispatcher never consults — and compared the id as text, so among eleven same-priority listeners the tenth printed second, measured, and the two halves of one output contradicted each other: the priorities column above walks the real order. Stability across boots is the boot's own: registration order is deterministic since the module phases run ordered. **Behavioural change**: rows within a priority now print in registration order, not in the source/owner/id collation
- debug: the `application` row of `debug:version` reads the process-wide declaration made through `cli/output.SetApplicationVersion` — the door that existed with zero callers — and the framework wiring no longer fills it with melody's own version: both rows printed `v2.13.0`, measured, so the one command that answers "what is deployed here" answered the framework version twice. An application declares its version with one call in its composition root, from wherever it keeps it — its own ldflags variable, its environment — and an application that never declared one reads `<unknown>` instead of a lie. **Behavioural change**: without a declaration the row prints `<unknown>` where it printed melody's version
- debug: `debug:router` renders the two discriminators the dispatch actually uses — `priority` and `order`, the registration rank that breaks a priority tie — on every row, in the table and the json document; `--verbose` adds the requirements, defaults and attributes as compact cells, and the json items carry the three maps always. Two routes differing only in priority rendered as identical rows, measured, so the command could not answer the question it exists for — which route wins — while the introspection's own `MarshalJSON` had been serializing all ten fields all along
- debug: the trace/stack noise filter of the rendered error context is a display concern and full verbosity turns it off: `debug:container ... -vvv` shows the context whole, stack keys included, where the drop used to be unconditional at every verbosity. Below full verbosity the drop stands — the frames would flood the table — and the filter's contract is now written on it: it strips diagnostic noise, not credentials; a producer that parks a credential in an error context owns that choice, exactly as every producer in this tree already refuses to
- config: a late `MarkSecret` covers the whole derivation chain. The retroactive scan walks to a fixpoint — a reader of a freshly marked name is scanned in turn — where it used to stop after the direct readers, so a mark arriving after the boot resolve redacted the key and its direct reader while the two-hop derivation printed the assembled credential in full, measured; a mark declared before the resolve already covered the chain by riding the resolution's own recursion, and the two paths now answer alike. Over-marking stays the direction of error: the scan can only redact more, never less

- example: the catalogue reading is served at `/catalog/report/` under the name `example.catalog.report`. The `/integration` prefix it sat under was named for a group of routes that no longer exists — the three that only demonstrated a backend went when the integrations were wired into the nomenclature itself — so a prefix was left standing over a single route that has nothing to do with it. `ReportDemoHandler` is `CatalogReportHandler`, named for what it renders
- example: the welcome text on the static index says what the application is — a product nomenclature of products, categories, currencies and users — instead of calling itself a small demo, and the api token shipped in `.env` is named for the example rather than for a demonstration. The word had outlived what it described: the application it names has been a working nomenclature since the integrations were wired to it

- logging: `LogOnRecover` only logs. It terminated the process when the recovered value carried an exit code, skipping every remaining deferred call, which is the one place in the framework where a library helper could end a program that had not asked to end. `LogOnRecoverAndExit` is the honestly named helper for that, and the three call sites that want it use it — one through it, two through its `LogOnRecoverAndExitAfter` sibling, which runs a final step between the record and the exit because a teardown deferred below never runs
- config: `Resolve()` reports an error once the application is serving. Resolution rewrites the parameter store, and by then every service holds values copied out of it, so a later pass cannot reconfigure anything that is running — it only rewrites the store under readers that expect it settled. Building a configuration by hand and resolving it before serving is unaffected
- config: a positive session ttl below one second fails the boot. Such a value does not describe a short session but a broken one: the file-backed store purges a lapsed entry on the same write that stores it, so a lifetime near the cost of that write returns success having persisted nothing, and one second is the finest unit an HTTP date can express in any case. Zero keeps its meaning of no expiry
- httpclient: the client sets `MaxIdleConnsPerHost` on its transport, exposed as `TransportConfig.MaxIdleConnsPerHost` and defaulting to `MaxIdleConns` (100), following an override of it unless pinned explicitly. It was never set, so `net/http` fell back to `DefaultMaxIdleConnsPerHost` (2) and the configured `MaxIdleConns: 100` was inert: since a `BaseUrl` means one client per upstream, a burst of 200 concurrent requests dialled 200 sockets, retained 2 and closed 198, each holding a `TIME_WAIT` entry for the 60-second MSL until the ephemeral port range was exhausted and every request failed with `connect: cannot assign requested address`, reported as `"request failed"` and indistinguishable from the upstream being down. **Behavioural change**: connection reuse against a single host now scales with `MaxIdleConns`; a caller who relied on the old two-connection ceiling must set `MaxIdleConnsPerHost` explicitly

- validation: a nil pointer embed is validated as "nothing was supplied" rather than "nothing to validate", so the constraints on its promoted fields run against their zero values exactly as a value embed's already did. A payload that mentioned no field of a `*T` embed previously skipped every constraint the embed promotes — naming any sibling field made `encoding/json` allocate the embed and re-arm them — so a body of `{"status":"open"}` on a request whose `*Audit` embed declares `ActorId` as `notBlank` passed validation and then dereferenced nil in the handler. **Behavioural change**: requests that relied on omitting the embed entirely are now rejected with the constraint errors the tags declare. A promoted field shadowed by an outer field of the same json name stays unvalidated, so the dominance rules are unchanged
- validation: exceeding the nesting-depth cap is reported as a validation error (`nestingDepthExceeded`) only when the truncated subtree could actually carry a `validate` tag, so nesting past the cap can no longer bypass validation while free-form tag-free client json is accepted up to the cap as before. An interface-typed member counts as possibly tag-bearing, its dynamic type being unknowable from the type alone: the walk descends into one and enforces the tags it finds, so clearing it statically let a payload nest past the cap and bypass them. **Behavioural change**: tag-free json reached through an interface — a `map[string]any` metadata field among it — is reported as `nestingDepthExceeded` past the cap rather than accepted at any depth. Past the cap the walk previously returned an empty error list, which `Validate` converted to `nil`, so nesting a payload one level deeper than the cap bypassed every constraint in it. The reachability check follows pointers, slices, arrays and map elements and is memoized per type; the cap value is unchanged. A member the payload sent as null or empty — a nil pointer or interface, a slice or map holding no element — is no longer reported at the cut: the walk returns no errors for it at any depth, so the error named a member that was never walked

- http: a route pattern whose optional parameter is not its final segment is refused at registration instead of being accepted and half-served. An omitted optional is dropped wherever it sits in the pattern, while a match only ever ends early at the tail, so `/blog/:locale?/posts` let `UrlGenerator.GeneratePath` mint `/blog/posts` — a path the router matched with nothing and answered `404`; the canonical locale-prefix shape `/:locale?/blog` was broken the same way whenever the parameter carried no default, while a trailing optional worked correctly all along. A mid-pattern optional that carries a default is still accepted, because the default is always substituted and the segment is therefore never dropped. **Behavioural change**: an application registering a mid-pattern optional with no default now panics at the definition site with `optional route parameter must be the last pattern segment unless it has a default` instead of booting and emitting dead links — give the parameter a default, move it to the end, or register the long and short patterns as two routes

- cli: in json mode a command's standard output is the rendered document and nothing else — the ansi start/finish banner that `cli.Register` wraps around every registered command is suppressed, and `--format=json` implies `--no-color`. Nothing is lost: `meta` already reports the command, its arguments, the start time and the duration, and `error` reports the final status. **Behavioural change**: `jq` and `json.Unmarshal` now consume `debug:*` output directly instead of failing on the first byte; a consumer that scraped the banner off stdout must read `meta` instead
- cli: a command whose envelope reports an error exits non-zero. `output.Render` returns an exit-coded error after writing the envelope, so `debug:container <name>` fails when the service cannot be resolved instead of printing `[success]` and exiting `0` — a deployment gate such as `app debug:container app.repository.order || exit 1` now catches a broken container. A registered service that errors or panics while being constructed is reported as `debug.buildFailed` rather than `debug.notFound`, so the operator is not sent after a registration that is in fact present. **Behavioural change**: a command that reported an error in its payload while exiting `0` now exits `1`
- cli: `--format` and `--order` reject an unrecognised value instead of silently substituting the default. `--format=JSON`, `--format=yaml` and `--order=ascending` now fail during flag parsing with a message naming the accepted values and a non-zero exit, matching how `--limit=abc` already behaved, instead of quietly printing the human table. Omitting either flag still defaults to `table` and `asc`. **Behavioural change**: a script passing an unsupported value now fails instead of receiving the table

- http: the response path saves and advertises the session published on the request under `RequestAttributeSession` at the moment the response is written, rather than the one the kernel captured before routing. **Behavioural change**: replacing that attribute in a handler now takes effect

- cli: `--order=desc` reverses the listing in `debug:router`, `debug:events`, `debug:parameters`, `debug:middleware` and `debug:container`, applied before `--limit`/`--offset` so a descending window returns the end of the list rather than the beginning; `--order=asc` remains the default. **Behavioural change**: the flag was accepted and ignored before, so an invocation that passed it now gets different output
- cli: the `--fields` and `--sort` flags are withdrawn. No printer ever read them and no command ever sorted on a supplied key, so they are removed from the flag set, from `output.Option` and from the `meta.flags` block of the json envelope rather than left advertising a projection the document never received; `output.SplitFields` goes with them. **Behavioural change**: passing either flag now fails as an unknown flag instead of being silently ignored. **Breaking**: `output.Option` no longer has the `Fields` and `SortKey` fields, so a command that constructs that struct as a literal no longer compiles, and `output.SplitFields` is gone

- rate limit: the default key extractor now keys on the client IP alone instead of `ip:path`. `SimpleRateLimit(n)` is therefore a true per-IP budget of `n` requests per minute rather than `n` per IP per path — a client that varied the path could previously never trip the limit. **Behavioural change**: an application relying on the old per-path budgets must set a path-aware `KeyExtractor` explicitly. Both limiters also bound how many distinct keys they track (`SetMaxKeys`, default 1,000,000): once the map is full and an idle-entry prune frees nothing, a request under an unseen key is denied rather than growing the map without bound. The reclaim walk runs at most once per window, so a full limiter cannot be made to pay a whole-map scan for every request under an unseen key
- security: an access control rule whose attribute list normalizes to empty is refused at construction instead of being accepted. Such a rule still matched its path, so it granted every authenticated principal and shadowed any longer-prefixed rule that would have denied — a silent privilege downgrade from a variadic call with no attribute, or from a role that a configuration value resolved to empty. Blank attributes inside a non-empty list are still trimmed
- http: `TokenBucketLimiter` is now `FixedWindowLimiter`, with the old type name and both constructors kept as deprecated aliases so existing code is unaffected. The limiter restores the whole allowance at the window edge rather than proportionally to elapsed time, so it is a fixed-window counter and an instant straddling that edge admits up to twice the rate; the name now says so. `SlidingWindowLimiter`, which `IpRateLimit` and `UserRateLimit` already use, holds the rate over every trailing window
- http: the request attributes the kernel owns are reserved under the framework's own underscore prefix (`RequestAttributeSession` is `_session`, `RequestAttributeScheme` is `_scheme`) and are published after the route attributes, so a route attribute can no longer replace the session object or the resolved scheme. Every other framework attribute already carried that prefix. **Behavioural change**: code reading these attributes through the exported constants is unaffected; code that hardcoded the literal `"session"` must move to `RequestAttributeSession`

- container: a scope closes what it built in dependency order, dependents before their dependencies, falling back to creation order, latest first, for anything the graph says nothing about — the same tie-break the container's own teardown applies, since the two share one walk — and reporting a cycle the way that teardown reports one. The previous order was the node name alone, which was defensible while the only scope-built values were incidental and is a coin flip now that a scoped repository holding a scoped transaction is the ordinary shape

### Removed

- cli: the dead unexported error presenter is gone — an error-printing path with sorted context and a bounded cause chain that nothing has called since the envelope renderer became the one presentation, drifting beside the live path it duplicated

### Fixed

- tooling: `.dev/validate/documentation.sh` reads the integration readmes, which nothing had ever compared. The check excluded `integrations/` from both of its halves and the citation band only ever asks the reverse question, so what a binding ships and its readme never names was unmeasured on every module of every major. A binding is now discovered from its module file and carries three names — the suite it ships with, the family that pairs it with the same module of another major, and the major itself — and what satisfies a demand is the family's own documents, because `Provider` is declared by the bunorm core and by both drivers and they are three different symbols. Reading it needed its own rule: these documents write a door as prose, not as a list, so the entry is the LINKED symbol wherever it stands, and a method is read under its own name rather than under its type — `Provider.OpenContext` had reduced to `Provider`, which made every method of every binding invisible. The two shapes were found the way the last five were, by symbols that cannot possibly be missing reading as missing; the framework half is untouched, measured entry for entry before the first repair.
- documentation: the integration readmes list the doors their modules ship. The rueidis readme named `ClientConfig` and `TimeoutConfig` as the optional configuration and no way to pass either, so `WithClientConfig`, `WithTimeoutConfig` and `NewProviderWithConfig` are named, together with what a partial literal costs — a configuration is taken whole or not at all, so a field left at its zero value turns `PingOnStart` off and switches client-side caching on, where the drivers fill in field by field; the two command-timeout constructors of the cache backend, announced in this changelog and documented nowhere, have their entries; the provider type and the rate limiter type are linked where the opening list only spelled them; the mysql and pgsql readmes gain the context and migration opens they always implemented, the chainable configuration methods, the exported `ProviderOption` a caller can write their own against, and the two typed-configuration constructors that were named only inside a sentence about zeros; the bunorm readme states what its registry constructor takes and what it fails with; and the migration readme names the door that answers the default option set.
- documentation: four counts in this block are corrected against the code they describe, and nine entries the first major records and this one omitted are written down — seven documentation corrections its package documents carry too, and the two validation-lane entries that govern every major. The kernel renders through seven fallback paths, not six; three call sites want the exiting recover helper, not two, and two of them come through the `After` sibling that runs a final step between the record and the exit; four sites ask whether a format is json, not six; and the session package does not carry "the same warning" as the cache serializer — its file storage warns that a JSON round trip changes the SHAPES a session comes back with, which is type drift within one schema, where the serializer's hazard is a schema that moved under a document nothing stamps a version on.

- documentation: the logging document states the file journal's rotation constraint — the default destination is a file, its descriptor is opened exactly once for the life of the process, no reopen door or rotation signal exists, so rename-based rotation moves the journal out from under the process silently (records landing in the rotated file or an unlinked inode) and `copytruncate` is the rotation mode that works with the `O_APPEND` descriptor
- documentation: the serializer document states what an untyped deserialization target receives — every JSON number as `float64`, an integer beyond 2^53 silently altered, arrays as `[]any` — where typed struct targets decode exactly; the config and bag documents state that the string grammar `Float`/`Float64` reads is Go's full `ParseFloat` (underscore spellings, hexadecimal floats, exponents), wider than the strict base-10 grammar `Int` reads, replacing the config document's claim that the three accessors read one grammar; the bag document states that `Get` hands back the stored value live — a `[]string` mutated through it writes into the bag behind its lock — with `All`/`StringSlice` as the copying doors; and the validation document states that an integer bound above 2^53 is compared in `float64` against a float-typed field, so a value within one ULP of such a bound can be misjudged against the declared number
- documentation: two security-relevant surfaces gain the caveat their behaviour always had. `NewAccessControlRegexRule` states that its pattern is compiled UNANCHORED — a substring match against the canonicalized path, so `"/public"` also matches `/admin/public-notes` — which is the opposite of a route requirement melody anchors, and names the `"^/public(/|$)"` form that bounds a rule to one section; the GoDoc, the `SECURITY.md` match-priority list carry it. `FileResponse` and `AttachmentResponse` state that they open the given path with no containment check, so a path built from client input must be confined to a known directory first, or they are a latent arbitrary-file read; the GoDoc and `HTTP.md` carry it
- documentation: three claims of the http document catch up with the code. The route-collision paragraph describes both regimes — during application boot every duplicate is recorded, the first registration wins, a colliding name leaves the route itself dispatchable, and boot fails afterward with the one aggregated `duplicate registrations detected at boot`, while the immediate registration-site panics the paragraph used to present as the only behaviour fire outside that window; the compression bullet stops claiming response bodies never fully land in memory, since the middleware buffers up to `MinSize` bytes to decide and serves a sub-threshold body whole from that buffer, with `MinSize` carrying no upper bound; and the request-body-limit section attributes the `413` to melody's own two reading paths — the automatic form parsing and `BindJson` — where it belonged to net/http before, which only closes the connection, so a controller reading the raw body itself was documented as covered and is not
- documentation: the config document states the bool grammar the typed accessor reads — the string spellings, the whitespace trim, and the refusal of the empty string — which until now was written only in the cron integration's readme; and the container quick-start stops passing `WithTypeRegistration(true)`, a restatement of the default that taught the option must be passed by an example that never resolves by type
- documentation: the application document's logging usage example compiles — `LevelLabels` maps to `LevelLabel` values built through `LevelLabelFromInt`, and the example handed it bare string literals the type cannot hold
- tooling: a validation lane whose script is missing or not executable fails the run instead of reporting success. `.dev/validate/all.sh` skipped the e2e harness, the documentation check and both live e2e scripts on a plain file test that returned zero, so `chmod -x .dev/e2e/run.sh` made `--e2e` print `validation completed` and exit zero having driven nothing — and the continuous integration job invokes the documentation check directly, so the same tree failed there while passing locally. `stack.sh` is tested for the same reason `run.sh` is; only one of the two was
- tooling: the race lane covers `session`, `http` and `internal` in every major, in `.dev/validate/all.sh` and in the continuous integration job alike. Those packages hold the state a request actually touches — the storages concurrent requests read and write, the middleware chain, the deep copy that keeps one request's session data out of another's — and their suites already contained tests written specifically to catch a race, with a goroutine and a wait group, which no lane ran under the detector: removing the read lock from `InMemoryStorage.Load` left every other lane green. They add some four seconds per major

- documentation: seventy-seven exported doors this major has always carried are listed at last, and the enumerations that pretend to enumerate stop leaving them out. The gravest is `security.RolesReplacer`, named in no document while its own comment says why it matters: a foreign voter that asserts its own concrete token meets melody's rebuild after the hierarchy expansion, abstains, and — abstaining under the affirmative strategy beside a role voter that grants — hands out the access it exists to withhold. Beside it, the access control merge strategies were given as the three string literals and never as the exported constants a caller is meant to pass, on a setter that panics on anything else; the session-fixation section taught the manual two-step rotation without naming `http.RegenerateRequestSession`, which does both and is what the session document already recommends over the manager; and `exception.Error` — what every constructor of that package returns — had no entry of its own anywhere.
