The rest are doors of the same kind: the deferred-resolution handle and the container's four registration sentinels, the process-identity family and the signal context every `Run` example is handed out of thin air, the config resolver a provider actually comes through against the container form that was listed in its place, the only setter of the cache wait the footguns discuss, the session cookie constant every passage calls "the browser cookie", the interface a userland constraint has to implement to get the behaviour the validation document describes three times, the cause-chain builders behind the `causeChain` keys the logging document reads, the http contracts declared beside two siblings that were listed, both cookie helpers, the file server path every request actually takes, and the four deprecated cors doors that were half listed
- tooling: `.dev/validate/documentation.sh` reads the entries its own check is built on. It saw 376 of the 770 a published major writes, because it anchored on the head of a bullet in the first column: the label-grouped entry (`- Tokens: [...]`), the door named mid-sentence, every bullet at any indent — 321 of them, the whole `security/config` surface among them — the entry that carries its siblings unlinked, and the entry written with its declaration keyword (`` `type Error` ``), were each read as nothing at all. Both halves widen together, since the same inventory decides what is demanded and what satisfies the demand, and the fix is a strict superset: no symbol the narrower reader enrolled is lost. Each link is also read with its own target rather than with the first one on the line, so a bullet naming three doors in three packages no longer looks all three up inside the first. What the widening then reported is recorded in `.dev/validate/documentation.baseline`, read as a set of claims the way the parity and citation baselines are — an unrecorded gap fails the run and a line that matched nothing fails it just as loudly. A `--undocumented` pass answers the question neither this check nor the citation band can put — which exported door no document of any major names — and reports rather than gates, because around nine hundred of some 1270 exported symbols are enrolled nowhere and most of that is correct: the documents list types, constructors and the doors an integrator comes through, not every method of every type. Each row carries the evidence available without judgement — the document that covers the package, and whether that document already writes the symbol as a code token while listing it nowhere
- documentation: four passages that resolved into another major's tree are re-pointed at this one. The rueidis readme linked the cache backend contract of v1 and its registration example imported six core packages of v1 into a module whose `go.mod` requires this major and not that one; `UPGRADE.md` claimed the example's mysql opt-out mirrors a `PGSQL_INSECURE` switch, whose only occurrence in this major was the sentence itself; and `DOCUMENTATION.md` called `../README.md` the repository root, which from this major's `.documentation/` is this module's own readme. The maintenance banner of `README.md` stops saying the major receives security and correctness fixes only — every release since v2.8.0 carried a new capability — and states the actual direction: driven to complete stability, then frozen

- documentation: the package documents, the upgrade guide and the roadmap are corrected where they described something the code does not do. `CLI.md` names `Run` as the method every command receives, not `Execute`, which no major declares. `APPLICATION.md` and `UPGRADE.md` stop prescribing a shutdown hook this major has no door for, and name the mechanism that exists — deriving an upgraded connection's lifetime from the context handed to `Run`, whose cancellation is what begins the shutdown. `SECURITY.md` describes an unrepublished session rotation as the fail-safe it is (the previous entry is gone, the rotated-away object is latched cleared, the response path expires the cookie) rather than as a silent revert. `HTTP.md` counts three `413` paths instead of two, states that an already-answered preflight from an allowed origin is decorated with the cross-origin headers rather than left untouched, marks the three deprecated cors doors it listed unmarked, records the second exclusion-list consultation the mount-root branch makes, and stops claiming `debug:middleware` renders `LastBuildReport`. `CACHE.md` stops promising that the next major fixes the `float64` round trip — no default serializer in the tree does — and spells the request context as `request.HttpRequest().Context()`, the melody `Request` having no `Context()`. `CONTAINER.md` describes what relaxed type registration actually relaxes (the registration, after which by-type resolution refuses every candidate), drops a duplicated `ScopeManager` row, and describes the example it introduces. `SERIALIZER.md` separates the success path, which answers `406`, from the error path, which falls back to a json body a refusing client still receives. `VALIDATION.md` records the second tag grammar the parser accepts and that `greaterThan`/`lessThan` reject a nil pointer where the string constraints tolerate one. `LOGGING.md` states that the exit helper writes two records, the second an emergency-level certificate no threshold can drop, and that the cause walk does not open an `errors.Join`. `CLI.md` records that `--no-color` omits the full-width banner rules rather than plain-texting them, and that `MergeFlags` also panics on a nil flag. `DEBUG.md` records that the `debug:*` family is registered on the development environment alone, so the documented deployment gate fires for a missing command rather than a failing service, and that the zero-value `EventCommand` leaves the deferred-listener block out. `CONFIG.md` records that a deferred parameter panics on every accessor until boot. `BAG.md` separates the helpers that return a parse error from `String`/`StringSlice`, which have no error return at all. `HTTPCLIENT.md` states that a positive `WithTimeout` is honoured on the streaming path. `CLOCK.md` stops saying a test waiting for a tick after advancing the frozen clock waits forever — it waits the real interval. `UPGRADE.md` stops announcing that `DecideAny` now refuses an empty attribute list where it granted one; it refused before the change too, and what is new is the refusal reason. `ROADMAP.md` stops promising incremental framework capabilities on this line while its own Direction section says the line is being frozen. `DOCUMENTATION.md` names the module root as such rather than the repository root, and records where the command-surface documents actually place their `Exported API` listing
- documentation: five source comments are corrected against the code they sit on. `cache.Remember` stops promising the next major fixes the `float64` round trip; the cors request listener describes the already-answered branch it decorates instead of claiming everything else falls through untouched; the boot recover handler stops saying `Close` asks `IsClosed` before acting, when the close is unconditional and the probe only decides whether the failure is reported; the `Clock` contract stops saying a tick expected after an advance never arrives; and the time-codec memo stops claiming every sibling memo in `validation` settles with `LoadOrStore`, when `nestedTagBearingTypeCache` deliberately does not
- documentation: the entries of this block that described code this major does not carry are corrected against it — the access-control refusal names `NewAccessControlRawPrefixRule`, the constructor that panics on `PUBLIC_ACCESS`, rather than `NewAccessControlRule`, which allows it; the late secret marking is described as the fixpoint walk the code performs rather than as a direct-reader pass with the closure deferred to another major; the scope close falls back to creation order rather than to a descending node key; the cli action is described as not closing the container at all; the event adapter entry describes the ordering index being removed rather than reordered; the runtime registration entry describes the publication preceding the resolution, which is the order the code takes and the order its own comment explains; the session-persistence records are described as carrying a one-way reference rather than the session id; the httpclient entry stops claiming the unsupported-body error names the call it came from, which the function raising it cannot know; and the time-codec entry drops the false parenthesis about every sibling memo

- documentation: the upgrade guide of this major catches up with the one the first major carries, and the two are now the same document. Thirteen sections that describe the `cron`, `rueidis` and `bunorm migrate` bindings never reached it — they were written while those bindings were still being ported, and the guide was left behind each time — and a fourteenth was carried under a narrower title (`Cache: a recovered panic carries its cause`) because only the cache half of the change existed here when it was written; it is now `Cache and cron: …` and names the cron runner and the bunorm manager registry, both of which capture the panic stack on this major too. The section order is aligned with the other major's as well: the guide has one `## Unreleased` block and no release structure at all, so nothing gave the two orders different meanings, while the divergence made every future section land in a place that had to be argued for. Measured after: the two files carry the same 147 sections with byte-identical bodies, and differ only by the `websocket/v3` note, which stays on one major alone because that integration ships a v3 binding and no other — its go.mod requires `melody/v3`, so no reader of either of these majors can import the module the note is about. `Register` also stops pointing at `container/container.go`, and the `CACHE.md` usage sample stops failing to compile, on both majors alike

- documentation: three documents of this major catch up with the ones the first major already carried. `ROADMAP.md` no longer lists as near-term and mid-term work three capabilities this major has shipped all along — the static file server's filesystem and embedded modes, the `security` package's firewall system, and the router's named routes, url generation, grouping and constraints — and states instead what is actually true of it: this major line is being driven to complete stability and then frozen, with new capabilities landing in the newest line. `DOCUMENTATION.md` gains the archetype-free documents (`CHANGELOG.md`, `SECURITY.md`, `CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md`, the GitHub templates and the per-integration `README.md` files), the naming rule for the GitHub issue templates and for where a package document may live, the note that the command-surface documents title their end-of-document listing "Exported API", and the caveat that the upgrade guide covers releases made since it was introduced. `UPGRADE.md` drops the compile-level note announcing that `session/contract.Session` gained `SetShared`: no major declares that method — the change was reverted before release — so the note described a breaking change an upgrader could neither see nor act on, and the neighbouring documentation check could not report it, since that check asks whether a symbol a major ships is documented and never whether a documented symbol exists. `.dev/validate/parity.sh` compares one major against the next after rewriting the import path, over the files, their text, the exported symbols, the test names and the file each test lives in, the section titles of the per-major documents and the dependency set of each `go.mod`, with the divergences that are there on purpose recorded in `.dev/validate/parity.baseline` — an entry that stops matching fails the check as loudly as an unrecorded difference, so the list cannot rot into a suppression list
- session: the file storage's atomic save fsyncs the directory after the rename, the way the cron generator's atomic writer always has — without it a power loss after a save could resurface the previous snapshot, silently logging out the user whose session had just been written; and the construction sweeps the `<name>.*.tmp` orphans a hard kill leaves between `CreateTemp` and the rename, each of which is a complete snapshot of every live session and its tokens that nothing ever opened again. The sweep touches only this storage's own temp spelling, at the one moment the per-process ownership the session documentation states guarantees nothing else is mid-rename over the path
- example: the embedded-env build embeds the committed `.env` alone. The `.env*` glob also baked the gitignored `.env.local` — the machine-local override file that holds real credentials precisely because it never enters git — into the shipped binary, where the loader's precedence then let it override the committed configuration at runtime. A file the repository does not carry must not ship in the artifact

- http: a multipart upload past `MaxRequestBodyBytes` is answered `413`, the status its urlencoded and json siblings already carry. Multipart is the one body the kernel does not pre-read — a handler drives it through `ParseMultipartForm` — so its `*MaxBytesError` surfaced as a handler error, and lacking a status was rendered `500` at error level while the same excess on a urlencoded or json body was a `413` warning. The kernel now maps a handler's `*MaxBytesError` onto `413` before it is logged or rendered, so the three body paths answer alike
- http, session: a live session id no longer reaches the log verbatim. The response path filed the full id — a bearer credential — into the record on a failed delete (the session survives the failure) and a failed save (the id is one the client presented), and the manager named it in its own save-refusal contexts; anyone with log-read access, a wider set than session-store access, could copy it into a cookie and resume the session. Every site now logs a truncated one-way `sessionRef` (a SHA-256 prefix) that correlates a session's records within a request without carrying the id
- http/cors: cross-origin headers reach a streamed response and a rate-limited preflight. The `Middleware` applied the allow-origin headers to the response the write path discards for a committed stream (Server-Sent Events commit headers before returning), so a cross-origin `EventSource` lost them; it now applies them to the writer before the handler runs, where a streaming handler's own commit carries them, and still decorates an ordinary handler's response once as before. The preflight request listener, which the rate limiter at a higher priority can answer with a `429` ahead of, now decorates that refusal with the preflight headers instead of returning early, so a browser reads it as a rate limit rather than an opaque cors failure
- http: a response carrying the session cookie is kept out of a shared cache. The write path added `Set-Cookie` to whatever response it wrote without reconciling its cacheability, so a response some layer marked `Cache-Control: public` — a static asset among them — that a listener then modified the session on would be stored by a shared cache under its url and replayed, with one client's session id, to the next. Emitting the session cookie now drops any `public` token and adds `private`, so a shared cache does not store it while a private browser cache still may

- container: a scoped parent writes no edge into the container's dependency graph. The parent filter matched the child alone, so a scoped service resolving a container-owned one — a logger, the configuration — recorded an edge keyed by the scoped node into a graph that is never pruned and whose teardown walks only container-created representatives, skipping every scope-keyed dependent: written per resolution, read by nothing, reclaimed never. With boot-planned scoped names the writes were idempotent and bounded; a live-scope registration under a request-derived name (`scope.RegisterScoped` with a tenant or job id in the name) left one permanent entry per distinct name for the life of the process — memory climbing with request count, pointing at the dependency graph in a heap profile
- application: the embedded-static boot guard reads through the interface, so a typed-nil `fs.FS` handed to `NewApplication` under `melody_static_embedded` is refused by the panic that names the argument instead of passing the plain nil comparison and dying later as an anonymous nil dereference inside `fs.Stat` — the exact hazard the environment sibling's guard already documented and closed
- application: two closes racing each other report the one teardown failure once. The closedness probe and the container `Close` were not atomic, so two concurrent `Application.Close` calls could both read the container open, the loser then receiving the winner's memoized error, and both presenting the single failure as their own incident — the emergency record written twice for one failure. A close now claims the performer role atomically; the probe keeps deciding against a container somebody else closed directly, which the claim cannot see
- cache: `NewDefaultRememberOption`'s GoDoc states the pinned-leader hazard the cache document already carried — under exactly the defaults (non-cancelable, unbounded wait) a callback that never returns pins its key's single-flight entry and every later caller for the life of the process, with `WithWaitTimeout`, `WithCancelable` and `WithContext` as the opt-out doors
- http: the Accept-family header parsers cut a value into at most a fixed number of members, so an unauthenticated header cannot convert its byte budget straight into live heap. `internal.SplitOutsideQuotes` — the one member and parameter splitter every negotiating reader shares (`Accept`, `Accept-Encoding`, the serializer's content negotiation) — produced one slice element per separator, so a megabyte of commas (well under the default `Server.MaxHeaderBytes`) became a million-element slice plus a four-element array per member, tens of megabytes of churn for one request, on the response-compression and error-negotiation paths that reach a client with no credentials. The split now stops at 64 members with the remainder left as the final member, a bound far above any real header and one every reader drops as an unparsable range
- http/static: the entity tag reads the modification time at nanosecond resolution rather than whole seconds. Two rewrites of a file within the same second that kept the same length produced an identical `ETag` and an identical second-resolution `Last-Modified`, so a deploy that swapped a bundle for one of the same size revalidated `304` and stayed served stale until the file's length or its second changed. `If-None-Match` carries the finer tag and is evaluated ahead of `If-Modified-Since`, so the change the second-resolution `Last-Modified` cannot see is caught. A filesystem whose `FileInfo` reports only whole seconds is unaffected, and the zero-modification-time form still carries the build version
- http, security: the kernel refuses a request path that folds to a different spelling, before it is routed or authorized. The router matches the path as sent — a trailing slash is dropped, but `..`, `.` and an empty `//` segment are not folded — while the access-control matcher folds the path through `path.Clean` before it selects a rule, and the firewall matcher reads the raw path with neither fold. A request routed to a protected handler under one spelling was therefore authorized against the folded spelling's rule: with a catch-all route under a protected prefix (a single-page-application, proxy or documentation mount) and a more permissive rule reachable by folding, `GET /admin/x/../../login` reached the `/admin` handler while `/login`'s `PUBLIC_ACCESS` rule granted it with the token never consulted, and `GET /admin/..` reached the handler while the folded `/` matched no rule and the listener let a no-match through. The kernel now answers such a path `400` — `http.requestPathIsCanonical` states the boundary, and the access-control fold stays as the matcher's own defence for a caller that consults `AccessControl` without the kernel. A trailing slash is not a fold and still routes; the static file server already refused a non-canonical path for the same reason. **Behavioural change**: a client that sends a path carrying `..`, `.` or `//` is answered `400` where the request was previously routed
- session: the tombstone's boundary is stated where the guarantee is promised — a session another request deleted cannot be saved again *within one process*: the record lives in the manager's own memory, not in the storage it guards, so a shared storage does not carry it between instances and a logout served by one node does not stop a peer instance's in-flight request from writing the entry back. The package document declared the property unconditionally, two bullets above the recommendation to deploy a shared storage, and the `Storage` contract's GoDoc now names the refusal as a per-process guarantee the storage is never asked to keep
- application: a shutdown no longer reports a clean stop it did not obtain. `net/http`'s `Shutdown` drains the connections the server still owns, and a **hijacked** connection is not one of them — a handler that upgrades to a websocket removes it from the server's accounting — so `Shutdown` returned immediately and `runHttp` answered nil while that handler was still running and the container was closing under it, with the request scope it opened, its session and everything it reached. Measured against a real `net/http.Server`: an ordinary handler held past the budget answered `context deadline exceeded` after the full wait, while a hijacked one answered `<nil>` in 0s with the handler still running. The http kernel now counts the request scopes it opens and the shutdown waits for them under the *same* budget after `Shutdown` returns; a drain that does not finish is recorded as `http shutdown left request scopes open`, carrying the count, and the process exits non-zero. **Behavioural change**: a deployment that serves upgraded connections and does not close them from a shutdown hook now exits non-zero after the budget instead of zero immediately, which is the signal that requests were still inside the server when it stopped
- event: a dispatch builds no debug record the journal would discard. Every dispatch assembled two context maps at the call site and resolved the listener's name through `reflect` plus `runtime.FuncForPC` **per listener per dispatch**, all of it handed to a `Debug` that a journal at error level throws away unread — on the hottest path the framework has, since the kernel dispatches at least three events per request. The journal is asked once per dispatch through the new `logging.LevelEnabled`, and the listener name is resolved where it is used rather than ahead of the branches that might use it, so the failure wrapper's context and the required-listener refusal still name the listener with debug off. Measured against a json logger at error level over a discarded stream: 17 / 22 / 50 allocations per dispatch at 0 / 1 / 8 listeners before, **11 / 12 / 12** after — the growth per listener is gone
- config: the refusal of an empty `MELODY_ENV` names the key, the parameter and the files the emptiness already selected. A present-but-empty value is read as `dev` by the dotenv source, which loads `.env.dev`, and refused by the kernel configuration immediately after with `environment may not be empty` and an empty context — so a deployment template that rendered `MELODY_ENV=` booted far enough to take the development values and then died naming neither the key to search for nor the reason those files were the ones read. Both readers keep their answers, deliberately: an empty value is an accident of a rendered template rather than a request to run in development, and `CONFIG.md` now says so where it used to promise the opposite
- config: `Parameter.Bool` reads through the shared parser its sibling accessors use, so a refusal carries the cause that names the parameter, the target type and the value. What it accepts and refuses is unchanged — the hand-written branches recognised exactly the shapes `internal.Bool` recognises — but a parameter holding a number, which is what `RegisterRuntime("feature.flag", 1)` hands over, was refused with a nil cause and a context carrying only the key: the operator learned that something failed and nothing about what, while every sibling answered with a `ParseError`. It was the last typed door outside the sentence `Int`'s own GoDoc makes, that one grammar answers for every typed reading of a parameter. A secret still withholds the whole cause
- validation: `NewMinLength` and `NewMaxLength` refuse a negative bound, which the tag door beside them has always refused with the reason written in a comment. A length is never negative, so a negative bound is a declaration mistake — and the constraints it built were worse than useless: `NewMinLength(-1)` accepted every value in silence, a rule that reads as enforced, validates nothing, and leaves no record that it does not, while `NewMaxLength(-1)` refused every value including the empty string with `this field must not exceed -1 characters`, which is what the client was handed. The refusal is a panic because a constructor cannot answer an error without changing every caller, and because this is a declaration mistake rather than an input — the shape the cron runner already uses for the same class. **Behavioural change**: a caller passing a negative bound now fails at construction instead of building a constraint that cannot do its job

- http: the rate-limit middleware and the rate-limit request listener honour the already-logged mark before writing their record, the way the exception listener and the five sites in the http kernel already do. They were the two readers of a limiter failure that filed unconditionally, so a limiter that reports its own store failures — which the redis limiter now does by default, since two of its three doors return nothing at all — produced two identical records for every refused request, at the moment during an outage when every request is refused and the journal is under the most load. The record is filed once, at the deepest point that knows the key and the failure mode
- cache: a `Remember` waiter can be given the request's context and leaves when the request does. The shipped default is an unbounded wait and `Wait` had no context door at all, so with a callback that hangs — the one case coalescing exists for — every waiter parked for as long as the callback took, holding its goroutine and with it the request scope, the session and everything the request reached. Measured: with the callback hung, 0 of 50 calls returned in 300 ms with the callers' context already canceled, and 50 of 50 once the callback was released. A canceled context now ends that caller's wait with `cache remember wait canceled by the caller context`, carrying `context.Canceled` or `context.DeadlineExceeded` as the cause so `errors.Is` reaches it, and told apart from the timeout by message. An answer already memoized when the wait begins is handed over whatever the context says, so a finished flight and a context that lapsed in the same instant no longer toss a coin
- session: `NewFileStorageFromFile` refuses a handle opened with `O_APPEND`, and the in-place write names offset zero rather than seeking to it. A seek is advice an appending handle ignores: `write(2)` on such a descriptor lands at the end whatever was sought, so every snapshot went **after** the document it was replacing and the truncation to the new length then cut the pair. Measured, in both shapes: a growing snapshot left the previous document intact with the new one dangling past it, which the decoder reads as one value and the trailing bytes as nothing — so `Save` returned nil, the next boot reported no error, and every save made since the handle was taken was simply absent; a shrinking snapshot cut into the old document and the next boot answered `failed to decode session storage file`, losing every persisted session. `WriteAt` refuses an appending handle by contract, so the write either lands at zero or fails as one, and the constructor asks the same question with a write of nothing so the operator learns at boot rather than at the first save
- debug: `debug:router --format=json` survives a cyclic route attribute. A route attribute is arbitrary `any` from userland, and `json.Marshal` answers a cycle with an error — which routed the value into the `%v` fallback, where `fmt` has no cycle detection of its own: the command died of `fatal error: stack overflow`, which no recover in the command layer turns into a reported failure. The cycle-guarded walk `debug:container` already carries, and whose comment names this exact hazard, now runs first, so the cycle renders as `<cycle>` and the rest of the attribute survives. It is asked to keep the noise keys, because dropping an attribute named `stack` or `trace` is error-context redaction and has no business in a route listing
- debug: `debug:container --format=json` keeps `errorContextJson` parseable when a context value refuses `json.Marshal`. The sanitizing walk passes an unrecognised scalar through untouched, so a provider failing with a `chan`, a `func` or a `complex` in its context reached the live fallback, which answered the Go syntax rendering `map[listener:0x5f8e40]` — and `.data.items[].errorContextJson | fromjson`, the consumer this very line documents, died on that row. The rendering now travels as `{"raw": …}`, a json object like the `{}` of a service that resolved, so the field parses on every row; the table keeps the bare rendering, where the value is read rather than parsed
- debug: `debug:events` declares the serving-process listeners on the branch that cannot inspect the dispatcher, in both formats. The declaration is the command's own and does not come from the dispatcher, so a dispatcher that does not implement the inspection contract cost the listing and, with it, the answer to "is access control wired?" — which is the one question the declaration exists to answer with something other than an absence, and that branch is the likelier one to be read, since a dispatcher that cannot be listed is already a reason to go looking

- security: an `AccessDeniedHandler` that fails leaves a record of its own failure. The refusal record was filed **before** the handler ran — it had to be, since a handler that answers the request returns early and dispatches no `kernel.exception` — and the mark set below it then suppressed the exception listener that used to carry the handler's failure, so a permanently broken refusal page reached no channel at all. The one record is now filed on whichever exit the path takes, carrying what the handler did with it: at warning with the decision's own reason when the handler answered, at error with `deniedHandlerOutcome` and `handlerError` when it failed or answered nil. A handler that PANICS is unaffected — the dispatcher records the panic with its stack and returns it — and only the line naming the refusal reason is lost there
- security: `RoleHierarchyVoter` no longer costs a delegate its token type. It handed the delegate a rebuilt `AuthenticatedToken`, so an integrator's own voter — the multi-tenant one the constructor's own GoDoc invites, which asserts the concrete token to learn whose data is being asked for — failed that assertion on every request and abstained. Measured: a tenant voter that refuses directly abstained through the wrapper, and under the unanimous strategy beside a granting role voter the request the tenant rule was written to withhold was **granted**. The token now answers its own twin when it can (`RolesReplacer`), and is rebuilt as before when it cannot
- security: the access control listener judges a typed-nil decision manager as the nil it is instead of comparing it against nil. `NewCompiledFirewall` is a public door and a registry assembled through it never passes the compile step, so a manager holding nothing reached `DecideAll` and answered every request behind the firewall with a recovered panic, where the missing-manager branch exists to answer it
- security: a dispatcher that cannot mark the access control listener required is named on the emergency channel instead of disarming the fail-closed guarantee in silence. The mark is what makes a listener stopping propagation ahead of access control fail the dispatch closed rather than letting the request reach its handler unchecked; the framework's own event adapter refuses the same condition with a panic, and this site swallowed it
- http: an error of rule WIRING returned by a handler is filed at error, not at warning. `logHandlerError` runs first on the production path and marks what it filed, so the exception listener's own error branch for a wiring fault — added with the classification itself — was unreachable for everything the kernel had already recorded: a route broken permanently by a `validate:"reqiured"` typo sat in the dashboard at warning, among the users who mistyped their address
- exception: the readers of the cause chain understand a joined error. `LogContext`, `BuildCauseChain` and `BuildCauseContextChain` all anchored on the single-valued `errors.Unwrap`, which answers nothing for an `errors.Join`, so a failure that gathered what several replicas or several destinations had to say reached the record as one flattened line of text with the context of every branch gone. The walk is breadth-first over both unwrap shapes and the depth budget bounds the number of links rendered, so a wide join costs no more than a deep chain; a chain of single wraps produces exactly the sequence it always did
- container: a resolution performed after the teardown finished is refused instead of being answered out of the maps. It used to hand back the instance found there — already closed, every `Close` in the process having run on it — with a nil error, so a caller holding a resolver had a live-looking handle to a dead service. Both doors ask: the fast path that answers a memoized singleton without reaching the creation guard, and the guard itself, which a resolution through a scope reaches
- container: the panic a service's `Close` raises is recorded with its cause and its stack. This is a containment boundary — nothing above it sees the panic and nothing below it survives — so what the record dropped was gone: an error-shaped panic value kept only as its stringified message collapsed to one line at the render boundary, taking the context map and the cause chain of the very error the `Close` raised with it, and the frames that ran existed only inside the recover
- application: the normal return of `Run` tears the container down under the same ten-second shield the panic path uses, and exits non-zero when it has to abandon it. The teardown loop is strictly sequential with no budget of its own, so one `Close` that never returns — a pooled connection draining to a peer that is gone, a session file on a vanished mount — parked every service behind it and the process with them. The healthy shutdown was the one without an escape while the dying one had ten seconds and a way out

- container: the teardown closes a service before what it resolved through a resolver it kept, not only before what it resolved during construction. The dependency edge was read from the live resolution stack, which the provider's own return empties, so `container.Lazy` and the `ContainerCarrier` pattern recorded no edge at all and the ordering fell back to descending name order — an outbox holding a lazily-resolved redis handle had that handle closed first and drained, at `Close`, through a dead connection, with the teardown reporting success. The owning node now travels on the view of the resolution the provider is handed, so a resolution made long afterwards still belongs to it. A handle built over the **container itself** has no owner and keeps the old ordering, which `Lazy`'s own GoDoc and `CONTAINER.md` now say out loud
- container: a resolution with nothing to write takes the container's read lock instead of its exclusive one — no scope layered over it, no dependency edge to record, and the instance already built. Every resolution took the exclusive lock, even a bare read of a singleton built at boot, so dependency injection had a ceiling that did not move with the number of cores and got *worse* with them: measured at 1.23 million resolutions a second on one goroutine and 0.83 million on eight, against 2.65 and 5.43 million now. A resolution that would record an edge, one through a kept resolver included, still takes the exclusive path that writes the graph
- session: burying a tombstone prunes only the burials that have lapsed, walking them in the order they happened, instead of sweeping the whole record on every call. The record grows with the rate of logins and every login and every logout buries one, so the cost per burial grew with the traffic driving it — twenty thousand burials took 2.43 seconds and one more cost 166 microseconds with the manager's lock held, against 20 milliseconds and 768 nanoseconds now
- session: `SaveSession` and `DeleteSession` hold a lock keyed to the session id across the storage call rather than one lock for the whole manager. The tombstone check and the write stay one critical section, which is the invariant — for the same session; two requests saving *different* sessions no longer wait on each other, and a storage behind a network no longer serialises every session write in the process. Sixteen concurrent saves of distinct sessions against a 2 ms storage took 35.5 ms and now take 2.8 ms, which is what hitting the storage directly costs. The premise the previous comment rested on — that both storages melody ships serialise internally anyway — is true of those two and false of the published `session/contract.Storage` an application implements over redis or a database
- session: the in-memory storage sweeps expired sessions in chunks, releasing the lock between them, the way the cache backend's sweep already did. It is the default storage and its map grows with the number of signed-in users, so one whole-map pass under the exclusive lock stalled every request in flight: at two hundred thousand sessions the worst concurrent `Load` waited 21.4 ms, and now waits 3.9 ms. A session saved again while the sweep is between chunks is read a second time before it is deleted, so a user who came back is not signed out by a reading taken before they did
- http/middleware: the sliding-window rate limiter trims the expired marks by index instead of rebuilding the whole window on every call. The rebuild ran before the limit was even checked, so a refused request cost exactly as much as an admitted one and held the lock every key shares: at `IpRateLimit(10000)` one call allocated 245 KiB and took 72 microseconds, and an innocent client sharing the limiter with one attacker on a saturated key saw a mean decision latency of 72 microseconds and peaks of 1.87 ms. A call now costs about 200 nanoseconds and allocates nothing, whatever the limit
- http/middleware: the compression middleware takes its gzip writers from a pool per compression level instead of building one per response. A fresh writer costs about 800 KiB of window and hash tables, which made it the largest allocator on the request path — 816 KiB per compressed response, against 2 KiB now. A writer is reset onto the response's own pipe when it is taken and returned only once `Close` has returned, so no two responses can share one deflate state
- cache: a `RememberOption` that asks for both no coalescing and no wait — `NewDefaultRememberOption().WithStampedeProtectionEnabled(false).WithWaitTimeout(0)` — is now honoured instead of collapsing to the constructor defaults. The two fields landed on their zero value, which the option is guarded against as the unconfigured zero struct, so the caller who spelled the deliberate off-and-no-wait got stampede protection armed with an unbounded wait — the opposite on both fields, invisible from the option's getters. `waitTimeout` is a pointer now, so an unspoken wait is told apart from a deliberate zero; a value built outside the constructor still reads as the defaults. **Behavioural change**: a two-field-off option now runs every caller's callback rather than coalescing them
- security: a failing token source that returns a sub-500 `HttpException` — an expired jwt, a malformed `Authorization`, a bad signature — is recorded at warning rather than error. The resolution listener deposited every client token refusal at error and then marked it, so the level a deliberate 4xx earns was unreachable and every client with an expired token paged whoever reads the error stream, while the api-key sibling refusal already travelled to the exception listener at warning. A token backend that is genuinely down, and any refusal at 500 or above, keep the error level. **Behavioural change**: the log level of a client token refusal moves from error to warning
- security: a substituted `AccessDecisionManager` no longer loses the role hierarchy in silence. The compilation asserted on the concrete `*security.AccessDecisionManager`, so a manager of the integrator's own — even a wrapper that only delegated, to log or cache decisions — skipped the whole hierarchy upgrade: `ROLE_ADMIN: [ROLE_USER]` was refused with `403` on the enforcement path while `security.IsGranted(runtime, "ROLE_USER")`, which expands the hierarchy straight from the compiled firewall, kept answering `true` for the same request, with no record on either side. The hierarchy now travels through the `RoleHierarchyAware` capability, and a manager without it beside a declared hierarchy is refused at compilation, naming the firewall. **Breaking**: a configuration pairing a hierarchy with a foreign manager now fails the boot instead of enforcing without the hierarchy
- security: `NewRoleHierarchyVoter` takes any `Voter` as its delegate, not only the built-in `*RoleVoter`. The wrapper calls nothing but `Supports` and `Vote`, so the narrowing bought nothing and cost an integrator's own voter — multi-tenant, ownership — the ability to see the expanded roles: the only way out was copying the wrapper, which meant every foreign voter reimplementing the expansion rule. **Breaking at source** for a caller that leaned on the narrowing; every call passing a `*RoleVoter` compiles unchanged
- application: `serializer.ServiceSerializerManager`, `validation.ServiceValidator` and `http.ServiceUrlGenerator` are registered behind the `Has` gate the logger, the cache, the session and the firewall manager already had. Registered unconditionally, they could not be substituted at all: a module registering the serializer manager to add a media type — xml, msgpack, cbor, `application/vnd.api+json` — got the boot's duplicate-registration exit, code 1, rather than a substitution, and the manager has no registration method, so content negotiation was closed to everything but a fork. The router, the dispatcher, the clock, the config, the route registry and the process role stay ungated on purpose — they hand back objects the kernel owns and the request path reads directly, so a gate would promise a substitution the framework would then ignore
- application: `serializer.ServiceSerializer` is registered, so the two published resolvers answer. `SerializerMustFromRuntime` and `SerializerFromRuntime` were documented against an id nothing ever registered, so the Must door panicked for every caller and the soft one answered nil — by construction, on every boot the framework has ever performed. The registration is the default json serializer, behind the same `Has` gate; the id is what those two resolvers read, while content negotiation reads the manager, which is now written down on both the constants and in `SERIALIZER.md`
- cache: the in-memory backend judges a `Decrement` in the order the shared contract fixes — the closed backend first, then the key, and the magnitude of the delta last. `Decrement(key, math.MinInt64)` against a backend already closed answered "delta overflows int64 when negated" here and "cache backend is closed" through the redis sibling, which is exactly the difference the contract's own sentence exists to remove
- logging: the json logger reports its own failed write, once, on stderr. A `var/log` that is full, read-only or on a vanished mount silenced the entire journal with no signal on any channel, while the other way the same function can fail — a value that will not marshal — has had its fallback since it was written. The echo is written directly rather than through `EmergencyLogger`, which is itself a json logger and would re-enter the write that just failed, and it is skipped when the logger's own output already is stderr
- config: a failed parameter resolution names the environment key beside the internal alias. A `MELODY_*` key and its `kernel.*` alias are one parameter stored under both names and the sorted walk reaches the alias first, so `MELODY_LOG_PATH=%app.name%/application.log` failed the boot naming `kernel.log_path` — a name the operator had never written anywhere

- http: the public write door refuses a status outside net/http's writable range before it touches the writer, headers included. The response's headers were copied on first and the status judged after, so a caller that handled the returned error and wrote its own response sent it carrying the refused response's `Set-Cookie` on top of its own; the internal write path has refused the same range ahead of any rendering all along
- http: the exception listener renders the error text and its cause under the containment its siblings received. In debug mode a panic value whose own `Error()` dereferences exactly the nil that produced the panic raised a second panic while the listener was rendering the first — absorbed one level up by the dispatcher, at the price of the whole debug payload the listener exists to build, so the client received the kernel's fallback body and the journal an extra "event listener panicked"
- session: the `NewFileStorageFromFile` writer writes before it truncates, and truncates to the length it just wrote. The order was a truncation to zero followed by the write, so a process killed between the two — an OOM kill, a `docker kill`, a deploy with no grace period — left a zero-length file, which the next boot reads as "no sessions at all" and answers by logging every user out without a single error. The temp-and-rename its `FromPath` sibling uses is not available to this door, which writes through a handle the caller owns and a rename would unlink; what remains is a torn document under a kill mid-write, and that is now written on the contract instead of being silent

- debug: `debug:container --build` reports its failures on the envelope, so it exits non-zero the way the single-service door always has. The sweep whose declared purpose is to build everything and report the failures with their causes answered `"error": null` and exit 0 over every one of them, leaving them reachable only as `data.items[].error` — a deploy step spelled `app debug:container --build --format=json || exit 1` passed a wiring that does not resolve. The envelope now carries `debug.buildFailed`, the failure count, the failed names and the first failure as its cause
- debug: two fields of one `debug:container` item stop changing their json TYPE with the value of the row. `errorCauseChain` was `null` on a service that resolved and a list on one that did not, and `errorContextJson` was the empty string beside a json object, so `jq '.data.items[].errorCauseChain[]'` died at the first healthy service and `.errorContextJson | fromjson` died with "Cannot parse ''". Both keep one type across every row of one document — an empty array and `{}` — while the table keeps the empty cell a person reads
- debug: `debug:events --format=json` declares the serving-process listeners at every verbosity, on `data.servingProcessListeners`, beside the listing rather than instead of it. The declaration exists so that "is access control wired?" is not answered with an absence meaning "not in this process"; the table has printed it at every verbosity all along, while the json document carried it only under `--verbose`, so a consumer auditing the wiring read a list the two security listeners were simply missing from — and `--verbose` reparents the payload, so it could not be reached without rewriting the query
- debug: `--order=desc` reaches both halves of the `debug:events` document. The listing was reversed and the listener detail left ascending, contradicting the selector's own comment that the detail is ordered "the way the listing is ordered". The direction now orders the events; inside one event the rows keep the dispatch order their `order` field reports, which is not a sortable direction
- debug: `debug:router --format=json` produces its document when a route carries an attribute the encoder cannot represent. Route attributes are arbitrary `any` from userland, and one closure among them made the whole envelope fail to marshal — the printer has no fallback, so the command answered ZERO bytes on stdout, indistinguishable from a missing binary. A value that marshals keeps its json type, so a `[]string` attribute stays a list; only one that cannot degrades to the `%v` rendering the verbose table already prints
- debug: every `debug:middleware` item carries `reason`, empty where there is nothing to say. It was omitted from every active row, so a consumer keying on it could not tell an active middleware from a malformed document; what `--build` cannot fill — the name and the priority, which the build provider does not hand back — is now stated on the type and in the documentation rather than guessed at by position
- serializer: a header refusing one registered type is no longer answered `406 Not Acceptable` while another registered type was never refused. `Accept: application/json;q=0` against a manager holding json and plain text refused the whole negotiation, because the refusal was a single flag raised by one covered candidate: the client that said "anything but json" was denied the plain text it never rejected. A refused type is now recorded as such and can never be served — not by the negotiation and not by the fallback, which steps past json when json is what was refused — and `ErrNotAcceptable` is answered only when every registered type is refused
- application documentation: `NewApplication` is documented on its real signature, `NewApplication(embeddedEnvFiles, embeddedPublicFiles)`. Both parameters are `fs.FS`, so the compiler never objects to them being swapped, and the reference and its copy-paste example both taught the inverted order: a deployment built with `-tags melody_env_embedded melody_static_embedded` handed the public files to the environment source and the configuration files to the static server, failing the boot on "no `.env` keys contributed" — invisible under the default tags, so it surfaced at the release build

- httpclient: a per-request credential header no longer follows a cross-origin redirect on the streaming path. `RequestStreamWithContext` bound the caller's context to the request AFTER it was built, and the per-request credential names travel to the redirect policy through that same context — the whole context was replaced and the plant went with it, so the policy found nothing to strip and an `X-Api-Key` passed through `WithHeader` reached the host the first server chose, in clear, measured. The context is now bound where the request is created, which closes the class rather than the site: no later `WithContext` can drop the plant. The buffered path always stripped correctly
- http: the wiring panics of the request setup are answered instead of resetting the connection. Everything between the scope opening and the main recovery guard — the request logger, the request context override, the config and dispatcher resolutions, the routing — plus a panic raised inside the main guard itself, used to escape `ServeHttp`: net/http closed the connection with no response, no structured record was filed and the terminate listener never fired, so the access log had no line at all. A last guard, registered under the scope-close defer so the response is written while the scope is still open, files one record carrying the method, the path and the stack, and answers a rendered 500. `nethttp.ErrAbortHandler` still travels by identity. What cannot be restored is stated where it is lost: no terminate dispatch, hence no access-log line, and no `kernel.exception` dispatch, hence no application error page
- security: a 403 names the branch that produced it. The nine refusals of the access decision manager were identical to the byte — same status, same message, empty context — so a firewall naming an attribute no configured voter looks at, which is a wiring fault answered fail-closed, was indistinguishable in the journal from a client who simply lacks the role. Each refusal now carries its reason, the strategy and the attribute in the exception context, which the response never renders; the listener files exactly one record naming the reason, the firewall and the matched rule, at warning for a refusal and at error for the wiring fault alone. The record is filed before the access denied handler can answer and return early — that exit dispatches no `kernel.exception` and used to leave no trace at all — and the value the dispatch carries is marked, so the exception listener attaches its coordinates instead of filing the refusal a second time. `DecideAny` refuses an empty attribute list, the guard its `DecideAll` sibling always had. **Behavioural change**: one record per refusal, at the level the branch deserves, where a real denial left a generic `unhandled exception` and the wiring fault left the same
- http: a handler failure that carries no melody error files one record, not two. `MarkLogged` marks the nearest `AlreadyLogged` implementer in the chain, and `errors.New`, `fmt.Errorf` and every runtime error implement none — so the mark the kernel wrote had nowhere to land and the exception listener filed the same failure again, measured at two records for a handler returning `errors.New` and two for any runtime panic. The handler-error writer and the panic recovery now hand the exception dispatch a marked carrier keeping the original as its cause, while the application's error handler and the debug message still receive the error the handler returned. The record also names the request method beside the path
- http: `ReadFrom` on the recording response writer records the commit only after the copy, and only when a byte actually reached the delegate — the convention `WriteHeader` and `Write` already follow in the same type, and the door `io.Copy` takes for every streamed body. The flag was raised before the copy, so a body source that failed — or panicked — before the first byte was classified as a committed stream: the recovery skipped its 500 and the client received an implicit empty 200 for a handler bug, measured end to end. `Flush` records after the delegate returns under the same convention. **Behavioural change**: a streamed response whose source dies before the first byte now answers the rendered 500 instead of an empty 200
- http: the terminate event and the access log report the status a self-committed stream actually carries: the recording writer now records the committed status code, and `writeResponse` reports it for the response the kernel substituted — the journal recorded 204 for every streamed 200 and a rendered-but-never-written 500 for a panic mid-stream, so status-distribution queries over the access log were wrong for every streaming route. A hijacked connection records no status and keeps the substitute. **Behavioural change**: the status the access log reports on streaming routes changes from the synthetic response's to the wire's
- http: the kernel's handler-error writers — `controller handler error` and `not found handler error` — read the already-logged mark before filing, record a deliberate 4xx at warning and everything else at error, and mark what they filed, the discipline the panic recovery and the exception listener already share. Without the mark every handler failure produced two records, the first at error even for a routine 404 or 429, so failure counts were doubled and an operator alerting at error was paged for every throttled client. **Behavioural change**: one record per handler failure, at the level the status class deserves
- http: a handler that honours its context and returns the request context's own cancellation is answered in the journal as `request cancelled by client` at warning — it was recorded as `controller handler error` at error plus a second error record from the exception listener, indistinguishable from a genuine handler fault, so error-rate alerting fired on the client's own disconnects
- http: a response-write failure the client caused — the broken-pipe family, the cancelled request context — is recorded at warning under `failed to write response; client disconnected`, and both classes of the record now carry the method and path: at error every impatient download paged the operator, in a record that could not even name the route it happened on
- http: `WriteToHttpResponseWriter` refuses a status outside net/http's `[100, 999]` by name through its own error return — it is a public door, and the delegate's panic turned an external caller's arithmetic mistake into a connection reset where the signature promises an error
- http: `Kernel.SetForwardedHeadersPolicy` and `NewForwardedClientIpResolver` copy the trusted proxy list instead of retaining the caller's slice. Every request's proxy-trust decision reads that list — whether `X-Forwarded-Proto` is believed, which sets the session cookie's `Secure` attribute, and which peer a rate limit keys on — so a caller reusing its slice after the hand-over rewrote the trust decision mid-serving, as an unsynchronized write racing every request goroutine; every sibling configuration door already copies its caller lists. The contract states the ownership, and states that the leftmost `X-Forwarded-Proto` convention requires the trusted edge to SET the header rather than append to it
- http: `CompressionMiddleware` reads a nil configuration as the default one, the way the cors middleware and the route group read their absent options, and normalizes an out-of-range level or minimum on a private copy instead of writing back into the caller's object — it used to panic on the nil dereference and to silently rewrite the caller's own configuration at construction. The deprecated `CorsMiddleware` reads nil as its replacement's default service instead of panicking. **Behavioural change**: a configuration handed to `CompressionMiddleware` keeps the values it was built with
- http: `static.NewFileServer` refuses nil options by name instead of panicking on the dereference — refusal rather than a default, because the default here would be a live file server over the `public` directory, and a nil that is almost always a wiring mistake must not start serving files nobody asked served
- http: the static file server's two ordinary-control-flow exits — the non-retrieval method and the out-of-prefix path — are recorded at debug instead of info: with the middleware registered globally they fired once per api request, exactly the per-request noise the package's own logging comment keeps out of the journal. **Behavioural change**: the two records drop from info to debug
- http: `NewHttpMiddlewareDefinition` copies its four constraint lists and `MiddlewareBuildReport.SetInactive` copies like every sibling accessor of the report — a registrant reusing a slice silently rewrote the ordering constraints the pipeline was registered under, and the one non-copying setter let a caller rewrite a report the diagnostics already held. **Behavioural change**: mutating a slice after registration is now inert
- http: the rate-limit middleware records a limiter call the caller's own cancellation ended at warning under `rate limiter call cancelled` — at error it read as a store outage against a perfectly healthy store, once per disconnect on a rate-limited route
- httpclient: `RequestOptions.Headers` and `Query` hand out copies. The live maps invited writes that bypass the canonicalization `SetHeader` exists to enforce, and a non-canonical spelling planted through the getter beside the canonical one made the request-time winner a map-iteration choice — in what is often a credential header, the exact nondeterminism the setters refuse. **Behavioural change**: mutating the returned map no longer changes the options; the setters remain the one door that writes
- exception: `LogContext` and the cause-chain rendering produce an error's text under a recover, the containment the container teardown's close-error rendering already applies. Both run inside recovery defers, where a value whose `Error()` panics — usually on the very nil field that made it panic-worthy — raised a second panic past the recovery that was reporting the first: the connection was reset with the original failure reaching no record at all, measured
- http: `RecoverToError` normalizes a typed-nil error to the generic branch the way the exit handler's resolver normalizes it, the kernel's debug-mode message renders through the same containment, a discarded response body whose `Close` panics inside the recovery defer is contained into the error the caller already reports, and an application serializer that panics on the error payload degrades to the json fallback under the renderer's own "an error response always exists" — each of these second-order failures used to escape `ServeHttp` and reset the connection, measured
- logging: the exit handler's resolve step runs under its own shield, honouring the claim of the comment beside the other steps — a recovered value whose `Error()` panicked unwound into `main` and the process died with the Go runtime's exit code 2: no record, no certificate, no stderr echo, no before-exit teardown, measured. A resolve that panics is answered with a generic record under the caller's own exit code
- security: each of the three direct 401 refusals of the access-control listener files one warning naming its reason — `missing_token`, `token_not_authenticated`, or `missing_security_context`, the last a wiring fault rather than a client mistake. The branches answer the request directly by design, and used to complete with no trace at all while the byte-identical-looking 403 filed a warning with its reason. **Behavioural change**: a 401 now leaves a record
- cache: `SetMultiple` validates its batch over sorted keys on the in-memory backend, so a batch carrying two malformed keys names the same culprit on every call instead of one chosen by map iteration — the rule the redis backend's batch reporting already follows
- event: a subscriber installation on `EventDispatcherAdapter` is one critical section against its twin and its removal, the guard the concrete dispatcher's `subscriberMutex` already carries: without the outer section two concurrent `AddSubscriber` calls for one identity both passed the duplicate refusal and installed every listener twice — measured under four-way contention — and a `RemoveSubscriber` interleaved with an installation removed the half already installed while the rest kept arriving under a record the remover was just told is gone
- event: the adapter's `RegisteredEvents` breaks equal-priority ties by the wrapped dispatcher's listener id — the tiebreak dispatch actually uses — instead of an adapter-side counter issued under a different lock. The comment on that counter claimed taking it after the listener id prevented inversion, but with no common critical section two concurrent registrations could receive the two counters in opposite orders, and the inspector then reported an execution order the dispatch never uses, permanently; the counter is gone
- http: a response status code outside net/http's `[100, 999]` answers the rendered 500 instead of an implicit empty 200. `WriteHeader` panics on such a code before anything reaches the connection, but the recording writer raised its header-commit flag before delegating, so the recovery read the response as a committed stream, skipped writing its error page, and the client — and every monitor — saw a success for a handler bug. The flag is now raised only after the delegate returns, and `writeResponse` refuses the out-of-range code by name ahead of the delegate, logging the code it refused. **Behavioural change**: a handler bug that produced such a code now answers 500 instead of 200
- http: `PrefersHtml` splits Accept members and parameters outside quoted sections through `internal.SplitOutsideQuotes`, the serializer reader's grammar — the third layer of the one-grammar rule after the joined field lines and the shared q-value reader. A bare split cut through a quoted parameter value, so `text/html;p="a,b";q=0, application/json` lost the explicit html refusal for this reader while the serialized branch honoured it: the html error page was served to the one client that refused html, measured
- http: the compression middleware joins every line of a repeated `Accept-Encoding` field before parsing, the way both Accept readers join theirs — reading only the first line dropped a coding the client named on the second — splits members outside quoted sections through the same shared grammar, and resolves a repeated coding to its higher q, the tie rule of every Accept reader: last-wins made `gzip;q=0.5, gzip;q=0` and its reversal answer differently for one statement
- http: the compression peek buffer grows with the bytes actually read instead of being allocated at the full `MinSize` upfront: the threshold has no upper bound, so an oversized — or unit-confused — minimum turned every eligible response into an allocation of that size before a single byte arrived, when a response below the threshold needs no more memory than its own length
- serializer: a full negotiation tie — equal quality and equal specificity — resolves through the json-first convention `defaultSerializer` states for the empty header. `Accept: */*` is the RFC spelling of the same "I take anything", but it answered the lexically first candidate, so a manager carrying `application/hal+json` beside `application/json` served hal to the wildcard and json to the empty header. **Behavioural change**: a deployment with several serializers may see the wildcard's answer move to json
- cache: `Remember` answers one shape for one key — the computing call now returns the value passed through the manager's serializer round-trip (one local encode+decode, no backend involved), the exact shape every cached call answers. Without it a callback's `int` came back `float64` and its struct came back a `map[string]any`, but only from the second call on: a type assertion held on the cold path and failed on the warm one, the works-in-dev heisenbug. **Behavioural change**: the miss return of `Remember` over a `cache.Manager` is now the decoded generic shape, not the callback's own value
- cache: the in-memory counter accepts exactly the redis integer grammar — no whitespace padding, no plus sign, no leading zeros, no minus zero — where a trimmed `ParseInt` adopted them all: the same payload incremented through this backend and errored through redis, measured live, while the refusal's own comment claimed redis parity
- cache: a nil payload is stored as the empty payload and reads back as an empty non-nil slice through both backends, and the rule is written on the `Backend` contract: redis has no nil to store, so the in-memory backend preserving the distinction let a caller tell the implementations apart by reading back what it wrote — measured live
- session: the response path decides a session's fate from one atomic `Snapshot` — values, modified flag and cleared flag read under a single lock acquisition, used by `Manager.SaveSession` and by `writeResponse`'s branch decision alike. Reading them through the individual accessors let a `Clear` racing the response land between the reads: the save branch wrote the emptied map — or the full pre-logout values — under a live id with the cookie re-issued, both calls reporting success, a within-request twin of the cross-request race the tombstone already refuses. **Breaking**: `Snapshot` is added to `session/contract.Session`, which breaks an out-of-tree implementation of that interface
- config: `Resolve` walks the parameters in sorted name order, so a boot with several broken templates fails on the same parameter every time — the dotenv reader's own rule, stated there for exactly this reason; the fixpoint itself was always order-independent, but the failure's identity was chosen by a random map walk
- debug: `debug:router` orders tied rows by registration order and `debug:middleware` orders same-name inactive entries by reason, making both comparators total: under an unstable sort, routes sharing pattern and methods — distinct at dispatch by host, priority or requirements — and same-name inactive entries flipped their rows from run to run, in the commands that exist to show which of them answers
- httpclient: header maps are canonicalized at the door and two spellings that collapse onto one header are refused by name — the serializer's convention for its mime keys. The maps are applied with `Set`, which canonicalizes, so `x-api-key` and `X-Api-Key` in one config stayed two entries whose survivor was chosen by map iteration order: a different value per request, in what is often a credential header. **Breaking** at construction for a configuration that carried two spellings of one header — a state that was already answering nondeterministically
- cache: a `With` setter called on the exact zero-value `RememberOption` first reads the receiver as the constructor defaults, then applies its own field. The whole-struct normalization in `Remember` covered only the pristine zero value, so a zero option plus one setter carried a waitTimeout of zero — every miss answered "cache remember callback timed out" while the callback computed in the background — and configuring cancelability alone silently disarmed the stampede protection. A deliberate no-wait or protection-off option is spelled through the constructor and stays what it says. **Behavioural change**: an option assembled from the zero value with setters now reads unset fields as the defaults instead of as zeroes
- container: `MustGet` and `MustGetByType` panic with the failure the way `FromResolver` and `FromResolverByType` return it — a melody error travels out whole with the service name or type written into its context in place, and only a foreign error is wrapped naming it. The rebuilt wrapper shed the log level, the already-logged mark, the capture stack and every wrapper above it, so a provider failure logged once at its terminal record filed a second full record at every recovery site that read the mark off the fresh wrapper. `container.MustGet` also delegates to the resolver context the way `MustGetByType` always has, ending its private copy of the same wrapper. **Behavioural change**: the panic value of a failing `MustGet`/`MustGetByType` is now the original error, so its message is the cause's own rather than "failed to get service instance"
- container: the wrapper a coalesced waiter receives for a failed creation inherits the already-logged mark of the failure it carries. The mark is read at the nearest implementer, so the fresh unmarked wrapper shadowed it and every waiter on the one failed creation filed a full record for the failure the owner had already logged — identical outages, asymmetric log output decided by scheduling
- event: the wrapper the dispatcher returns for a failing listener inherits the already-logged mark of the listener's error, the reading the panic half has always applied to a marked panic value; the kernel comment that promises the mark is visible "on anything wrapping a marked error" is now true on the error path too
- event: a listener that fails while also stopping propagation with a required listener behind it has its failure travel as the cause of the stop's refusal, through the new `NewRequiredListenerSkippedErrorWithStoppedListenerFailure`. The failure was deliberately returned unlogged on the promise that the caller's single record names it, and on exactly this path the caller received the causeless stop refusal instead — the reason the request failed closed existed on no log at all
- http: `UserRateLimit` and `UserRateLimitWithResolver` refuse a nil user-id callback at construction with a named panic, the way the middleware constructor refuses its missing limiter. Accepted, the nil callback died on the request path — an invalid-memory-address panic once per request, inside a closure no caller can reach. **Breaking**: a construction that used to succeed and fail at serve time now fails at boot
- http: `PrefersHtml` reads the Accept header under the serializer's rules — every line of a repeated field joined before parsing, and a member whose q parameter falls outside the RFC 7231 qvalue grammar dropped whole. It read only the first line while the error renderer's serialized branch joined them all, so the two negotiators of one error response saw two different views of the client's preference, and its bare float parse honoured `q=Inf` as an infinite weight and let `q=NaN` poison every comparison. The shared grammar lives in `internal.ParseQualityValue`, the one reader all three negotiating sites now use
- http: `acceptsGzip` drops an Accept-Encoding entry whose q parameter falls outside the qvalue grammar instead of scoring it with a bare float parse, under which `q=Inf` switched the compression on and `q=NaN` switched it off
- http: the static file server honours an explicit cache max age of zero as `Cache-Control: public, max-age=0` — always revalidate, with the ETag and Last-Modified machinery intact — because the configuration door validates zero as a distinct choice; only a negative value reads as unset and takes the 3600 default. Coercing zero shipped an hour of client-side freshness to the operator who asked for none. **Breaking**: `MELODY_STATIC_CACHE_MAX_AGE=0`, and a zero passed to `NewFileServerConfig` with the cache enabled, now emit `max-age=0` where they used to emit `max-age=3600`
- http: `cors.NewService` reads a nil method or header list as the one default `DefaultService` grants — Authorization included — and keeps an empty list as the expressed preference, the reading the origins field always had. Two default header lists meant an SPA sending `Authorization` worked on the zero-configuration deployment and died at preflight the moment the operator narrowed only the origins, and one Config read the same empty spelling as "nobody" on origins and "everything" on its siblings. **Breaking**: an explicitly empty `AllowMethods`/`AllowHeaders` now advertises nothing where it used to advertise the defaults, and the header fallback gains `Authorization`
- session: `NewManager` and its siblings refuse a positive ttl shorter than one second, the refusal the configuration door has always given for `MELODY_HTTP_SESSION_TTL`: below one second the value is not a short session but a broken one — the storage purges every lapsed entry on the write that stores the new one, so `SaveSession` reported success and persisted nothing. Zero keeps its meaning of no expiry. **Breaking**: a manual wiring that passed a sub-second ttl now fails at construction instead of silently storing nothing
- bag: the zero-value `ParameterBag` accepts its first write — `Set` and `AppendString` allocate the nil map the way `exception.Error.SetContextValue` always has — instead of half-working: the reads answered the zero value while the first write died on a raw nil-map assignment
- config: the comment at the session-ttl default registration states the default that exists — zero, no expiry, with the boot warning as the compensation — instead of asserting the bounded lifetime the constant it registers contradicts

- event: a subscriber installation and removal are each one critical section against their twins. The duplicate refusal and the per-listener installation ran under separate lock acquisitions, so two concurrent `AddSubscriber` calls for one identity both passed the refusal that exists to keep the ambiguity out — every listener live twice, each dispatch firing it twice — and a `RemoveSubscriber` interleaved with an installation removed the half already installed while the rest kept arriving under a record the remover was just told is gone. The dispatcher presents itself as safe for concurrent use everywhere else; now the subscriber surface holds it too
- cache: the refusal order joins the shared backend contract — the closed answer wins over the key judgment, and a batch write judges the ttl before its keys, the redis backend's order. The two implementations of one contract refused the same call differently when it was wrong in more than one way, which is the distinguishability the shared key grammar exists to prevent. **Behavioural change**: an operation on a closed in-memory backend now answers "cache backend is closed" where a malformed key used to answer for it, and a batch carrying both a malformed key and a negative ttl is refused for the ttl
- http: a rate-limit handler that produces neither response nor error still refuses the request through the middleware door, answered 429 exactly as the listener door answers it. Passed through, the nil response was normalized into an empty 204 — a refused request served as success — while the identical handler outcome behind the listener already answered 429
- http: `RateLimitMiddleware(nil)` is refused with the named panic its listener twin gives for the same wiring mistake, instead of an invalid-memory-address panic at boot
- http: `pipeline.Builder.Describe` mirrors `Build`'s nil-factory refusal, so `debug:middleware` no longer reports as healthy a pipeline the serving boot refuses; the description's promise — the same refusals, no factory invoked — now covers all three of them
- httpclient: the streaming path judges an invalid explicit response-body cap before anything is dialled, the rule the buffered path always held. Refused after the exchange, a POST that had already committed its side effect answered with an error phrased as though nothing had been sent, and a caller retrying on it duplicated the operation. **Behavioural change**: a streaming request with a non-positive explicit cap now fails without reaching the server
- container: `FromResolverByType` dresses its failure the way `FromResolver` does — a melody error travels out whole with the service type written into its context, a foreign error is wrapped naming the type — instead of returning the raw error with nothing to say which resolution failed
- http: `RouteGroup.HandleWithOptions` reads nil options as the default options — an unnamed route answering every method, still carrying the group's prefix, requirements and defaults — the answer the router's own door always gave for the same input and the reading the Symfony model it mirrors gives to absence. The group was the one registration surface that refused it with a panic. **Behavioural change**: a grouped registration passing nil options now registers instead of panicking
