- container: a scope override is answered by the type-keyed resolutions of every type its name is registered under, exactly as the container-level override propagates. The scope filed the override under the value's concrete type alone, so once the container had memoized the named instance — the ordinary warm state of a running process, one earlier request suffices — `Get(name)` through the scope answered the override while `GetByType(interface)` answered the container's instance: two different services inside one request, decided by whether anything had resolved the name before. The registered types are read from all three layers — the container, the scope plan, the scope's own registrations — and the maps written stay the scope's own, so the container and every sibling scope keep answering the container's instance and the override still dies with its scope. The propagation carries the sibling's guard with it. **Behavioural change**: a scope override whose value cannot sit under a type its name is registered under is refused with an error where it used to be accepted and filed half-way — answered by name, never by type
- container: the override assignability guard judges the value the way the readers will, on both the container and the scope: raw assignability for an interface registration, whose stored value is asserted against the interface at resolution, and canonical identity for a value-typed registration — a string service is registered under `*string` and its own provider's builds sit raw under that key, so a raw string override occupies exactly the slot a built value occupies. The container refused that override while serving identical values its own provider built, a rule stricter than its own storage
- container: a lazy handle follows the scope of the resolver it was built over instead of memoizing forever. The memoized value is served for as long as that scope lives; once the scope closes, the handle turns terminal — the scope-is-closed error on that call and every later one — and drops the value, the closure and the resolver together, so a dead request's state is neither served to every later caller nor held against the garbage collector. A handle over the container is untouched: a resolver that cannot answer the liveness question is read as open, and the concrete scope now answers it through `Closed()`, deliberately off the contract so no out-of-tree implementation breaks. A handle shared across scopes still answers with its own scope's value while that scope lives — the resolver is captured at construction, so sharing one handle across requests is resolving per call spelled wrong; `FromResolver` with the current request's resolver is that door, and the handle's documentation now says so. **Behavioural change**: a handle whose scope has closed errors — previously it served the dead value indefinitely
- application, http: a duplicate route joins the aggregated boot collision report instead of panicking one duplicate per boot attempt. Routes were the single registration channel outside the report — services, parameters, configurations and cli commands were all collected and raised together — because the report fired before the routes ever registered, so a consolidation that produced several route duplicates surfaced them one panic at a time, and the comment on the report claimed a completeness it did not have. `Boot` arms a recorder on the route registry for the boot window — the first registration wins, a dispatch duplicate and a claimed name are recorded under their own kinds, and the origin names the registration call site — and disarms it after the report has had its chance to raise; a route registry used outside an application boot keeps its immediate refusal
- application: a fatal failure of the configured logger fails the boot, at the step that owns it. The logger provider's panic — an unopenable log file, a module configuration of the wrong type — was recovered by the container into an error that `bootLogger` silently swallowed behind its emergency fallback, so `Boot` reported success for a process whose next logger resolution could only panic: the failure surfaced in `runCli`/`runHttp`, attributed to the run instead of to the configuration that broke it, measured as a boot that printed success and a command that never ran. The logger is now resolved once, eagerly, at the end of the container phase — whoever registered it — which also memoizes the instance, so nothing opens the log file a second time
- application: a relative `MELODY_LOG_PATH` is anchored to the project directory — the rule `ensureRuntimeDirectories` already applies to the logs and cache directories, now through the one shared helper — and the file's parent directory is created before the open. The path was handed to `os.OpenFile` verbatim, so the same binary with the same configuration wrote the log or died by working directory, and an operator path pointing outside the logs directory had no owner to create its parent; the default is untouched, because it is absolute through `%kernel.project_dir%`. An empty path still means the standard output, as before
- application: the final record of a process that dies without a live container logger is written to the destination the configuration names, not only to stderr. Every boot failure before the container phase — an unresolvable parameter, a refused environment, a module hook — left nothing in `var/log` for a deployment that collects it: the exit handler now opens the configured destination best-effort and writes the record there, falling back to the emergency logger only when that destination is itself unusable. The kernel view is built with the configuration, so the destination is readable for every failure after construction
- logging: the exit handler writes one certificate record at emergency level — "process exiting after unrecovered error", with the exit code and the error in its context — through whatever logger it resolved, always. The detailed exit record travels at the error's own level, which an operator threshold silently discards while the writer still marks it as logged, so a process filtered to `MELODY_LOG_LEVEL=emergency` died leaving a zero-byte log file and a suppressed-then-marked record left even the exit handler believing there was nothing to write. The certificate passes every threshold by construction and says what the detailed record does not: that the process is exiting, and with what code
- http: the kernel exception listener reads the already-logged mark before it writes, the way the kernel's own two writers beside it already read it. An error recorded upstream — the recovery of a panicking handler, a failed dispatch — was logged a second time under "unhandled exception": one failure, two records, measured live as two lines for one panicking request. What only this listener knows — the request id, the method, the path — is attached to the already-logged error's own context instead of being rewritten as a duplicate record; a key the error already holds is kept, and an empty coordinate is not written
- security: a token-source resolution failure is recorded once, and the record carries the request coordinates. The resolution listener logged it at error level and then dispatched `kernel.exception`, whose listener logged the same failure a second time; the failure is now normalized through `exception.FromError` before the record is written, so the logged mark has a link to live on — a token source is free to return a plain error, on which the mark does not stick — marked once the record is written, and the record names the method and the path beside the request id the request logger already contributes
- logging: the cause keys a caller set survive the record. `LogError`'s enrichment overwrote `cause`, `causeChain` and `causeContextChain` in the rendered context, while `exception.LogContext` beside it preserves a caller-set value for each of the three; the two renderers of the same convention now answer the same way, computing only the keys that are absent
- security: the two doors that take a `Runtime` from application code read it, and its scope, through the interface. `Runtime` is an interface an application may implement, so a typed nil passed a plain comparison and the scope was dereferenced bare — inside `IsGranted`, which by the comment beside it is reached from goroutines a handler starts and no recover covers, so the promised `(nil, false)` denial became a panic that ends the process. `SecurityContextFromRuntime` now answers not-found for either absence, and `SecurityContextSetOnRuntime` refuses with an error naming which one was missing. The container is deliberately not consulted as a fallback: the security context is a scope override and nothing registers it at process lifetime, so widening the lookup would be the wrong direction
- application, cli: an exit code is taken from a wrapped `*ExitError` only when the chain actually carries one. `errors.As` matches a typed-nil link and reports success, and both readers took that as an exit: the application handed `exception.Exit` a nil it refuses, so a failed command's own error was discarded in favour of a message about melody's own plumbing, and the cli aggregation asked a nil receiver for its code, got the out-of-range `0`, and had `NewExitError` panic on it — after the container was already closed, so that record reached stderr alone
- application: the kernel is read through the interface at the two doors that ask whether there is anything to tear down. A typed nil passed the plain comparison and reached `ServiceContainer()` on a nil receiver — in `resolveExitLogger`, which is evaluated as an argument and therefore runs before the exit handler's own per-step shield begins, so the panic replaced the one being reported and skipped both the teardown and `os.Exit`, ending the process as a bare traceback
- application: the logger provider reads the module configuration before it opens the log file. `LoggingConfigurationFromModules` panics on a configuration registered under the supported name with a type that does not implement the interface, and a descriptor opened above it was left with no owner at all: the container stores only what a provider returned, so nothing could ever close it, and a creation failure is not memoized, so every later resolution opened another one
- application: the module, module-provider and cli-command registration doors refuse a typed nil. The plain comparison accepted one, stored it, and let the boot report success; the failure then surfaced as a bare dereference inside the module's own hook, or at the `Modules()` call one line below the guard, instead of as the refusal each guard exists to give
- http: `Request.Input` answers the first value of a repeated key, as `FormValue` beside it and `url.Values.Get` already did. The request bags keep a single key and a repeated one apart by type and `bag.String` refuses a slice with a panic — right where the key is the programmer's, wrong at this door, because the shape of a request parameter is the client's to choose: `?tag=a&tag=b` turned every handler reading a parameter by name into a 500 with a full stack record, unauthenticated and free to repeat. The whole array stays reachable through `bag.StringSlice`, which is what the panic was pointing at. **Behavioural change**: a repeated key panicked and now answers its first value
- http: the static file server refuses a spelling that `path.Clean` folds into the mount root, which is the refusal the branch resolving every other path already carried. The matchers in front of the application compare the raw path, so `/open/..` is a url no rule on the mount ever saw, and answering it served the mount's index page from behind whatever rule the folded-away prefix carried. The index file is named by configuration and never by the request, so the target could not be aimed elsewhere — the exposure of that one page could. Canonical is the mount root itself, with or without its trailing slash. **Behavioural change**: `/.`, `/..`, `//` and `/anything/..` answered the index file and are now declined, so the request continues down the rest of the chain
- security: an access-control rule matches every spelling of the path it names. net/http hands the path through unfolded, so `//admin/panel` and `/open/../admin/panel` were matched by no rule naming `/admin` — and a request no rule matched is granted, with the token never consulted. A route ending in a catch-all parameter, which is how a single-page application, a proxy or a documentation mount is registered, accepts exactly those spellings and hands them to its handler: the rule was written, the route was reachable, and nothing in between said the two disagreed. The matcher now folds the path before comparing, which can only make a rule match a request it did not match before
- security: a typed-nil token reads as the absence it means at the three doors where a token crosses from application code into the framework — the authenticator, the token resolver and the firewall's token source — and at `SecurityContext.IsGranted`, whose constructor already read the firewall beside it that way. A nil pointer of the application's own token type is not equal to nil once it is inside the contract, so the plain comparison took it for a live token and the anonymous token each of those doors exists to hand back was never built. `AuthenticatedToken.IsAuthenticated` answers true without touching its receiver, so such a token reads as **authenticated** all the way to the first `Roles()` call, which panics inside the authorization listener: a 500 in place of the 401 the configuration asked for
- http: a typed-nil response reads as no response at all. `Response` is an interface, so a nil pointer of a response type a handler or a listener left unassigned arrives as a non-nil interface; every reader asks `nil == response` to decide whether a response exists, so it was taken for one and carried to the writer, where the first `Headers()` call panics — and the recovery defer then dereferenced the same value while closing it, a second panic inside the handler that had already recovered, leaving `ServeHttp` with no response at all and the connection reset. The four kernel events normalize it where they store it, and the kernel's handler door, `writeResponse`, `WriteToHttpResponseWriter` and the discarded-body close read through the interface
- http: the kernel reads a nil match result from `Router.Match` as the "no match" the contract permits. The contract returns a pointer beside a flag and the kernel's own second call, four lines below, already checked it; the first one dereferenced it, above the line that installs the recovery defer — so a `Router` implementation reporting no match the idiomatic way took the connection down with no response, no terminate event and no access-log line
- http: the exception listener reads an `*HttpException` out of the chain through `exception.AsHttpException`, the door the same function used twice already. `errors.As` matches the dynamic type of a typed nil and reports it as found, so a handler returning an unassigned `*HttpException` as its error made `StatusCode()` dereference nil inside the listener; the two branches this replaces asked the same question twice, one of them without the guard
- http: `SetCookie` allocates the response header map when the response has none. A nil header map is a state the contract permits — `SetHeaders` stores the nil it is given — and every other consumer of `Headers()` in the chain checked for it; this one wrote into it, which on the session paths is a panic on a response the handler had already produced
- http: the response the middleware chain produced is published to the recovery defer as soon as the chain returns, rather than after the error branch below it. The defer closes only what it can see, and a file-backed body — a `FileResponse`, or the static middleware's open file — was invisible to it for the whole of that branch: a panic raised while the kernel built the log context out of the handler's error left the descriptor open for the life of the process, with nothing recording it
- exception, logging, http: the already-logged mark is read at the depth it is written, through the single reader `exception.IsAlreadyLogged` that now sits beside the writer `exception.MarkLogged`. The writer marks the nearest `AlreadyLogged` implementer in the chain, while the two recovery helpers and the http kernel each read it off a concrete `*exception.Error` of their own — so the three agreed only when the marked link happened to be that type. A marked `HttpException`, and anything wrapping a marked error, read as unmarked and was rendered a second time: one failure, two records. A nil error and a typed nil carry no mark and are answered without being walked, because `errors.As` descends by calling `Unwrap` and every `Unwrap` in the package reads a field off its receiver
- container: `Scope.RegisterScoped` refuses a closed container, which is the answer the other two registration doors already gave. A registration made on a live scope of a container that had closed reported success for a service whose every resolution the creation guard then refuses — the same silent success the plain door was fixed for. The flag is read under the read lock the block already takes, so nothing is held across the scope's lock and the documented container-then-scope ordering is untouched
- container: `FromResolver` and `FromResolverByType` refuse a nil resolver with an error naming the service instead of dereferencing it. The dereference raised the runtime's own nil-pointer panic, which is not an error value, so the recovery helpers filed it as an opaque panic and no context named the resolver that was never wired; `Lazy` defers the call, so the crash also surfaced far from the wiring line that caused it
- logging: `NewDefaultLoggerWithLabels` copies the labels on the way in, which is the convention the json logger and the logging configuration already applied at their own doors and this one did not. The map is read lock-free on every `Log` call while the caller keeps its reference, so a later write against those reads is a fatal concurrent map access no recover reaches
- logging: a typed-nil logging configuration registered under the module name is refused where it is registered. It is a non-nil interface, so the plain nil guard missed it and the type assertion below accepted it — a nil pointer satisfies the interface — and the boot then died inside the logger provider naming `service.logger`, instead of naming the configuration that was registered wrong
- exception: `AsHttpException` refuses a typed nil with the plain one, the guard every other public entry of the package already carried. `errors.As` walks the chain by calling `Unwrap`, and every `Unwrap` in the package reads a field off its receiver, so a typed nil handed in dereferenced the nil it holds inside the search itself
- exception: `ExitError.ExitCode` and `ExitError.ErrorValue` answer a nil receiver instead of dereferencing it, as `Error` and `Unwrap` already did. `errors.As` matches the type on a typed-nil link and reports success with a nil pointer, and the callers that read the code straight off that result — the cli aggregator among them — dereferenced it on the line that decides how the process ends. Nothing can have meant a code of nil, so the out-of-range `0` is what a caller reads back and the exit paths treat it as the anomaly it is
- event: the error a listener panicked with travels as the cause of the dispatcher's wrapper, and the record is written through the renderer that walks the cause chain. The panic value was kept only in a context slot, where the json logger stringifies an error to its bare message — so the context map and the cause chain of the very error the listener panicked with reached no record at all: the reason a cache write failed was gone while its stack survived, and the wrapper, marked logged, suppressed every second chance downstream. The recovery helpers beside the http kernel already handled the identical shape by passing the recovered value as the cause; the dispatcher now does the same, marking the wrapper only after the record is written, and a typed-nil panic value reads as the no-cause it means
- container: a typed-nil error from a resolver implemented outside the package reads as the success it means, on both the name and the type path. Read as a failure, `FromResolver` walked it into `errors.As` — whose `Unwrap` call on a nil receiver panics — or wrapped it in "service not registered" for a service that is; on the type path it passed raw to `exception.FromError`, which reads a typed nil as nil, and `exception.Panic(nil)` then replaced the whole failure with its own "panic called with nil error" — a diagnostic naming only the reporting machinery. The container's own providers have had this normalization at their gate since the resolver hardening; the boundary that admits foreign implementations now applies the same rule
- clock: `ClockMustFromContainer` and `ClockMustFromResolver` refuse a nil or typed-nil argument by name. A nil container dereferenced inside the container package, so the panic blamed the container instead of the argument the helper was handed
- config: a runtime parameter is named inside the cause of its conversion errors, not only in the outer context. `Float()` and `Duration()` passed the environment key to the shared parsers, and a runtime parameter has none — so the cause carried an empty `parameterName` one line under an outer context that names the parameter, and an operator reading the cause chain concluded the parameter was anonymous. The identity the parsers stamp is now the environment key where one exists and the registration name otherwise
- internal: the three `float64` refusals of `Int` carry three distinct causes — not finite, not an integral number, outside the int64 range — and the refused scalar value travels in the error context. Collapsed into one "parameter is not a 'int'" they blamed the type, which was never the problem: the same branch accepts `2.0`, and because the value was not a string the record contained no number at all, so the operator could not tell whether to change the encoding, the value or the magnitude. The context now carries the refused value for the scalar shapes — strings as before, and the numeric kinds an operator needs to see; on the config path a secret parameter withholds the whole cause from the log, so nothing new leaks there
- logging: a typed nil no longer panics the error-reporting path anywhere in the package. `LogError` dereferenced one in three places — an error that is itself a typed nil reached `Error()`, a typed-nil `*exception.Error` found in the chain by `errors.As` reached `Level()`, and a typed-nil logger passed the plain nil comparison and dereferenced the nil receiver on the first method call; the json logger's context normalization called `Error()` on a typed-nil value stored under a context key; the cause enrichment routed a typed-nil cause into the one branch that renders the cause directly — the only input that ever reached that branch, so it panicked every time; and both recover helpers dereferenced a typed-nil `*exception.Error` or `*exception.ExitError` panic value inside the handler that must not panic. Each site now reads the nil the producer meant: the entry returns, the chain link contributes nothing, the logger falls back to the process logger, the context value renders as null, the cause is absent, and the panic value is logged as the plain panic payload it is
- logging: the exit handler shields the two steps between the recovery and the exit. `LogOnRecoverAndExitAfter` ran the logging step and the before-exit hook unguarded, and the hook is the container teardown — code that runs arbitrary service closes at the exact moment something is already wrong — so a panic there replaced the deliberate exit code with the Go runtime's code 2 and skipped both the stderr echo and `os.Exit` itself. Each step now runs under its own recover: a failing step costs one stderr line naming it, and the resolved exit code, the echo behind it and the exit itself survive
- logging: `LogOnRecover` logs the exit wrapper that carries no error value as the anomaly it is. The logging step was conditional on the wrapper carrying an error, so the zero-value `*exception.ExitError` — constructible outside the constructor that refuses nil — produced no record at all from the helper whose purpose is the record, while `resolveRecoveredExit` names the same anomaly on the exit path; the two halves of the file now agree
- logging: an error logged by `LogOnRecover` is marked as logged whether or not it panics again. The mark sat inside the re-panic branch, so with `panicAgain` false the error was logged and left unmarked, and any surviving reference — a creation failure the container memoizes and shares by design — was logged a second time downstream
- logging: the recover helpers capture the stack of the panic still in flight. Every other recovery boundary in the framework records `debug.Stack()` and these two did not, so a runtime panic — the nil dereference whose only diagnostic is its frames — logged through `LogOnRecover` or the exit handler recorded the symptom with no way to locate the site; the deferred handler runs with the panicking frames intact, which is the only moment they are still capturable. The stack travels as `panicStack` in the constructed error's context, the key the http kernel already uses
- logging: the json logger's marshal fallback keeps the context as text. One unmarshalable value — a channel, a function, a NaN — used to cost every other key of the record: the fallback carried only the message, the level, the time and the marshal error, so the service name and the cause chain vanished exactly when the record described a failure. The whole normalized context now travels as one rendered string beside the marshal error, and a fallback whose own encoding produced nothing writes nothing instead of a bare newline
- logging: errors nested inside context values render as their messages. The normalization converted only top-level values, so an error one level down — inside a provider map carried by the cause context chain, or in a slice — reached the encoder unconverted and marshaled as `{}`, every field unexported and no marshaler, with no error to say the diagnostic was lost; the normalization now descends the map and slice shapes the package itself produces, bounded in depth, and a typed nil among them renders as null
- logging: `jsonLogger.Closed` answers without taking the write lock. The probe is asked by the process-boundary exit handler before it trusts the logger with the final record, and the answer was serialized behind an in-flight `Write` — so a writer stalled on a full pipe hung the one handler that must reach `os.Exit`, in exactly the scenario the probe exists for. The flag is atomic now; the writes to it still happen under the write lock
- logging: `NewRequestLogger`'s decorator forwards `Closed()` to the logger it wraps. The exit handler treats a logger that cannot answer the question as live, so the wrapper hid a dead file-backed base behind a live-looking face and the final record was handed to it and silently dropped; `Close` is deliberately still not forwarded, because the wrapper lives in a request scope and does not own the shared writer
- logging: `LoggerFromRuntime` records the resolution it cannot serve. A resolution that produced nothing without an error returned nil silently — and every caller's nil check then skips its own record too, so the failure vanished twice; both shapes now leave one emergency record naming the service

- httpclient: a url carried into an error no longer carries the secret in it. The cause of a failed exchange is net/http's own `*url.Error`, whose text quotes the whole request url — query string included — and the cause chain is rendered into the log record, so the most ordinary failure there is, a refused connection, wrote out a token passed through `WithQuery`; a url net/url refused to parse leaked the same way, plus the password spelled in the userinfo, which nothing in net/http redacts. Every url this package puts into a message or an error context is sanitized — userinfo and query values replaced, scheme, host, path and parameter names kept — and net/http's error travels as its inner reason instead of its url-quoting wrapper. The package already stripped `Referer` on a cross-origin redirect for exactly this reason
- httpclient: a `[]byte` request body is copied instead of aliased. net/http writes the request body on its own goroutine and `Do` returns as soon as the response headers arrive, so against a server that answers without draining the body — an early 4xx, a redirect, a validating endpoint — the transport was still reading the caller's slice after the call had returned: a pooled buffer reused right after the request is a data race, reported by the race detector, and torn bytes on the wire. The json path was never affected, because `json.Marshal` produces a slice of its own
- httpclient: a typed-nil basic credential reads as the nil it means. `SetBasic` accepts any implementation of the contract, so a nil `*BasicAuthorizationOptions` boxed through it passed the interface nil check and reading the username off it dereferenced nil on the request path, where the promise is an error rather than a panic; both the authorization and the basic credential are now judged through `IsNilInterface`
- httpclient: the response body cap is validated before the request is sent. It was read after the exchange, so a `Post` carrying a non-positive cap — a value computed from a remaining quota that reached zero — reached the server, committed its side effect, and then answered with an error phrased as though nothing had been sent; a caller retrying on it duplicated the operation. The cap is caller input known before anything is dialled
- httpclient: `WithMaxResponseBodyBytes` is enforced on the streaming path. `RequestStream` read the same options and never consulted the cap, so a caller who asked for ten bytes was handed everything the server sent, with nothing to say the guard did not exist there. A cap the caller named is applied to the stream; one they did not name is not, because the default is sized for a body held whole in memory, which is exactly what a stream is not
- httpclient: an uppercase url scheme names an absolute url. The absolute-url test compared the prefix byte for byte while `isSameOrigin` one file over compares schemes with `EqualFold`, so `HTTP://other.example/path` — a spelling a url harvested from a document or a `Location` header legitimately carries — was treated as a path and hung under the base url, sending the request to the base host with the absolute url as a path segment
- httpclient: `StreamResponse.Body` answers with a failing reader rather than a nil one after `Close`. Close nils the body, so the ordinary consumption shape — `io.Copy(destination, streamResponse.Body())` — dereferenced nil and took the process down when a watchdog closed the stream while the consumer was deciding to read, which is the whole reason `Close` is reachable from another goroutine. The reader reports that the stream is closed and its own `Close` still succeeds, so a consumer's deferred close stays correct
- httpclient: `HttpClient.Close` releases the idle connections the client is holding, and `RequestStreamWithContext` bounds a stream from the outside. Every client owns a transport with a hundred-connection idle pool kept for ninety seconds, and dropping the last reference to the client released none of it, because each parked connection has a read loop keeping the transport reachable — the documentation's own example built a client per call, so a service calling it ten times a second held hundreds of sockets nothing would reuse. The streaming path carries no whole-request deadline by design, so a stream a server never ends was bounded by nothing the caller held; a context is the remedy, and the close obligation the caller has always carried is now stated in the contract, on the method, and in the documentation
- httpclient: the errors of a failed exchange say which request failed, on the paths that know it. `failed to read response body`, `response body exceeded max size` and the cap refusal carried no method, no url and no status — in a service calling many endpoints the record could not say which request died. `unsupported body type` stays as it was: it is raised while the body is being encoded, before any request exists to name, so it carries the type it was handed and nothing else

- exception: the error utilities tolerate the typed nil they exist to describe. `LogContext` called `err.Error()` before any guard could see that the non-nil interface held a nil pointer, so the recovery reporting a failure died on the line that logs it, replacing the diagnostic with a bare nil dereference; `FromError`, `FromErrorWithLevel` and `FromErrorWithLevelAndContext` dereferenced a typed-nil `ContextProvider` that `errors.As` had matched in the chain; `MarkLogged`'s own nil guard compared an interface produced by a successful assertion — which is never nil — so the guard could not fire and `MarkAsLogged` wrote through the nil receiver; and both cause-chain builders panicked on a typed-nil link, one calling `Error()` through it and the other handing it to `errors.Unwrap`. A typed nil now reads as the nil it means at every entry, a typed-nil link ends both chains at the same place, and the walks stay aligned link for link
- exception: the mutable error state is locked, because the single-threaded-per-request premise does not survive the sharing the framework itself introduces. The container hands every waiter of a failed service creation a wrapper whose cause is the same live `*Error` the owner's resolution then writes in place — `SetContextValue("serviceName", …)` against the waiter's `Context()` iteration is a concurrent map write against a map iteration, a fatal runtime error no recover reaches, reproduced under the race detector before the fix — and `Container.Close` returns one memoized error to every concurrent caller. `Error` and `HttpException` now guard their context map and already-logged mark with an internal mutex; the immutable fields stay lock-free and no signature changes
- exception: `MarkLogged` marks at the depth `logging.LogError` reads. The mark was written on the top value alone while the reader searched the chain, so marking a wrapped error was a silent no-op and the one failure produced two records — the exact double logging the resolver's in-place context write exists to prevent. **Behavioural change**: a mark on a plain wrapper now lands on the markable error inside it
- exception: `LogContext` anchors the cause walk on the top error's own wrap link instead of the nearest `*Error` a deep search found. Anchored deep, the walk skipped every link above the found error and dropped that error's own context from the record entirely whenever it was not the top — an `HttpException` wrapping an `*Error` logged neither the inner message in the chain nor the inner context anywhere. The chain now starts one link below the top error, `BuildCauseContextChain` reads each link through the `ContextProvider` contract so an http exception — or any userland error carrying a context — contributes its context instead of a silent nil, and every extra context map passed to `LogContext` is merged in order where everything past the first was silently dropped
- exception: the zero values the constructors cannot reach are safe to touch. `&exception.Error{}` and `&exception.HttpException{}` compile with a nil context map, so the first `SetContextValue` panicked on the assignment — including the container's own in-place write against a user-built error found in a resolution chain; the write now allocates the map. `(&exception.ExitError{}).Error()` dereferenced its nil error field at the exact moment the process boundary prints why it is exiting, and its `Unwrap` boxed the nil field into a typed nil for every walker downstream; the one names the anomaly, the other returns an untyped nil
- exception: `IsHttpException` answers whether `AsHttpException` finds a usable exception, so the pair cannot disagree on a typed nil: `Is` answered true while `As` answered nil, and a caller that trusted `Is` and then dereferenced panicked on the disagreement. `AsHttpException` also stops returning the typed nil itself when that is what the chain held
- exception: `NewExitError` refuses an exit code outside `[1, 255]`. `os.Exit` hands the code to the operating system, which keeps its low 8 bits: 256 reported success from a dying process, a negative read as 255, and 0 contradicted the error the constructor already requires. **Behavioural change**: the refusal is a construction-time panic naming the code
- exception: `NewHttpException` and `NewHttpExceptionWithCause` refuse a status code outside `[100, 599]`. `net/http`'s `WriteHeader` panics below 100 and above 999 deep in the response path, one response write away from the mistake, and a status the writer clamps to 200 served an exception as success. **Behavioural change**: the refusal is a construction-time panic naming the status
- exception: `ValidationFailed` puts its detail under the `errors` context key — the one the kernel exception listener copies into the json payload — instead of `validationErrors`, a key nothing on the response path reads, so the detail the caller attached reached no one. **Behavioural change**: the context key changes and a client of an endpoint using the helper starts receiving the detail in the 422 payload
- debug: the error-context walk descends into a defined map or slice type whose underlying shape it already handles, so the framework's own `exceptioncontract.Context` nested inside a context no longer rides past all three guards at once. Unconverted, a self-holding nested context survived into `json.Marshal`, whose cycle error routed it to the `fmt` fallback that has no cycle detection of its own — a fatal stack overflow rendering a debug page, the exact death the cycle guard was built against, only one named type away from the shape its tests proved; a deep chain of named maps recursed inside the encoder past the depth bound, and a redacted key inside a named map reached the fallbacks in the clear. The conversion keeps the backing pointer, so the cycle keying is unchanged
- debug: `debug:container` reports why a build failed, not only that it did. The message of a melody error is its message alone — the dial refusal, the missing file, the refused credential all live below it — so the one detail the operator runs the command to learn reached neither the table nor the json; the causes now render as `caused by` lines in both table views, as `errorCauseChain` in the json items, and on the envelope error's cause details
- debug: the error context of a failing service is read through the `ContextProvider` contract rather than the concrete `*exception.Error`, so an `HttpException` — or any userland error carrying a context — in the resolution chain contributes its context to the report instead of nothing
- debug: the table-cell truncation stays out of the json document. `errorContextJson` was cut at the 220-byte display width on both formats, handing a machine consumer an unparseable fragment with no sign anything was dropped; the json envelope now carries the full context and the table keeps its verbosity-driven truncation
- debug: the `debug:container` list summary puts the shown count before the ok/error split, so the split reads as scoped to the window it was computed over: only the windowed services are resolved, and an unqualified `8 ok | 2 error` beside a larger total implied the rest were neither instead of unprobed
- debug: `debug:events` counts distinct subscribers across the dispatcher in its summary. Summing the per-event distinct counts reported one subscriber listening on three events as three subscribers — a number nothing in the process matches; the per-event column is unchanged
- debug: `debug:events --format=json --verbose` carries the listener detail — priority, source, owner and the required and may-skip marks that say whether the fail-closed dispatch guarantee is armed. The detail existed only in the table format, so a machine consumer could never learn it at any verbosity. **Behavioural change**: under `--verbose` the json `data` becomes `{"events": …, "listeners": […]}`; without the flag the shape is unchanged
- debug: `NewMiddlewareCommand` refuses a nil provider at construction and the zero-value command returns a named refusal through the report — every value the provider returns was guarded while the provider itself was not, so a wiring mistake surfaced as a bare nil-function call far from where it was made
- debug: a secret parameter whose value is nil renders as `(empty)` instead of the mask. `fmt` renders nil as a non-empty placeholder, so the redaction reported a value being present — the opposite of the one answer the secret column exists to give
- event: the reason a listener failed reaches the log. The record the dispatcher wrote carried the listener's identity, type, priority and duration but never the error itself, and the wrapper it returned was marked as already logged — which suppressed the caller's own record, the http kernel's, the one that renders the cause and the cause chain. A `kernel.request` listener failing on a database that is down answered 500 and left the words "the database is down" in no log at all. The failure now travels unmarked with its cause and its context, so the caller writes one complete record
- event: a typed-nil error returned by a listener reads as the success it means. A listener handing back a nil pointer of its own error type returns a non-nil interface, which aborted the dispatch, skipped every listener behind it and failed the request closed for a listener that had reported success
- event: `Dispatch` refuses a typed-nil event with the error it promises. The guard compared the interface against nil literally, so the typed nil passed it and dereferenced on `Name()`; the recovery that exists to describe such a panic then dereferenced the same value while building its context, and that second panic escaped in place of the diagnostic — leaving the caller a bare memory address for a mistake the package names precisely one file over. Both sites read through the interface, as `NewEventFromEvent` already did
- event: `AddSubscriber` refuses a typed-nil subscriber before calling into it. `SubscribedEvents` was invoked ahead of the identity guard that names the mistake, so a subscriber whose method reads its receiver raised a bare nil dereference inside application code at boot, while `RemoveSubscriber` — given the same value — produced the precise message. `NewEventDispatcher` and `NewEventDispatcherAdapter` read their arguments through the interface for the same reason: a typed-nil clock surfaced only at the first dispatch, without the wiring site that caused it
- event: `EventDispatcherAdapter.RemoveListener` scrubs its own bookkeeping whether or not the wrapped dispatcher still held the listener. Returning early when the wrapped dispatcher answered false left the adapter holding a record of a listener that no longer exists — reported by `RegisteredEvents` forever and removable by nothing, since every retry took the same early return; `RemoveSubscriber` compounded it by discarding the subscriber's registration list first, so the orphan lost its last route to removal
- event: `NewEventFromEvent` carries the stopped propagation. The copy took the name, payload and timestamp and dropped the flag, so a listener reached through the adapter — which hands each listener a copy — was told the propagation was live on an event that had been stopped
- event: a listener panic whose value reports itself already logged is not logged a second time. A listener calling a helper that logs an error, marks it and panics it had that one failure recorded twice
- event: `EventDispatcherAdapter` reports the execution order the dispatch actually uses. It kept a registration index of its own and broke ties between equal priorities by it, while the dispatch breaks them by listener id, so two concurrent registrations that received the two counters in opposite orders left the inspector reporting an order the dispatch never took — permanently, for as long as both listeners stayed registered. The separate index is gone and `RegisteredEvents` sorts on the listener id, the one value both sides agree on

- cli: the finish banner tells the truth about a command that panicked. The banner defer read an error the panic path never assigns, so the unwinding printed `[finished] [success] [duration=...]` for a command that died — followed by the process exiting non-zero; the panic now marks the banner `[failed]` and is re-raised unchanged, an `*exception.ExitError` keeping its exit code, while the closes stay with the outer layers whose recover handler needs the container open to write the fatal record through the file logger
- cli: a typed-nil error reads as the success it means. A command returning its error through a concrete typed pointer — and a substituted runtime whose `Close` does the same — handed over a non-nil interface around a nil value, which was read as a failure and reached `Error()` on a nil receiver on the printing line: a masked panic on the exit path in place of the success the command reported
- cli: the action no longer re-reports the close failure of a container the command closed itself. A repeated `Close` answers the first teardown's memoized error, so a command that closed the container and folded the failure into its own result had that one failure presented again as a fresh shutdown incident; the action no longer closes the container at all: the scope stays its to close and its failure its to report, while the container's teardown belongs to the recover handler that owns the process exit, which closes it between the final record and the exit
- cli: the banners honour `--no-color`. The start and finish banners, the red error line and the command-not-found header wrote their ansi sequences unconditionally, so a run redirected into a file carried escape codes around an output that honoured the flag — the suggestion header even printed them beside a table rendered under the no-color option it itself had set
- cli: a printing failure no longer replaces the failure the envelope carried. `Render` returned the print error alone, so an envelope whose `Data` does not serialize lost the business failure that existed nowhere else — the record said "json: unsupported type" and nothing else; the envelope's exit-coded failure now travels as the cause beside the named print failure
- cli: `output.NewMeta` honours the caller's Go version the way it honours the application and melody versions — the field was the one of the three silently discarded — and `NormalizeOption` clamps a negative verbosity level like its integer siblings
- config: a broken `.env` no longer prints its neighbours' credentials. The godotenv parse error quotes the file content it choked on — the whole remaining tail for a malformed variable name, so one broken key on line three put `DB_PASSWORD` from line seven into the emergency log, preprocessing markers included — and that error rode the cause chain the logger renders in full; the failure now travels as a content-free description beside the path of the file to open
- config: a template referencing a non-string parameter reports the type, never the value — the raw `environmentValue` sat in the error context one line under the comment forbidding exactly that, and a signing key registered as bytes is precisely what a template would reference; the typed accessors additionally withhold the value-quoting strconv cause for a parameter marked secret, keeping it for ordinary parameters whose mistyped pool size deserves the diagnostic
- config: a runtime registration whose template fails to resolve leaves nothing behind. The parameter was published into the map before the resolution that could panic, so a recovered failure left the raw `%env(...)%` served as the value to every later reader and the name burnt — the corrected retry answered "duplicate parameter name" forever. The publication still comes first — the resolution's secret propagation marks the reader it finds in that map by name, so a parameter resolved before it was published was absent at the only moment the propagation looks — and a failed resolution now removes the name again before the panic. Nothing observes the intermediate state: publish, resolution and rollback all run under the write lock every reader takes
- config: a runtime parameter is named in its conversion errors. Registered without an environment key, it was identified in every error by its empty `environmentKey` — "cannot convert" named nothing an operator could find; the registration name now travels in the parameter and its diagnostics
- config: the serving refusal in `Resolve` is airtight. The flag was read before the write lock was taken, so a `MarkServing` racing the call could land between the check and the store rewrite the refusal exists to prevent; both sides now meet under the configuration write lock, and the comment that claimed consumers read parameter values without a lock — contradicted by the parameter's own `valueMutex` — says the true thing
- config: the error for a reference `MELODY_ENV` cannot resolve names its window. The environment name selects which `.env.<name>` files load next, so a reference in it can only read `.env` and `.env.local` — and the old "undefined key" reported a key the operator could see defined in `.env.prod`, with advice about escaping dollars that had nothing to do with it
- config: `loadRequiredDotEnvFile` is deleted — nothing called it on this major; every load path is optional and the missing-files condition is handled by the boot warning and the http refusal
- application: the embedded-environment guard reads through the interface — a typed-nil `fs.FS` passed the plain nil comparison and died later as an anonymous nil dereference inside `fs.Stat`, instead of the refusal that names the argument
- application: a configuration that fails to build is reported as what it is. `NewApplication` wrapped every `NewConfiguration` failure as "could not resolve the config parameters" — doubled for a genuine resolve failure, which already carries that exact message, and false for a validation failure, sending the diagnosis to the placeholders for an http port that was simply out of range
- cache: `Remember` refuses a typed-nil cache through the interface. The guard was a plain nil comparison while the package's constructors already read through the interface, so a `(*Manager)(nil)` boxed into the contract passed it and the first method call dereferenced the nil receiver — a panic on the request path, in place of the exact error the guard exists to return
- cache: a typed-nil error from third-party code reads as the success it means. A callback declared with a concrete error type — or a backend or serializer of the application's own — hands back a nil pointer boxed into a non-nil interface; the manager treated a healthy operation as failed on every call, and on the single-flight path the boxed nil was memoized as the flight's failure, handed to every waiter, and panicked the first one that rendered it. Every third-party boundary in the package now normalizes through the interface
- cache: the panic a `Remember` leader recovers is attributed to the side that panicked. The callback's own panics are already recovered inside the safe wrapper, so what the leader's recover catches is the cache side — a backend or serializer that panicked in Get or Set — yet the fabricated error said `callback panicked`, sending the diagnosis after a callback that never misbehaved; it now says the cache access panicked
- cache: a serialization failure names the key it happened under. `Set` and `SetMultiple` surfaced the serializer's bare encoding error — with dozens of dynamically built values, nothing located which one refused to encode; the error now carries the key in its context, the way the counter path always has
- application: the record that explains a dying process is written through a logger that still writes, before the teardown instead of after it. `Run` deferred `Close` after the exit handler, so on any panic the container teardown ran first: it closed the file-backed json logger — the default `MELODY_LOG_PATH` is a file — and a closed logger silently drops every write, so the handler resolved the dead instance from the closed container (reads deliberately survive Close), "logged" the failure into it, marked it logged, and exited; the stack, context and cause chain of every fatal cli failure survived only as a one-line stderr echo. One handler now owns both duties in the only workable order — the record first, through `logging.LogOnRecoverAndExitAfter`'s before-exit hook the teardown runs in — and the exit logger refuses a container logger that reports itself closed, falling back to the emergency logger, which covers the cli path where the command's own action closed the container before `Run` could see the error at all
- application: a container-close failure in cli mode is reported once. The container memoizes its close error, so the failure the cli action discovered and folded into the command's own result was re-received by the application's deferred `Close` and emergency-logged a second time, presenting one incident as two at two severities; `Close` now asks the container whether somebody already closed it before treating the memoized answer as its own discovery
- application: a serve failure racing the shutdown signal is reported instead of discarded. When the listen failed in the same instant the context was cancelled, the select's choice of branch was arbitrary, and the shutdown branch returned nil with the real failure still sitting in the channel — a process that never served a byte reported a clean shutdown and exited 0. The shutdown branch now reads the serve error too; `Shutdown` closes the listeners before returning, so the receive is bounded
- application: an http server failure is logged once. `runHttp` wrote it to the log and returned it raw, the caller wrapped it into the process-ending panic, and the exit handler rendered the same failure a second time; the error now leaves `runHttp` marked as logged
- application: a cli command's name is judged trimmed at registration, because that is the spelling it is dispatched under. A name with padding passed the empty check and the duplicate check raw while the cli registration trims: a padded duplicate panicked at every dispatch instead of joining the aggregated boot report, and a command whose `Name()` carried a stray space registered under a spelling no argv can produce — the suggestion table blocked every invocation of a command that exists, with the suggestion naming the very command being asked for
- application: the go run detection matches a whole path segment. It looked for the substring `/go-build`, which also matched an installation path like `/opt/go-builder/bin`, silently redirecting all configuration discovery from the executable's directory to the working directory; only a segment named `go-build` followed by nothing but digits — the go tool's temporary build directories — counts now
- application: the process-boundary exit handler survives an application assembled without `NewApplication`. It dereferenced the kernel to reach the container logger, so the one handler that must not panic panicked on the nil kernel and replaced the original failure with its own stack; it answers with the emergency logger instead
- logging: `LogOnRecoverAndExit` survives an exit error carrying no error value. `exception.ExitError` is an exported struct, so its zero value is constructible around the constructor that refuses nil, and the handler dereferenced the nil error inside the exit path — the sibling `LogOnRecover` had the guard; the anomaly is now logged as its own error and the carried exit code is honored
- runtime: the nil guards read through the interface. A typed-nil scope or container passed `runtime.New`'s plain nil comparison and every resolution then panicked on the request path; a custom `Runtime` whose `Scope()` yields a typed nil had its healthy container silently bypassed, turning the promised may-not-be-nil error into a panic inside the resolution. Construction refuses typed nils where they can be named, and the resolver selection falls past a typed-nil scope to the container

- container: a scoped service finishing after its scope closed is closed best-effort instead of leaking. The store refuses the write — the scope's maps are gone — and the freshly built value was dropped on the floor: an error for the caller, a silent leak for the transaction or connection the provider had opened, on every handler-spawned goroutine that outlives its request. The container-close race one branch above has always closed the value it discards; the scope-side twin now does the same
- container: a scope's teardown collapses the name and type filings of one instance into one node before it orders anything. A scoped service resolved by type is filed under both keys, the dependency edge targets the name node, and the type alias — carrying no edges, sorting ahead of every `scope:service:` key — closed the shared instance in the first heap wave, underneath its still-open dependents: the transaction closed before the repository holding it, deterministically. The same missing collapse closed a dual-filed VALUE-typed service with an uncomparable field twice, once per node. The alias link is recorded at the moment of the dual filing and the edges of both nodes are merged onto the survivor
- container: a scoped dependency the scope already holds is depended on as hard as one this resolution builds. The early answer for an already-created instance returned before the dependency edge was recorded, so the close-order guarantee held only for whichever resolution came first and the teardown fell back to the name lottery — resolve the transaction before the repository and it closed first, resolve them in the other order and it closed last
- container: a provider panicking with a typed-nil error no longer takes the process down. The recovery's error assertion accepts the non-nil interface, and rendering its context called `Error()` on the nil receiver — a second panic inside the recovery handler, running with the container mutex unlocked, which escaped through the caller's deferred unlock as a fatal unlock-of-unlocked-mutex with every waiter on that creation parked forever. The typed nil is normalized away and the context rendering is contained on its own, so an error whose `Error()` panics costs its context and nothing else
- container: a provider declared with a concrete error type can succeed. Its nil `*MyErr` boxes into a NON-nil error interface, so a healthy service failed every resolution — and the typed-nil cause it carried was a delayed panic for the first `Error()` walk at log time. A typed-nil second return value now reads as the "no error" the provider meant
- container: the owner of a finished creation drops its waiters' wait-graph edges under the lock that wakes them. A woken waiter clears its own edge only after re-acquiring the mutex, and in that window the stale edge read as a cycle to any resolution the owner ran next — a spurious "circular service dependency detected across concurrent resolutions" between two resolutions that shared nothing but the lock they were queued on, a transient 500 during warm-up that no retry could reproduce
- container: an override installed while the provider ran wins the slot. The creation blindly overwrote it by name while the type-keyed map kept the override, so an installation its caller was told succeeded was half revoked and name and type answered differently for the rest of the process; the racing resolution now yields the override and closes the value it built
- container: an override replacing an instance the container itself built no longer leaks it. The replaced value left the only maps the close sweep reads with nothing else holding it; it now waits in a graveyard the teardown closes — once, after the ordered walk, under the identity marks that protect an instance a provider handed to several names. An evicted override stays its installer's, exactly as before; the scope-side `ClosedWithScope` eviction gets the same graveyard
- container: a close that both fails and cycles reports the cycle's nodes. The node list survived only in the failure-free branch, so the one teardown that needed the full diagnosis — which services failed AND which ones cycled — got half of it; the scope's cycle report now names its nodes too
- container: a melody error passes back through `FromResolver` whole, with the service name added to its context in place. The rebuild from message, context and cause produced a lookalike that had shed the log level, the already-logged mark, the capture stack and every wrapper above the found error — a refusal already logged at its source escalated to Error level and logged again at the kernel boundary
- validation: a shared, non-cyclic pointer is validated under every path that reaches it. The cycle detector's visited set spanned the whole call rather than the current descent path, so the same `*Item` referenced by two sibling fields was walked under the first and silently skipped under the second — every constraint beneath the second occurrence unenforced, which on a server-built payload meant a full pass over values never looked at. An entry is now removed when its subtree returns, so a genuine cycle is still cut by the ancestor that is on the path while a DAG is walked whole
- validation: a custom constraint that returns a typed nil error is a success, not a panic. A constraint written with a concrete `*ValidationError` variable returns a non-nil interface holding nil on its success path; the plain nil comparison passed it through and the first method call dereferenced it, panicking inside `Validate` on the request path. The result is now read through `internal.IsNilInterface`, the same guard the registration path already applies to the constraint itself
- validation: the time-codec verdict settles on one value per type. The memo was written with `Store`, and this is the memo whose producer runs application code — the decode probe calls the type's own `UnmarshalJSON` — so two goroutines computing opposite verdicts for the same type each got their own answer and the frozen one was decided by store ordering. The first stored verdict now wins for everyone
- validation: `ValidationError.ToExceptionError` hands the exception a copy of the context. The constructor copies on the way in and `Context()` copies on the way out precisely to keep the map private, but `ToExceptionError` attached the live map, so a consumer mutating the exception's context mutated the validation error through it
- validation: a refused rule parameter names its reason. `WithParams`' error was dropped on the floor, so a rejected parameter value, a malformed tag and a constraint that takes no parameters all collapsed into one generic `invalidRuleSyntax` with nothing to tell them apart — and the refusal is memoized, so no later request could ever surface it either. The reason now travels as `cause` in the error context, a tag-syntax error names the comma-separated `part` the parser refused, and the internal constraint lookup fails closed on a name the registry cannot answer instead of handing `Validate` a nil interface
- http: `BindJson` keeps the real error as the cause. A body that failed to read and a body that failed to parse both surfaced as a bare message, so the decoder's own diagnosis — offending offset, field, type — never reached the log, and a read failure could not be told apart from malformed input; the responses are unchanged

- session: a session deleted while a request was still running cannot be written back. `Storage.Save` is a blind upsert, so a request that loaded the session before a logout deleted it re-created the entry when its own handler finished — with the identity intact and the cookie re-issued — and the window is as long as a request takes, which is long enough for someone holding a stolen cookie to keep a revoked session alive by repeating a slow request. The manager now remembers a deleted id for `session.TombstoneRetention` and refuses a save under it, carrying `session.ErrSessionDeleted` as the cause; a rotated-away id is buried the same way, so the id a rotation retires cannot be re-created either. The record is bounded by the window rather than by how many sessions were ever deleted: each burial prunes what has outlived it. **Behavioural change**: `SaveSession` now returns an error for a session another request deleted, where it previously re-created it
- session: `Session.Clear` ends the session and the ending latches. `Set` lifted the cleared flag, so a logout handler that cleared the session followed by anything writing to the same object — a middleware or an event listener leaving a farewell message — had the response path take the save branch instead of the delete branch: the values were overwritten, but the pre-logout id stayed alive in the storage and was re-issued to the browser under the same cookie. A caller that wants a usable session after clearing one asks the manager for a new session. The latch that rotation already applied is now what `Clear` itself does, so the unexported `abandon` is gone. **Behavioural change**: a write after `Clear` no longer revives the session
- session: `NewManager` does not close the storage it was handed; `NewManagerOwningStorage` is the constructor for a caller that built both by hand and wants one `Close` to end both. A storage registered as a service is closed by the container that created it, so a manager that also closed it closed it twice — harmless for the two storages melody ships, both idempotent, and reported as a failure by the second call of a storage wrapping a connection, which turned a clean shutdown into `failed to close container services`. The rule is the one `NewFileStorageFromFile` already follows for an injected file handle: what you were handed, you do not close. **Behavioural change**: `Manager.Close` no longer closes the storage unless the manager was built as its owner
- http: a session write lost to a storage outage answers 500 instead of the response the handler produced. The handler wrote to the session and returned success on the assumption the write would land — a login answering `302 /dashboard` with the identity never stored, or an attempt counter that never grows while the backend is down, which makes a session-based limit fail open in exactly the conditions an attack produces — and the client could not tell the difference from a line in the server log. The session cookie is still suppressed, so the browser is never pointed at an id nothing persisted, and the delete path is unchanged: a failed logout still expires the browser cookie and serves the handler's response, because clearing a cookie can only end a session and never resurrect one
- session: `Manager.SaveSession` refuses a typed nil session instead of dereferencing it. The guard compared against `nil` while `RegenerateSession` beside it used `internal.IsNilInterface`, and a nil pointer of a session type is not equal to nil once it is carried in the interface — the zero value of an unassigned `*session.Session` passed the check and the first method call on it panicked, replacing the error the caller was given a return value to act on. On the response path that panic happens inside the kernel's recovery defer, where recover has already run, so it escaped `ServeHttp` and the client was served nothing at all
- session: `Manager.SaveSession` holds the session id to the standard the load and delete paths hold it to. It refused only the empty string, so an id those two reject was stored under a key `Manager.Session` can never read back and `DeleteSession` can never remove: the save reported success over an entry that stayed in the storage for the rest of the process, and the later clear logged a delete failure the manager itself had manufactured
- session: `NewManager` refuses a negative ttl. Both storages test `0 < ttl` and store an entry with no expiry at all when it is false, so the value that asks for the shortest possible session produced the one that never ends — silently, and in the opposite direction from what it asked for. The configuration path already refused it; the constructor is the door a caller that wires the manager itself comes through. Zero keeps its meaning of no expiry. **Breaking**: a negative ttl now fails at construction
- session: `Session.All` returns a copy that reaches all the way down, the depth both storages already copy at. A copy of only the top level handed back the very map or slice a nested value held, so a caller mutating it changed the live session without passing through `Set` — the session was not marked modified and the change was never persisted — and once the response path had handed the same value to the storage, a caller still holding it raced the copy the storage makes, which is a concurrent map iteration and write the runtime ends the process on rather than a panic any recover absorbs
- session: `InMemoryStorage` refuses `Load`, `Save`, `Delete` and `Clear` after `Close`, the way `FileStorage` already did. It had no closed state at all, so the operations kept succeeding against a store whose cleanup goroutine had stopped: an entry saved after close was reclaimed by nothing except a load that happened to name it, and the map grew for the rest of the process. The two storages the framework ships now answer the same way, so an application that swaps one for the other does not inherit a different failure. **Behavioural change**: these four operations returned success on a closed in-memory storage and now return an error
- session: the instant a session expires counts as lapsed in `InMemoryStorage`, the boundary `FileStorage` already drew with `now >= ExpiresAt`. One storage used `Before` and kept the session alive for that instant while the other dropped it, so the same lifetime described two different sessions depending on which storage was configured
- session: a load of a lapsed entry answers "no such session" even when the housekeeping flush that removes it cannot write. The answer was settled before the flush was attempted and the flush is work no caller asked for, but its error was returned in place of the answer — and `Manager.Session` turns any load error into a panic, so an expired cookie on a store that cannot be written answered 500 while a client holding no cookie at all was served a fresh session
- http: the kernel tests what a session manager hands back with `IsNilInterface` rather than against `nil`. The manager is a replaceable service, and an implementation that reports "not found" by returning a nil pointer of its own session type hands back an interface that is not equal to nil: the kernel took it for a live session, skipped minting one, and published it on the request, so the first handler to touch the session dereferenced nil
- http: `writeResponse` tests both the session and the session manager with `IsNilInterface` before persisting. The same typed nil that reached the request also reached the response path, where the dereference happens inside the recovery defer — the second panic on a request whose first one had already been recovered, which leaves `ServeHttp` with no response to write
- http: a session write dropped because the response was already committed is logged. Refusing the write is right — no `Set-Cookie` can reach a client on a committed response, and storing the session would leave a row nothing can ever reference — but a handler that streamed its response and then rotated the session reaches the same branch, and for it the rotation has already destroyed the previous entry while this drops the replacement: everything the session held is gone, from two calls that both reported success. The silence made that indistinguishable from the ordinary case the branch exists for, a first-time visitor on a stream with nothing worth storing
- http: the kernel refuses a urlencoded submission whose body could not be read instead of dispatching it as an empty form. The body is buffered before `ParseForm` drains it, and a read that failed — the request-body ceiling refusing an oversized submission, or a client abandoning the upload — was discarded: the form parser then failed on the poisoned reader, its failure was recorded as a warning, and the handler ran against a request whose form was simply empty. An oversized form was therefore processed as an empty one and answered with whatever the handler makes of a blank submission, while the json binding path answered the identical condition with 413. The read failure is now carried on the request and answered before the handler is reached — 413 when the ceiling stopped the read, 400 for a body the client broke — negotiated between html and json like every other kernel refusal
- http: the error handler an application installs runs under the kernel's own panic recovery. It is called while the failed handler's response is still open and held in nothing but a local variable, so a panic escaping it unwound past every close the kernel performs: a file-backed body leaked a descriptor until a finalizer ran, and a pipe-backed one leaked permanently along with the goroutine feeding it. All three call sites — the handler-error path, the not-found path and the recovery path itself, where the panic would have been a second one — now log the panic with the stack of its site and fall through to the default error response, which is the answer a panicking error handler could not produce
- http: the recovered-panic log record carries the stack of the panic site. The error value names the symptom and never the line that raised it, and because the kernel recovers, net/http's own stack print never fires either — a nil-pointer dereference in a handler or middleware left one log line from which the fault could not be located, while a panic in an event listener on the same request had its stack recorded. Every other recovery boundary in the framework already reported one
- http: a client that refuses gzip with an uppercase `Q=0` is not sent a gzip-encoded body. The quality parameter was matched case-sensitively, so the refusal was skipped and the coding kept its default weight of full acceptance — the parameter name is case-insensitive, and the accept-header parser beside it already normalised before the same comparison
- http: the cross-origin allow list treats the port as part of the origin. A scheme-less entry was compared against the origin's host with the port discarded, so `app.example.com` granted every port of that host — and `RestrictiveService`, which pairs its list with credentials, therefore handed credentialed read access to anything an attacker could get served from another port of an allowed host. An entry that means to allow a port now names it, and the wildcard forms match on the same terms
- http: an explicitly empty cross-origin allow list refuses every origin instead of becoming the wildcard. A list built from an environment variable that failed to arrive is empty rather than absent, and rewriting it to `*` turned "no origin is allowed" into "every origin is allowed" with no error, no log line and nothing to distinguish it from a deliberately permissive service. A nil list is still the absence of a preference and keeps the permissive default; an empty list paired with credentials fails the boot, since credentials with nothing to grant them to can only come from a list that failed to load
- http: the static file server consults its excluded-path list against the index file the mount root resolves to. The list was read with the spelling the client sent, and only afterwards did `/` resolve to the configured index file, so an application that excluded `/index.html` to serve that page through its own handler — a shell carrying a csrf token, or one behind authentication — had it answered off the disk by the outermost middleware for the one spelling a browser actually uses, cached under `Cache-Control: public` where the configuration enabled caching. The exclusion held for the path that was written and failed for the path that is requested
- http: `BindJson` binds a body under a request-body ceiling at the top of the int64 range. The one byte read past the limit — what tells an exactly-at-limit body from an oversized one — wrapped the reader's allowance negative, so every body read as empty and every valid json request was answered "empty request body"; the allowance now saturates
- http: the rate limiters' idle prune holds its threshold for a window past the midpoint of the duration range. Twice the window wrapped negative, every entry read as idle, and the cleanup emptied the map — so a limiter configured with a window long enough never to refill had its whole budget restored at every cleanup interval. Both the fixed-window and the sliding-window limiter shared the expression
- internal: the deep copy is linear in distinct nodes instead of exponential in depth on shared substructure. Bounded by depth alone, a value that reaches one node through two edges doubled the work at every level — a 28-level two-edge chain is 2^28 visits, and a session carrying one hung the request forever with the session lock held, while the depth guard, watching only depth, never fired. The traversal now memoizes visited nodes: a node reached twice is copied once and stays shared inside the copy, and a cycle closes onto its own copy instead of riding the recursion ten thousand levels down to be capped with an alias to the live original — an isolation breach exactly where isolation was promised. Slices are keyed by backing pointer and length, because two slices over one array with different lengths are different nodes. The copy still descends into maps and slices only — a pointer, or a struct or array holding one, is returned as-is, which remains the documented boundary of what session data may safely carry. A cyclic value in a json-backed session storage now surfaces as the encoder's cycle error instead of a ten-thousand-level document

- tooling: two behaviours nothing asserted are covered. `Run` tells the configuration the wiring phase is over before it dispatches, which is what turns a late `Resolve` into an error instead of a silent rewrite under services that already read their parameters — the `config` package tested what the marker does, nothing tested that `Run` sets it, and deleting the call left both `./application/...` and `./config/...` green. And the kernel response normalizer leaves a nil response alone rather than synthesizing a second empty 204 beside the kernel's own, which had no test file at all: putting the synthesis back left every package under `./http/...` green
- serializer: `NewSerializerManager` refuses a typed-nil serializer instance alongside the untyped one.
